xvi The Water Research Foundation

Executive Summary
ES.1 Key Benefits
• Developed a machine learning (ML) Toolkit framework (i.e., workflow) to guide users in the
 steps or workflow needed for implementation of ML solutions. The steps include: Define
 Problem, Get Data, Prepare Data, Train Model, and Deploy Model.
• Using the ML Toolkit framework, developed and demonstrated data-driven process controls
 in real full-scale facilities for five promising Applications (water resource recovery facility
 process technologies) that provide a whole plant approach and offer substantial energy and
 resource recovery benefits.
• Conducted a life cycle assessment (LCA) and a techno-economic analysis (TEA) to quantify
 the benefits realized from implementation of each application in this project compared to a
 relevant conventional baseline scenario. These two analyses demonstrated the benefits
 from both a holistic sustainability perspective as well as an economic perspective.
• Developed an ML Toolkit (i.e., website) to support the development and training of
 wastewater process engineers and others to enable more broad-scale implementation of
 ML solutions. The ML Toolkit walks users through each step of the ML Toolkit framework
 and demonstrates the steps through the five case study applications. Each case study
 includes a link to access the project code via a Google Colab notebook, a short project
 overview video, and a code walk-through video.
• Summarized ML implementation considerations and lessons learned for each of the five
 case study applications as well as the project overall. The lessons learned will guide those
 considering embarking on a data-driven controls approach at their utility or in their business
 so they can avoid unexpected challenges or reinventing the wheel.

ES.2 Background, Purpose, and Objectives
Water and wastewater treatment facilities consume 3% of all energy in the U.S., but reductions
in electricity consumption, chemical input, greenhouse gas emissions, as well as improved
energy recovery, and smaller design safety factors for more precise designs could be realized
with improvements in process monitoring and control. Current water resource recovery facility
(WRRF) data collection, monitoring, and control approaches use 20th century process
monitoring and control systems, which require large design safety factors to ensure reliability in
the absence of more advanced, precise controls. Implementation of modern data-driven
control tools (sensors, algorithms, decision support methods) can lead to more efficient
operations that provide intrinsic reliability with better overall process performance at full-scale
(increased resource recovery, higher quality effluent). The purpose of this report is to highlight
a project in which five emerging WRRF process technologies (referred to as “Applications”
throughout this report) with the potential to maximize energy recovery from wastewater and
minimize both electrical and intrinsic energy demand served as testbeds to develop methods
and demonstrate the impact of this digital design revolution.

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs xvii

The objectives of this project are to:
1. Develop and demonstrate data-driven process controls in real full-scale facilities for five
 promising Applications that provide a whole plant approach and offer substantial energy
 and resource recovery benefits.
2. Create an ML Toolkit of new process control approaches along with an implementation
 guide of five examples for application at utilities across the U.S.

As shown in Figure ES-1, the five promising WRRF Applications and their host utilities are:
1. Carbon diversion: high-rate contact stabilization (DC Water)
2. Biological nutrient removal (BNR): ammonia-based aeration control (ABAC) / ammonia vs.
 NOx (AvN) + partial denitrification with anammox (PdNA) (HRSD)
3. Disinfection: peracetic acid (PAA) (Metro Water Recovery)
4. P-recovery: MagPrex® (Metro Water Recovery)
5. Holistic biosolids optimization (DC Water)

This project advances the above objectives through the following seven tasks which are part of
the Statement of Project Objectives (SOPO):
 • Task 1: Experimental Design and Data Collection (Chapters 5.x.3 Get Data)
 • Task 2: Data and Process Analysis (Chapters 5.x.4 Prepare Data)
 • Task 3: Modeling and Machine Learning (Chapters 5.x.5 Train Model)
 • Task 4: Pilot-scale Implementation (Chapters 5.x.6 Deploy Model)
 • Task 5: TEA and LCA (Chapter 6, Appendix A)
 • Task 6: Data-driven Process Controls Toolbox (Chapter 1.4 ML Toolkit)
 • Task 7: Outreach and Technology Transfer (Appendix B)

Tasks 1-5 address Objective 1, and Tasks 6-7 address Objective 2. The report chapters that
cover each of the seven tasks are noted in parentheses above. A final task and milestone table
for the project is provided in Appendix C.

The implementation of data-driven process controls for these five technologies supports DOE
EERE’s and IEDO’s mission to accelerate the innovation and adoption of cost-effective
technologies that eliminate industrial greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions. GHG emissions
reductions are achieved through reduction in electricity including aeration energy for
degradation of organics and BNR (scope 2), reduction in chemicals including methanol for BNR,
PAA for disinfection, magnesium chloride for phosphorus (P) recovery, and polymer for
dewatering (scope 3), and reductions in direct emissions of CO2, N2O, and CH4 from the
wastewater treatment process (scope 1).

There are no plans for commercialization of the products from this project. The ML Toolkit is a
publicly accessible website. While it targets users in the water industry, it is available to anyone
who accesses the site. Providing open access via the ML Toolkit allows utilities to design
methods that will work for them. Also, being open access provides opportunities for broader
involvement toward innovative solutions since the protocols are not locked down and owned
by a few.

To better quantify the benefits realized through the Applications in this project, both a life cycle
assessment (LCA) and a techno-economic analysis (TEA) were conducted. These two analyses
demonstrated the benefits of this project from both a holistic sustainability perspective as well
as an economic perspective. Multiple impact categories in the LCA were investigated including
global warming potential, cumulative energy demand, marine and freshwater eutrophication,
as well as water deprivation. These impacts were calculated using ReCiPe 2016, Cumulative
Energy Demand, and AWARE water footprint calculation methods. All these impact categories
as well as the capital expenditure (CAPEX) and OPEX costs of the TEA were levelized per m3 of
treated water to provide a standard basis between Applications.

A summary table describing the impact in each category normalized per m3 of product water
(mainstream water treated) can be seen in Table ES-3. The baseline and final scenario can be
seen for each Application (with an intermediate step for the MagPrex® initial implementation),
along with the associated percent improvement that resulted from the optimization work
related to this project.

Table ES-4 shows a similar summary table for the TEA, both in terms of CAPEX, OPEX, and
combined costs associated with the inputs of each Application. The full LCA/TEA for each
Application can be seen in Appendix A.

The project team also identified the following cross-cutting general themes, considerations, and
lessons learned from the project overall:

• Simpler Can Be Better: Users should first carefully consider the reasons an ML solution may
 be necessary and the right approach for a particular challenge. Users should also consider if
 a simpler approach is suitable before electing to use a more complex ML model.
• The Importance of Problem Definition: For developing solutions using ML approaches, it is
 important to take time to first clearly articulate and define the problem that needs to be
 solved. Users need to make sure they are solving the right problem.

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs xxvii

• The Art of Outlier Removal: Correctly identifying and removing outliers from datasets can
 be challenging. Users need to negotiate the tension between capturing the 5%
 edge/extreme data points versus justifying their removal as outliers.
• Experimental Design and Collecting Wide-Ranging Data: In developing experimental designs
 to collect data for ML solutions, users should consider when and where to collect samples
 so that one captures “interesting” data. In particular, it is important to collect wide-ranging
 data that describes the system fully and catches any anomalies/perturbations that may take
 place. Getting wide ranging data near failure points may be difficult to justify for full-scale
 systems and warrants considering pilot-scale studies that push treatment trains and allow
 for perturbation and "interesting" data to be collected safely. It is important to determine
 where to draw the line in data collection such that a sufficient amount of data is collected
 but not too much such that resources are expended unnecessarily.
• Mechanistic Models: For wastewater process challenges, users may want to consider the
 balance between using a complex mechanistic model versus a simple, black box ML model.
• Computer Scientists & the Water Sector: In order to implement ML solutions at WRRFs
 more widely in the future, the water sector needs to consider how to attract more
 computer scientists, data scientists, statisticians, etc. Alternatively, the water sector can
 work to train wastewater process engineers in these disciplines.
• Operator Engagement & Training: Operators trust in ML was found to directly correlate to
 their (1) general understanding of control systems, (2) historical and regular engagement
 with the engineering team as control upgrades are made, and (3) trust in the process rather
 than trust in an individual model or ML technology broadly. Integrating operations staff into
 training, decision making, and problem solving helps achieve successful outcomes.
• Challenges of Real-World Deployment: Deployment of models within a utility environment
 results in a variety of challenges including data quality issues, sensor drift and process
 anomalies, retraining models in dynamic data environments (e.g., process upgrades,
 seasonality effects), cybersecurity systems, integrating model outcomes into SCADA
 systems, ensuring regulatory approval and permit compliance, etc. WRRFs will need to
 strategize beyond model development to ensure these efforts provide long-term value.
The project team developed an ML Toolkit that can be accessed via the WRF 5141 project page.
The ML Toolkit and final report from this project have been developed to support the
professional growth, development, and training of wastewater process engineers and others to
enable more broad-scale implementation of ML solutions. The ML Toolkit includes a brief dive
into AI's evolution and its place in wastewater process engineering as well as understanding ML
algorithms. The Toolkit then walks users through each step of the ML workflow and
demonstrates the steps through the five case study Applications. Each case study includes a link
to access the project code via a Google Colab notebook, a short project overview video, and a
code walk-through video.

Future research is recommended to support further development and deployment of ML
models, including hybrid models, at full-scale facilities. In particular, recommendations include
research on additional case study examples for different types of applications, developing the
business case for various data driven process controls, and developing and sustaining the
workforce necessary to support the implementation of these solutions in the water sector.

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs xxix

CHAPTER 1
Introduction and Background
1.1 Project Background
Water and wastewater treatment facilities consume 3% of all energy in the U.S., but reductions
in electricity consumption, chemical input, greenhouse gas emissions, as well as improved
energy recovery, and smaller design safety factors for more precise designs could be realized
with improvements in process monitoring and control. Current water resource recovery facility
(WRRF) data collection, monitoring, and control approaches use 20th century process
monitoring and control systems, which require large design safety factors to ensure reliability in
the absence of more advanced, precise controls. Implementation of modern data-driven
control tools (sensors, algorithms, decision support methods) can lead to more efficient
operations that provide intrinsic reliability with better overall process performance at full-scale
(increased resource recovery, higher quality effluent). This report highlights a project in which
five emerging WRRF process technologies (referred to as “Applications” throughout this report)
with the potential to maximize energy recovery from wastewater and minimize both electrical
and intrinsic energy demand served as testbeds to develop methods and demonstrate the
impact of this digital design revolution.

As shown in Figure 1-1, the project team brought together three leading water utilities that are
early adopters of the Applications. Metro Water Recovery (Metro), Hampton Roads Sanitation
District (HRSD), and DC Water have invested a combined $2.5 billion in the previous 10 years
towards intensified, resource efficient municipal wastewater treatment. Utility experience
guided the selection of the emerging Applications for further Technology Readiness Level (TRL)
advancement as all have demonstrated enhanced treatment performance and energy
efficiency, yet are currently constrained by limitations with existing process control systems.
Importantly, this project goes beyond process control technology development for a single
Application at one utility; rather, five distinct Applications across the three utilities illustrate
critical control functions that are currently limited by a lack of online instrumentation, unknown
full-scale dynamics, and basic digital infrastructure. With this diverse testbed, the project team
developed and demonstrated foundational elements of next generation process control
systems that can then be tailored to the needs of a diverse set of WRRF unit processes.

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 1

The plant process flow diagram shows the locations of the 5 Applications which demonstrate a whole plant approach for
 data-driven process controls. Utility and academic project partner roles are identified and color coded. AvN: ammonia
 versus NOx control; ABAC: ammonia-based aeration control; PdNA: partial denitrification-anammox.
 Figure 1-1. Emerging Resource Efficient Applications Selected for this Project and The Target Recovery and
 Efficiency Objectives They Address.

2 The Water Research Foundation

To respond to dramatic changes in water quality and quantities, most WRRF control schemes
use manual and/or strict feedback (FB) proportional-integrated (PI) control (see Figure 1-2).
These systems are reactionary and extremely limited to known, generally linear, relationships
to flow or water quality variables that are measured in real-time. Current WRRF distributed
control systems (DCS), supervisory control and data acquisition (SCADA) systems, and utility
data management systems (DMS) rely on personnel with specialized process knowledge to
make manual setpoint adjustments. The slow evolution of advanced control schemes has been
impeded for decades by the historical lack of reliable sensors (Love and Bott 2001; Ohmura et
al. 2019) and the lack of accurate models for a variety of treatment processes at full-scale
WRRFs. Traditional mechanistic modeling has been used to capture full-scale process dynamics;
however, these models do not provide the precision needed for advanced control (Newhart et
al. 2019). For example, feedforward (FF), as well as FB, control strategies often fail because (i)
downstream treatment is not accurately modeled for all conditions, and (ii) single-sensor
control strategies are inadequate. Even novel, emerging treatment process Applications
included in this project have used simple FB and rule-based controllers despite the limitations
noted previously. Through this project, the project team developed and demonstrated
advanced data-driven control schemes which are reflective of true full-scale performance based
on multiple sources of historical data to overcome the assumptions and limitations of
mechanistic, PI, or rule-based controllers.

 Figure 1-2. Spectrum of WRRF Control Strategies (L To R): Simple to Complex; Energy Intensive to Energy
 Efficient.

1.2 Project Objectives and Tasks
The objectives of this project are to:

1. Develop and demonstrate data-driven process controls in real full-scale facilities for five
 promising Applications (i.e., WRRF process technologies) that provide a whole plant
 approach and offer substantial energy and resource recovery benefits.
2. Create a machine learning (ML) Toolkit (i.e., website) of new process control approaches
 along with an implementation guide of five examples for application at utilities across the
 U.S.

The five promising WRRF Applications are:

1. Carbon diversion: high-rate contact stabilization

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 3

2. Biological nutrient removal (BNR): ammonia-based aeration control (ABAC) / ammonia vs.
 NOx (AvN) + partial denitrification with anammox (PdNA)
3. Disinfection: peracetic acid (PAA)
4. P-recovery: MagPrex®
5. Holistic biosolids optimization

The specific data-driven objectives and end of project goals/estimates for each Application are
provided in Table 1-1. Figure 1-1 includes a plant process flow diagram that shows the locations
of the 5 Applications. Each Application’s target level of performance is provided in Table 1-2.

technologies that eliminate industrial greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions. GHG emissions
reductions are achieved through reduction in electricity including aeration energy for

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 5

degradation of organics and BNR (scope 2), reduction in chemicals including methanol for BNR,
PAA for disinfection, magnesium chloride for phosphorus (P) recovery, and polymer for
dewatering (scope 3), and reductions in direct emissions of CO2, N2O, and CH4 from the
wastewater treatment process (scope 1).

There are no plans for commercialization of the products from this project. The ML Toolkit is a
publicly accessible website. While it targets users in the water industry, it is available to anyone
who accesses the site. Providing open access via the ML Toolkit allows utilities to design
methods that will work for them. Also, open access provides opportunities for broader
involvement in new solutions since the protocols are not locked down and owned by a few.

1.3 Project Innovation and Impact
WRRFs underwent an instrumentation revolution over the last 3 decades. Whereas advanced
monitoring in the 1980s only required a few dissolved oxygen sensors, it is now typical to find
sensors for a wide range of physico-chemical variables at facilities; for example, dissolved
oxygen, oxidation-reduction potential, pH, temperature, total suspended solids, chemical
oxygen demand (COD), and analyzers or ion-selective sensors for nitrogen and ortho-phosphate
nutrients. This investment has led to a rich stream of data. A typical WRRF collects as many as
30,000 simultaneous signals. The potential of this data stream is rarely exploited today due to
challenges with sensor management, data quality assurance and quality control (QA/QC), and a
historical lack of drivers to extract valuable information from such data. Today, the potential
power of the information contained in these data is extraordinary and can ensure the efficient
and reliable application of novel, highly efficient WRRF processes, given that their state is
sensitive to disturbances and is only optimal in a narrow region of operation.

Advancements in technologies for data-driven analytics, diagnostics, and control from the field
of ML can now be deployed at scale on premise or in the cloud. Recent advancements in
computing hardware (e.g., graphics processing unit (GPU) computing) and algorithms (e.g.,
deep learning) vastly accelerated AI model experiments and development. In contrast to
mechanistic modeling, ML offers the promise of highly automated systems that produce
insights and recommendations for optimal operation. The combination of state-of-the-art
sensor signals with ML methods will enable optimal operation of the selected high-efficiency
processes in the face of disturbing events and upsets.

This project integrates recent advancements in next generation sensors and ML tools to achieve
full-scale process efficiencies while better achieving treatment goals (e.g., reduce energy and
chemical inputs, maximize energy and nutrient recovery). In addition to developing and
demonstrating data-driven process controls in real full-scale facilities for five Applications, this
project created an industry-wide accessible ML Toolkit to help others more broadly integrate
innovations in sensing and data-driven process control to improve WRRF performance.

1.4 ML Toolkit
The water sector may have all the best sensors, tools, and technology available to implement
ML, but without people, namely professionals trained in ML related disciplines, new ML
approaches will not be able to be implemented. The water sector is currently lagging behind

6 The Water Research Foundation

other sectors in terms of adoption of ML and AI, and workforce is one of the key challenges.
The water sector needs to consider how to attract more computer scientists, data scientists,
statisticians, etc. Additionally, the water sector can train wastewater process engineers in ML
related disciplines.

The ML Toolkit was designed to specifically help support the professional growth, development,
and training of wastewater process engineers and others new to the realm of ML and artificial
intelligence (AI) to enable more broad-scale implementation of these solutions. The ML Toolkit
is an interactive and pragmatic resource to help users understand how ML can be applied to
their work and make informed decisions on whether to incorporate ML solutions. The ML
Toolkit includes a brief dive into AI's evolution and its place in wastewater process engineering
as well as understanding ML algorithms in wastewater process engineering.

The ML Toolkit walks users through each step of the workflow for applying ML to address
wastewater process engineering challenges and demonstrates the steps through the five case
study Applications. The ML Toolkit includes five real-world process control case study
Applications to help utilities: (i) achieve maximum energy and resource efficiency, (ii) achieve
performance goals (e.g., Tables 1-1 and 1-2); and (iii) better manage the risk of implementing
new technologies in the water sector. Each case study includes a link to access the project code
via a Google Colab notebook. Each case study also includes a short project overview video as
well as a code walk-through video. The ML Toolkit also includes links to additional helpful
resources. The ML Toolkit website can be accessed via the WRF 5141 project page.

1.5 Report Structure
This final report and implementation guide is intended to provide critical guidance and support
for the wastewater sector to implement data-driven process controls throughout the plant to
optimize energy efficiency and resource recovery, including lessons learned from individual
case studies which serve as the foundation for guidance.

Chapter 1 provides an introduction to the project background and objectives, as well as a brief
overview of the accompanying ML Toolkit developed from the project which is an important
resource for users. Chapter 2 provides a modeling and ML primer to help wastewater
professionals understand the role of ML in wastewater process engineering. Chapter 3 outlines
the ML Toolkit framework (i.e., workflow) and walks users through what is included in each step
of the framework. Chapter 4 provides a Python primer for AI in wastewater process
engineering, including information about getting started in Python as well as additional
resources for learning Python and AI. Chapter 5 presents the five case studies of data-driven
process controls in real full-scale facilities for the five Applications that are the focus of this
project. Each of the case studies follows the steps used in the ML Toolkit framework to
illustrate the framework approach and allow for easy comparison of the approaches used by
each Application. Chapter 6 provides the major highlights of the LCA/TEA for each Application
as well as a summary of the benefits realized from implementation of each Application
compared to a relevant conventional baseline scenario. A more detailed LCA/TEA analysis for
each Application is provided in Appendix A. Chapter 7, the final chapter, summarizes the case
study results and synthesizes the lessons learned from the project.

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 7

CHAPTER 2
Modeling and Machine Learning Primer
2.1 The Modern Process Engineer's Evolving Toolkit
For process engineers, the core expertise lies in understanding, predicting, and controlling
complex water systems through the application of mostly mechanistic models. This chapter
builds upon that foundation, introducing ML not as a replacement for fundamental engineering
principles, but as a powerful and necessary extension of the modern analytical toolkit. It frames
the adoption of data-driven methods as a logical evolution in process modeling, catalyzed by a
revolution in data availability and computational power. The objective is to provide a
conceptual framework that equips users to identify opportunities for ML, understand its core
paradigms, and engage critically with its application in the WRRF environment.

2.1.1 The Bedrock: Mechanistic Models
The practice of process engineering is built upon the bedrock of mechanistic modeling. These
models, which are constructed from the fundamental principles of physics, chemistry, and
biology, provide a causal understanding of system behavior. A prime example from the
wastewater sector is the family of Activated Sludge Models (ASMs) (e.g., Henze et al. 2000). The
hybrid model developed for biological nutrient removal control in this report’s second case
study (Application 2) incorporates such a component, demonstrating the continued relevance
of these foundational tools. The strength of mechanistic models lies in their deductive
capability; because they are based on causal mechanisms, they can, once validated, be used to
extrapolate and predict system behaviors not explicitly present in the original calibration data
(Baker et al. 2018).

However, the power of mechanistic models is accompanied by significant practical limitations.
A core challenge is that these models are, by necessity, a simplification of reality, which results
in structural uncertainty and a constant need for calibration (Duarte et al. 2023. Sin and Al
2021). This calibration process can be exceptionally complex, time-consuming, and costly, often
requiring intensive measurement campaigns to estimate the dozens of parameters involved
(Sin and Al 2021). Furthermore, established frameworks can be inflexible. Such limitations were
evident even in this project; for example, in the carbon diversion case study (Application 1), the
report notes that traditional mechanistic modeling approaches were found to be "inadequate
for robust prediction of dynamic behavior" in the high-rate contact stabilization (HR-CS) system.
It is precisely these gaps—in handling complexity, integrating diverse data, and adapting to
dynamic conditions—that have created the imperative for new modeling paradigms.

This evolution represents more than a simple change in tools; it signifies a fundamental shift in
the epistemological approach to process control. Mechanistic models are driven by a quest for
causal understanding—a desire to answer the question, "Why does this system behave this
way?" (Baker et al. 2018). This bottom-up, first-principles approach has been the cornerstone of
environmental engineering for decades. However, the sheer complexity of modern WRRFs,
coupled with the overwhelming volume of available data, has made a purely causal modeling

8 The Water Research Foundation

approach for every process interaction practically intractable. ML, in contrast, thrives on
correlational power, focusing on the question, "Given these conditions, what is the most likely
outcome?" This data-first, top-down approach prioritizes predictive accuracy, even in the
absence of a complete causal explanation. For the modern process engineer, this does not
mean abandoning the search for causality but, rather, augmenting it with powerful predictive
tools that can navigate the complexity and uncertainty of real-world operations.

2.1.2 The Data and Computation Revolution
The shift toward data-driven modeling is not an academic exercise; it is a direct response to a
fundamental transformation in the operational landscape of WRRFs. Over the last three
decades, the sector has undergone an "instrumentation revolution." Whereas advanced
monitoring in the 1980s might have involved a handful of dissolved oxygen sensors, a
contemporary facility is now equipped with a vast array of sensors measuring a wide spectrum
of physicochemical variables, including oxidation-reduction potential, pH, temperature, total
suspended solids, chemical oxygen demand, and nutrient concentrations.

This investment has resulted in an unprecedented "rich stream of data," with a typical WRRF
now collecting as many as 30,000 simultaneous signals. This data deluge, which was previously
unmanageable, now represents an extraordinary asset. Concurrently, rapid advancements in
computing hardware, particularly the advent of graphics processing unit (GPU) computing, and
the maturation of sophisticated algorithms have made the tools of ML more accessible and
powerful than ever before. Instead of being explicitly programmed with scientific equations, an
ML model learns the intricate relationships, nonlinear dynamics, and hidden patterns directly
from a facility’s operational data.

This convergence of data and powerful computation is a direct response to the escalating
complexity of modern WRRFs. These facilities are no longer just wastewater treatment plants;
they are resource recovery centers tasked with achieving multiple, often conflicting, objectives.
They must meet stringent effluent quality standards for conventional pollutants while also
managing emerging contaminants like pharmaceuticals, minimizing energy consumption,
maximizing resource recovery (such as phosphorus and biogas), and reducing greenhouse gas
emissions. The five advanced Applications detailed in this report—from carbon diversion and
partial denitrification-anammox (PdNA) to peracetic acid (PAA) disinfection and holistic
biosolids optimization—are prime examples of this increased operational complexity. In such
highly nonlinear, multi-variable systems, where first-principles understanding is often
incomplete and dynamic influent conditions are the norm, the simplifying assumptions inherent
in traditional mechanistic models frequently break down. ML, therefore, emerges not merely as
a novel trend but as the essential analytical framework required to model, optimize, and
control the sophisticated and multifaceted processes of the modern WRRF.

2.2 A Framework for Data-Driven Problem Solving in the WRRF
At its core, machine learning is a method for using data to answer questions. For the water
process engineer, the power of ML is unlocked by framing operational challenges as specific,
answerable questions. The type of question asked determines the type of ML task employed. In
the context of a WRRF, these tasks can be grouped into a few key categories, each with direct

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 9

relevance to improving process control and efficiency. This section reframes abstract ML
concepts into the practical language of water process engineering, using the case studies from
this report as concrete examples of data-driven problem-solving in action.

2.2.1 Prediction (Regression): Answering "How Much?" or "What Will the Value
Be?"
Perhaps the most common and intuitive application of ML in water process engineering is
regression, the task of predicting a specific, continuous numerical value (Seborg et al. 2011).
Regression models answer questions like "how much?" or "what will the value be at a future
time?" This capability is fundamental to proactive control, allowing engineers to anticipate
system states and make adjustments before deviations occur. The case studies in this report
provide several powerful examples of regression in practice.
 • Optimizing Phosphorus Recovery (Application 4): To improve the efficiency of the
 MagPrex® system, an ML model was developed to predict daily influent orthophosphate
 (OP) concentrations. This forecast provides operators with timely guidance to adjust
 magnesium chloride (MgCl) dosing, moving beyond a reliance on infrequent weekly lab
 samples and preventing costly chemical overdosing.
 • Controlling Biosolids Concentration (Application 5): In the holistic biosolids optimization
 study, an ML model was trained to forecast total solids (TS) percentages using data from a
 non-contact acoustic sensor. This real-time prediction is designed to inform automated
 adjustments to dilution water, ensuring stable feed concentrations for downstream
 processes like thermal hydrolysis and digestion.
 • Enhancing Disinfection Efficacy (Application 3): For PAA disinfection, a key challenge is
 determining the correct dose under varying conditions. A regression model was developed
 to estimate the required integrated concentration x time (iCT), a critical parameter that
 dictates disinfection performance. By predicting the necessary iCT based on real-time
 water quality, the system can move towards more precise and cost-effective dosing
 strategies.

2.2.2 Classification: Answering "Which Category?" or "Is this Condition A or B?"
A second key task is classification, which involves assigning a discrete, categorical label to an
observation (Seborg et al. 2011). Rather than predicting a precise number, a classifier answers a
question like, "Is this condition A or B?" or "Which operational state are we in?" Classification
can often provide a more direct and actionable insight for operators.

The P-recovery case study (Application 4) offers a compelling example of how reframing a
problem from regression to classification can unlock greater operational value. The initial goal
was to predict the exact effluent OP concentration—a regression task. However, analysis
revealed that the model provided the most significant support to operations when it was used
to classify the chemical dosing strategy. The model could reliably determine if the current MgCl
dose was creating an "overdose" or "underdose" condition. This binary output is exceptionally
powerful because it maps directly onto an operator's decision-making framework: "increase
dose," "decrease dose," or "maintain current dose." A precise regression output, such as
"effluent OP is 28.7 mg/L ± 5 mg/L," contains uncertainty that can be difficult to act upon. In

10 The Water Research Foundation

contrast, a high-confidence classification of "underdosing" provides a clear, unambiguous signal
for immediate corrective action. This demonstrates a crucial principle of applied ML: the most
effective model is not always the one with the highest numerical precision, but the one that
provides the most actionable information.

2.2.3 Pattern Discovery (Unsupervised Learning): Answering "What's Hidden in
the Data?"
A more advanced, and fundamentally different, category of ML is unsupervised learning, which
is used for pattern discovery. In this paradigm, the algorithm is given data without any pre-
defined labels or target outputs and is tasked with finding hidden structures, groupings, or
anomalies on its own. This is analogous to giving a process engineer a decade's worth of sensor
data and asking them to find interesting or unusual patterns that no one had previously
noticed.

While not a primary focus of the five case studies in this report, unsupervised learning holds
significant potential for WRRFs. For instance, clustering algorithms could be used for advanced
influent characterization, automatically identifying distinct "types" of incoming wastewater
(e.g., "high-strength industrial," "dilute wet-weather") based on complex sensor signatures (Li
and Vanrolleghem 2022. Liu et al. 2023. Newhart et al. 2024. Kazor et al. 2016). Anomaly
detection algorithms could monitor equipment performance data (e.g., from pumps or blowers)
to identify novel signatures of impending failure long before traditional threshold-based alarms
are triggered.

The choice of problem framing—as regression, classification, or pattern discovery—has direct
and significant consequences for the entire ML workflow, particularly for data collection and
preparation. A regression task requires a dataset with continuous numerical targets (e.g., a time
series of measured OP concentrations). A classification task, however, often requires a more
intensive, expert-driven data labeling process. To train a classifier for overdose/underdose
conditions, for example, a process expert must review historical operational data and explicitly
label each instance as belonging to one of those categories. This transforms the task from
simple data aggregation to the creation of an interpreted dataset, where domain knowledge is
encoded into the labels that the model will learn from. This upfront investment of expertise can
yield highly actionable models, but it underscores that successful ML is deeply reliant on the
fusion of data science techniques with domain-specific human intelligence.

2.3 Many Tools: From Simple Baselines to Advanced Ensembles
To accomplish the tasks of prediction and classification, the process engineer can draw from a
diverse arsenal of ML algorithms. Each algorithm possesses a unique internal logic, making it
better suited for certain types of data and problems. This section provides a curated tour of the
key algorithms that proved effective across the five case studies of this report, organized by
their complexity and ideal use case. The varied success of these distinct algorithms is not
arbitrary; it demonstrates that the selection of a model is an implicit hypothesis about the
underlying structure of the data.

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 11

2.3.1 An Indispensable Baseline: Linear Regression
The simplest and most interpretable starting point for any modeling endeavor is often a Linear
Regression model. This method finds the "best-fit line" (or hyperplane in multiple dimensions)
that describes the relationship between input features and a continuous output variable. Its
primary value in a modern ML workflow is to serve as a crucial baseline. If a more complex,
"black-box" model cannot significantly outperform a simple linear model, the added complexity
is likely not justified. In this project, linear models were explored in several applications. For the
carbon diversion case study (Application 1), however, a simple linear model was found to be
consistently inaccurate, a clear case of underfitting, where the model is too simplistic to
capture the complex, nonlinear dynamics of the system.

2.3.2 Versatility: Tree-Based Ensembles (Random Forest & XGBoost)
For many problems involving tabular data—the rows and columns of sensor readings and lab
results common in a WRRF—tree-based ensemble models are the versatile workhorses. These
models can be intuitively understood as highly optimized flowcharts composed of a multitude
of "if-then" decisions (Hastie et al. 2009). Their power lies in their ability to automatically
capture complex, nonlinear interactions between features without requiring extensive data
preprocessing like feature scaling. Two prominent examples are Random Forest and eXtreme
Gradient Boosting (XGBoost).

2.3.3 eXtreme Gradient Boosting (XGBoost)
XGBoost was a standout performer across this project's Applications. It is an advanced
implementation of the gradient boosting framework, which builds decision trees sequentially.
Each new tree is trained to correct the errors, or residuals, of the preceding ones (Chen and
Guestrin 2016). XGBoost enhances this process with built-in regularization to control model
complexity and prevent overfitting, as well as numerous optimizations for computational speed
and efficiency (Chen and Guestrin 2016). The remarkable effectiveness of XGBoost was
demonstrated in three distinct case studies:
 • Application 3 (disinfection): It proved to be the most accurate model for predicting both
 the required iCT and the resulting log removal of E. coli.
 • Application 4 (P-recovery): It delivered the best performance for predicting OP
 concentrations and, crucially, for classifying dosing conditions.
 • Application 5 (biosolids): It achieved the best predictive power for forecasting Total Solids
 percentages from the complex frequency data generated by acoustic sensors.

2.3.4 Support Vector Machines (SVM)
A Support Vector Machine (SVM) operates on a different principle. It excels at finding the
clearest possible boundary, or "hyperplane," that separates data points into different classes
(Cortes and Vapnik 1995). By maximizing the margin, or distance, between the hyperplane and
the nearest data points of each class (the "support vectors"), the SVM finds a robust decision
boundary. A key advantage of SVMs is their effectiveness in high-dimensional spaces, where
the number of features is very large relative to the number of samples. This characteristic made
the SVM the most effective algorithm for the carbon diversion case study (Application 1). This
problem was characterized by an exceptionally high-dimensional feature space, with over 120

12 The Water Research Foundation

potential input variables influencing sludge settleability. The SVM was able to navigate this
complexity to build the most successful predictive model.

2.3.5 The Time-Series Expert: Recurrent Neural Networks (LSTM)
For challenges that involve data that unfold over time, a specialized class of models is required.
Neural Networks, inspired by the interconnected structure of the human brain, are powerful
function approximators capable of learning highly intricate patterns. Within this family, Long
Short-Term Memory (LSTM) networks are a type of Recurrent Neural Network (RNN)
specifically architected to handle time-series data (Hochreiter and Schmidhuber 1997). Unlike
standard networks that treat each data point independently, LSTMs have internal loops and
"memory cells" that allow them to retain information from previous time steps, enabling them
to learn long-term dependencies in sequential data (Hochreiter and Schmidhuber 1997).

This unique capability made an LSTM-based model a critical component of the solution for BNR
aeration control (Application 2). In this application, a hybrid model was developed that
combined a mechanistic ASM1 model with a data-driven component. The data-driven part, an
LSTM ensemble, was tasked with forecasting the error of the mechanistic model over time. By
learning the temporal patterns in the mechanistic model's inaccuracies, the LSTM could provide
a dynamic correction factor, significantly improving the overall predictive accuracy of the hybrid
system and demonstrating a sophisticated application of LSTMs in wastewater treatment.

The success of these different algorithms across the five case studies illustrates a deeper
principle: algorithm selection is a form of hypothesis testing about the nature of the problem.
Choosing an LSTM, as was done for Application 2, is an implicit hypothesis that temporal
dependencies are the most critical pattern to capture. Opting for an SVM in Application 1 was a
hypothesis that a clear decision boundary existed within a high-dimensional feature space. The
repeated success of XGBoost in Applications 3, 4, and 5 suggested that the underlying problems
were characterized by complex, nonlinear interactions within noisy, tabular data. This
perspective elevates model selection from a brute-force, trial-and-error exercise to a more
principled, hypothesis-driven process guided by an engineer's understanding of the problem's
fundamental structure.

2.4 The Art and Science of Model Selection
Choosing the right algorithm is only one part of the modeling process. A process engineer must
also navigate the fundamental challenge of selecting a model of appropriate complexity. This
section delves into the core theoretical principles that govern this choice, framing it not as a
search for a single best model, but as a strategic exercise in balancing competing objectives.
The case studies from this report provide a practical, real-world laboratory for understanding
these foundational concepts.

2.4.1 The "No Free Lunch" Theorem: There is No Silver Bullet
A foundational concept in machine learning is the "No Free Lunch" (NFL) theorem. In formal
terms, it states that when performance is averaged across all possible problems, any two
optimization algorithms are equivalent (Wolpert an Macready 1997). This implies that no single
algorithm can be universally superior to all others for every task. An algorithm that excels on

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 13

one class of problems will necessarily be paid for with inferior performance on a different class
of problems.

The "No Free Lunch" theorem and the results in Table 2-1 reveal a crucial truth: successful
machine learning is not about finding a single magic algorithm. Instead, it is a systematic
process of experimentation and refinement that encompasses the entire journey from raw data
to a final, validated model. This workflow involves a series of critical decisions, each of which is
a "lever" that an engineer can pull to tune the outcome. The table illustrates that success is not
just determined by the final choice of model, but by the entire chain of data preparation and
feature engineering steps that precede it.

2.4.2 Step 1: Data Cleaning and Imputation
The journey begins with the raw data collected from sensors, labs, and operational logs. This
data is inevitably imperfect. WRRF data can be messy and may contain outliers—erroneous
points caused by sensor malfunctions, data entry mistakes, or lab errors—that can mislead a
model if not addressed. It will also have gaps due to inevitable infrequencies in sampling,
equipment maintenance, or instrument downtime, resulting in missing values that most
algorithms cannot handle.

• Outlier Removal: This is the first line of defense. As shown in the case studies, methods can
 range from simple upper and lower thresholds to more sophisticated statistical approaches
 like Kernel Density Estimation (KDE), which automatically identifies extreme values. The
 goal is to remove clear errors without discarding valuable data from genuine process
 upsets.
• Data Imputation: This step addresses missing data by filling the gaps. The choice of method
 is critical. Simple approaches like using the average value can be effective, but more
 complex techniques like k-Nearest Neighbors (KNN) or multivariate linear models were also
 explored. A key challenge here is avoiding data leakage—accidentally using future
 information to fill past gaps (e.g., linear interpolation), which can create a model that looks
 deceptively accurate in testing but fails in real-time deployment.

2.4.3 Step 2: Feature Selection – Focusing on What Matters
A modern WRRF can generate thousands of data signals, but not all are relevant to the specific
problem at hand. Feeding a model with irrelevant or redundant information increases its
complexity, making it more prone to overfitting and harder to interpret. Feature selection is the
disciplined process of identifying and retaining only the most predictive input variables (or
"features").

• Techniques like analyzing Correlation Coefficients or using automated methods like
 minimum Redundancy Maximum Relevance (mRMR) help to filter the noise and focus the
 model on the true drivers of the process. For instance, in the Application 5 (biosolids
 optimization), Principal Component Analysis (PCA) was exceptionally effective at reducing
 the high-dimensional acoustic sensor data into a manageable number of informative
 components. This step is a direct application of the "simpler can be better" philosophy, as it
 aims to reduce model complexity before training even begins.
