2.4.4 Step 3: Model Selection and Training
Only after the data has been cleaned, imputed, and the features selected does the final model
training occur. This is where the algorithms from Section 2.3 are put to the test. As Table 2-1
clearly demonstrates, different models excel at different tasks. The process involves training
multiple candidate algorithms on the prepared data and rigorously evaluating their

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 15

performance using metrics like root mean squared error (RMSE), coefficient of determination
(R2), mean absolute error (MAE), and mean absolute percentage error (MAPE) among others.
Different evaluation metrics provide different types of information and choosing which to use
can depend on a multitude of factors including the need for interpretability, for balancing
goodness of fit versus prediction errors, and for penalizing the size of errors. This empirical,
evidence-based approach is the practical embodiment of the "No Free Lunch" theorem. The
model that provides the best performance on the validation data, without showing signs of
significant overfitting, is then chosen for deployment. By systematically splitting a dataset into
multiple subsets, or "folds,” and repeatedly training and validating the model on different
combinations of these folds, cross-validation (CV) helps provide a robust evaluation on how
models perform on new or unseen data. CV helps prevent the model from memorizing specific
patterns or anomalies in the training data and instead promotes the learning of generalizable
patterns across diverse data samples. As a result, training a model using CV can provide a more
reliable estimate of the model's real-world performance and aid in selecting models and
hyperparameters that lead to robust and generalizable results.

This entire workflow—from cleaning to feature selection to model training—is an iterative loop.
The choice of an imputation method might affect which features are selected, which in turn
influences which model performs best. Success requires a holistic view, recognizing that each
step is interconnected and contributes to the final goal of building a robust, reliable, and
trustworthy data-driven model.

This project serves as a powerful, tangible demonstration of the NFL theorem in action. Within
the confines of a single, coordinated research effort focused on the WRRF sector, no single
algorithm emerged as a universal solution. The fact that a Support Vector Machine (SVM) was
the top performer for carbon diversion (Application 1), XGBoost was the champion for
disinfection (Application 3), P-recovery (Application 4), and biosolids optimization (Application
5), and an LSTM ensemble was essential for BNR aeration control (Application 2) provides
undeniable evidence for this principle. Table 2-1, adapted from the project's summary findings,
provides a clear visual representation of this reality, showing the varied performance of
different methods across the five Applications.

2.4.5 The Central Challenge: The Bias-Variance Tradeoff
If there is no single best algorithm, the central challenge becomes finding the right balance of
model complexity for a specific problem. This is formally known as the Bias-Variance Tradeoff
(Hastie et al. 2009). It describes the inherent conflict between two primary sources of model
error:
 • Bias is the error introduced by a model's simplifying assumptions. A model with high bias is
 too simple and fails to capture the true underlying patterns in the data. This leads to
 underfitting, where the model performs poorly on both the data it was trained on and on
 new, unseen data (Hastie et al. 2009). A clear example of this was observed in Application
 1 (carbon diversion), where simple linear regression models were consistently inaccurate
 because they could not represent the complex system dynamics.

16 The Water Research Foundation

 • Variance is the error introduced by a model's sensitivity to small fluctuations in the
 training data. A model with high variance is overly complex; it learns the training data too
 well, fitting to both the signal and the random noise. This leads to overfitting, where the
 model performs exceptionally well on the training data but fails to generalize to new data
 (Hastie et al. 2009). The report notes that managing overfitting was a persistent challenge
 across the case studies. For instance, in Application 1 (carbon diversion), the complexity of
 the Random Forest model had to be carefully constrained to prevent it from memorizing
 the training data, and the analysis concluded that some of the most complex models,
 including certain ensembles and neural networks, were ultimately discarded due to their
 tendency to overfit.

This tradeoff can be viewed as a risk management framework. In the high-stakes environment
of a WRRF, where a model failure could lead to a permit violation or significant operational
disruption, predictability and robustness are paramount. A high-variance (overfit) model is a
high-risk proposition because its performance on new, real-world data is fundamentally
unpredictable. It may have been perfectly accurate on historical data but could be
catastrophically wrong tomorrow. In contrast, a simpler model with a degree of known bias,
while perhaps less precise on average, is often more stable and its behavior is more
predictable. The cost of a major failure often far outweighs the marginal benefit of slightly
improved average performance. Therefore, an experienced engineer will often implicitly adopt
a risk-averse stance, preferring a model with manageable bias to one with dangerously high
variance.

2.4.6 A Guiding Principle: Simpler May Be Better
This risk-based perspective leads directly to one of the most important cross-cutting lessons
learned from this entire project: simpler may be better. This is not merely a preference for
elegance, but a robust, practical strategy for navigating the Bias-Variance Tradeoff. The
recommended approach for any process engineer embarking on an ML project is to begin with
the simplest reasonable model (such as an average model, or linear regression or a simple
decision tree) to establish a performance baseline. Complexity should only be increased
incrementally and methodically. A more complex model, like an XGBoost ensemble or a neural
network, should only be adopted if it provides a significant, quantifiable, and robust
improvement in performance over the simpler alternative—an improvement that justifies the
increased risk of overfitting and the loss of interpretability. This disciplined, stepwise approach
is the most reliable way to arrive at a model that is not just accurate on paper, but also robust,
trustworthy, and effective in a live operational setting.

2.5 Beyond the Model
A successful machine learning implementation is built on more than just clever algorithms.
While model selection is critical, it is only one component of a larger system. This section
broadens the focus to the foundational, often non-technical, pillars that are essential for
translating a promising model into a valuable and sustainable operational tool. These pillars —
data quality, clear metrics, interpretability, and human collaboration—form the bedrock upon
which successful data-driven process control is built. They also reveal that an ML project is not

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 17

merely a technical exercise, but a socio-technical endeavor that requires a holistic approach to
succeed.

2.5.1 Pillar 1: Data is the Foundation
The most fundamental principle of machine learning is that a model is only as good as the data
from which it learns. The dataset must contain the patterns, relationships, and signals that the
engineer wants the model to capture. If the necessary information is not present in the data, no
amount of algorithmic sophistication can create it. This highlights that data collection is not a
passive act of aggregation but may require active and deliberate experimental design.

The biosolids optimization case study (Application 5) provides a powerful and instructive lesson
on this topic from the entire project. Initial attempts to build a model to predict Total Solids (TS)
from acoustic sensor data failed. The reason was not the algorithm, but the data itself. The
digester feed process was so tightly and effectively controlled under normal operations that the
historical data showed very little variability in TS concentrations. The model, when trained on
this narrow range of data, simply learned to predict the average value and was incapable of
forecasting deviations or extreme events. Success was only achieved after a dedicated
experimental campaign was designed and executed in collaboration with plant operators. By
intentionally and safely pushing the process to its operational boundaries, the team was able to
generate the data variability necessary to train a robust and responsive model. This
underscores a critical lesson: to build a model that can predict and manage process upsets, the
training data must include examples of those upsets.

2.5.2 Pillar 2: Metrics
To gauge the success of any engineering effort, it is vital to first define the finish line. In ML, this
means establishing clear, quantifiable performance metrics at the outset of the project. These
metrics provide an objective and rigorous standard for comparing different models and for
determining whether a model is performing well enough to be deployed. Throughout the five
case studies, metrics such as root mean squared error (RMSE), which measures the average
magnitude of a model's prediction errors, were used consistently to provide a quantifiable
assessment of model performance. By defining what "good" looks like in numerical terms, the
project teams could move beyond subjective assessments and make data-driven decisions
about which modeling approaches were most effective.

2.5.3 Pillar 3: Interpretability
One of the most significant barriers to the adoption of advanced ML models is the "black box"
problem. Complex models like neural networks or large ensembles can be opaque, making it
difficult for operators and engineers to understand why a particular prediction or
recommendation was made. This lack of transparency can erode trust, which is a non-
negotiable prerequisite for integrating a new tool into a critical process control loop.

To build this trust, it is essential to open the black box. Modern model interpretability tools can
illuminate the inner workings of complex models. A leading example used in this project is SHAP
(SHapley Additive exPlanations) analysis (Lundberg and Lee 2017). SHAP is a sophisticated
method, grounded in cooperative game theory, that assigns an importance value to each input

18 The Water Research Foundation

feature for every individual prediction, showing how much each factor pushed the prediction
higher or lower (Lundberg and Lee 2017). The application of SHAP in the carbon diversion
(Application 1) and disinfection (Application 3) case studies was highlighted as being crucial for
building the operational confidence needed for deployment. By providing clear, visual
explanations for model behavior, these tools transform a model from an inscrutable oracle into
a transparent and trustworthy analytical partner (Lundberg and Lee 2017).

2.5.4 Pillar 4: Human-in-the-Loop
The final, and arguably most important, pillar is the human element. A recurring theme
throughout this project's lessons learned is that ML is a tool to empower engineers and
operators, not replace them. The successful deployment of a data-driven solution is not simply
a technical handoff from data scientists to operations. Rather, it is the outcome of a deeply
collaborative effort where operators and other stakeholders are engaged throughout the entire
process—from initial problem definition to final deployment and ongoing monitoring. This
continuous engagement builds institutional knowledge, fosters trust, and ensures that the final
tool is not only technically sound but also practical, useful, and integrated into the existing
operational culture. While the water industry continues to grapple with how to responsibly
deploy AI and ML models, the need for a human-in-the-loop will be critical to maintain data and
process transparency, ensure accountability and reliability, and prevent any malfeasance.

Ultimately, these four pillars reveal that a successful ML implementation is a complex socio-
technical system. It is a system where technical components (algorithms, data pipelines) and
social components (operator trust, inter-departmental collaboration) are deeply intertwined. A
technical solution like SHAP is deployed to solve a social problem like a lack of trust. A social
interaction, like a collaborative experiment between modelers and operators, is required to
solve a technical problem like a lack of data variability. The failure of any one of these pillars
can jeopardize the entire endeavor, regardless of the sophistication of the algorithm.

Therefore, this implementation guide, and this chapter in particular, should be viewed as more
than a technical manual. It is a blueprint for organizational change. Effectively leveraging these
foundational pillars requires a shift in how a utility operates: data must be viewed not as a
passive record of the past, but as a strategic asset to be actively curated for the future.
Communication channels must be opened between data scientists, process engineers, and
front-line operators. Traditional organizational silos must become more permeable to foster
the cross-disciplinary collaboration that is essential for success. With these foundational
concepts and pillars in mind, the operator and process engineer are now prepared to explore
the practical, step-by-step framework for implementing their own data-driven solutions.

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 19

CHAPTER 3
Overview of ML Toolkit Framework
The project team developed an ML Toolkit framework (i.e., workflow) to guide users in the
steps or workflow needed for implementation of ML solutions. The framework was developed
and refined by the entire project team to capture cross-Application challenges and
opportunities and it incorporates the principles and foundational concepts discussed in Chapter
2. The implementation of ML solutions involves the collaboration and nexus of several key
disciples, including wastewater process engineering, plant operations, and computer science.
The ML Toolkit framework and key disciplines needed for each step are shown in Figure 3-1.
The report sections that follow provide detailed descriptions of the activities and key
considerations associated with each step of the process.

 Figure 3-1. ML Toolkit Framework including the Steps in ML Implementation and Key Disciplines Needed.

20 The Water Research Foundation

3.1 Define Problem
The first step in the ML Toolkit framework is problem formulation, where one pinpoints the
operational inefficiencies or predictive challenges that could benefit from using ML models.
Within the water sector, this might involve identifying patterns in system performance that
predict maintenance needs or optimizing chemical dosages to meet varying demands. It is
important to include representatives from operations, engineering, laboratory, and control
systems in the initial problem formulation.

Problem definition should clearly define the scope of the project including (1) the expected ML
response given specific process changes, (2) how the ML model will interact with the control
system (i.e., direct or indirect), (3) how to assess the performance of the proposed ML system
using defined, quantifiable metrics, and (4) determining the performance threshold required to
merit progressing to the deployment stage after data collection, preparation, and model
training.

3.2 Get Data
After defining the scope of the problem, relevant data is gathered. The data comes from an
array of sources in a water treatment plant: sensors and instrumentation measuring real-time
quantities, chemical and biological analyses providing concentration levels, or historical
maintenance logs. Data could represent measured or calculated values, or actuation data from
applied commands. Each data point or observation should represent the conditions at the time
the data were collected. A data collection plan that explores a wide range of operational and
environmental conditions is essential because the outcomes of ML models are limited to the
data used to train them. This may include additional sampling at underrepresented times of day
(e.g., afternoon and evening), changes to setpoints (e.g., extreme high and low values), or wet
weather events.

3.3 Prepare Data
During and after data collection, raw data must be analyzed and processed to best represent
the treatment process for the ML model. In the context of process engineering, this may involve
removing outliers, imputing infrequently collected or missing values, or identifying important
(or not important) variables or features. In computer science and statistics, a feature is a
general term for a model input. It may be identical or distinct from the original, measured
process variable, depending on if a data transformation has occurred. For example, using a
statistical method like principal component analysis (PCA) will generate new “variables" but as
these are transformed versions of original data are no longer variables in the conventional
engineering sense. Hence, you often see the terms used interchangeably for the purposes of
modeling treatment processes.

A short list of data preparation methods explored in this work can be found in Table 2-1. Not all
data analysis methods will accurately represent a process or data availability; thus, this step
demands a keen eye for the nuances of both data science and water process operations to
produce a clean, accurate, and comprehensive dataset for model training. For example, outliers
should be removed if they are erroneous (e.g., data entry error, sample contamination, sensor

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 21

maintenance, electrical surge effecting sensor output). Manually removing outliers is accurate
but time consuming and not practical for real-time systems. Setting upper and lower limits is a
common control strategy but are frequently too generous to identify more subtle changes.
Kernel density estimation (KDE) is a statistical method which estimates a probability density
curve, most commonly as a normal or Gaussian distribution curve, which can identify extreme
statistical outliers automatically as new data becomes available after manual tuning. Selection
of an outlier removal method depends on the process. For example, negative values are
impossible for most variables measured at WRRFs. Therefore, a strict threshold to replace
negative values with “0” is appropriate. The most important consideration for an outlier
detection method is automation. Can a set of rules be developed that, when always followed,
will provide the best representation of the environmental and operational conditions? This set
of rules will need to be automated for real-time deployment as new data is collected, and thus
the manual approach or threshold approach may not be sustainable beyond the research phase
of a project.

Another frequently used data preparation method is imputation, which involves creating a
predictive model for a single or multiple variables prior to ML model development. The model
could be as simple as the arithmetic average or as complex as ML. For example, clustering
algorithms like k nearest neighbors (KNN) uses a multivariate grouping approach and produces
an average of those similar observations to impute missing data. However, it is important not to
use future data when imputing if a method is to be used in real time, such as when doing linear
interpolation between two points in time. When the model is deployed, the future value cannot
be known and linear interpolation cannot be performed. Thus, the model training and testing
errors may appear low in simulation, but without the incorporation of future information when
deployed will perform poorly. This is an example of data leakage. In this case, interpolating
using the ‘last known value’ is often used to best represent real world availability of laboratory
values. Data imputation methods should both consider data availability and an accurate
representation of the process. In the absence of information on how a process behaves
between two time points, it is best to not impute and to either (1) exclude the variable due to
infrequent data collection, (2) exclude the missing observation for all variables, or (3) use the
last known value. Imputing in this case has been found to introduce more model error than
resolve it (Newhart et al. 2021).

It is important that data preparation be performed using data downloaded from the same
process that will be used for deployment. For example, if the ML model will download data
directly from the data historian using Python, then data for the preparation step should be
downloaded directly from the data historian using Python. While it is tempting to do the
preliminary work using data extraction methods that have less overhead (e.g., downloading
into Excel using pre-established data connections), often these data are compressed or
averaged differently and can cause major performance discrepancies during the deployment
stage.

3.4 Train Model
With the prepared dataset, an ML model may be trained by conducting feature engineering and
model selection. These two steps are frequently performed together as which model inputs

22 The Water Research Foundation

(features) are important is significantly influenced by the underlying model structure. However,
as ML models increase in complexity, the demand for data increases exponentially. Without
proper constraints it is possible to overfit a model where a statistical measure of performance,
like R2, suggests success but fails when presented with new data. Thus, it is important to reduce
the model complexity whenever possible, including reducing the number of model inputs or
predictors by feature selection or dimension reduction.

In feature selection, important variables are identified based on their correlation with the
model output (e.g., correlation coefficients, minimum Redundancy Maximum Relevance
(mRMR)) or changes to model accuracy when added or removed (e.g., feed forward,
permutation). In dimension reduction, the variation in the data is modeled such that the
changes in the data can be expressed in fewer model predictors (e.g., principal component
analysis (PCA)). Both approaches reduce the number of model inputs and consequently the
number of ML model parameters to be fit. Given the relatively small datasets found in water
treatment, this is incredibly important. Unfortunately, there is no single model type that
performs ‘best’ for water treatment applications given the variation in processes and properties
of the data collected. Therefore, it is common to explore many different model types, like those
presented in Table 2-1.

In general, statistical approaches assume certain properties about the data being modeled
whereas traditional ML approaches do not require these assumptions be upheld for successful
modeling. Statistical models are much less complex than ML models and therefore could be
more stable in data-sparce environments. Modified multiple linear regression approaches, like
ridge regression, lasso regression, and adaptive lasso, should be explored as a more simplistic
baseline that can be easily programmed into SCADA systems. There are a few major categories
of ML models explored in this work: neural networks, ensemble models (random forest,
XGBoost, AdaBoost), and clustering or similarity approaches (k nearest neighbors, support
vector machines). The specifics of these models are well documented elsewhere; we will
emphasize here that multiple algorithm structures should be explored during model training to
identify which performs best with a given dataset.

3.5 Deploy Model
Deployment is the stage where ML models transition from theoretical constructs to integral
components of the operational process. It involves integrating the model into a new or existing
operational change workflow. This could range from executing the model on an ad hoc basis for
operational support, integrating real-time predictions of chemical kinetics into an FB or FF
control loop, or alerting maintenance teams about potential equipment failures. This stage
requires a deep understanding of the operational technology onsite, from SCADA systems to
process control algorithms.

Deployment takes on many forms, depending on the problem statement and the operational
structure of the WRRF. If the objective is autonomous control with a ML prediction (frequently
referred to as a digital twin), deployment should be divided into three phases: a digital shadow,
supervised control, and unsupervised control. For purposes of this report, a digital shadow is
defined as a model that operates in real-time to make predictions but does not actively interact

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 23

with controls. The goal of this stage is to observe model behavior “in the wild”; to identify and
address unexpected model behavior and quantify performance. The subsequent stages involve
allowing the model predictions to be used in control in a setting with and without an additional
human-in-the-loop (where a human is directly supervising to ensure the underlying control
sequence responds to the new predictions appropriately), respectively. The performance
metrics defined during the problem formulation stage should be measured during each phase
to ensure that the project merits continuation or needs to be revised.

24 The Water Research Foundation

CHAPTER 4
Python Primer for AI in WRRFs
4.1 Introduction to Python
For this DOE project, the project team chose Python as the main implementation programming
language for the ML Toolkit. Python is a versatile, open-source, high-level programming
language known for its simplicity and readability. It has become the go-to language for many
fields that are entering the realm of artificial intelligence where Python is heavily used, and
recent experiences by team members indicate that Python is quickly becoming the
programming language of choice among coders in the water industry. It has extensive libraries,
a supportive community, and an ability to easily ingest, analyze, and create models for large
amounts of data. For designers and operators of WRRFs who may not have an extensive coding
background, Python has a low barrier to entry and a bevy of resources for tutorials and
troubleshooting. It can also be easily integrated to run locally on onsite servers or cloud-based
platforms. Importantly, popular process modeling platforms (e.g., SUMO, Biowin, GPS-X) have
made it possible to seamlessly integrate between their platform and Python. This summary
provides an overview of Python, its popularity, essential tools and libraries, and guidance on
getting started with Python for AI applications in WRRF systems.

4.1.1 Why Python?
Relevance to WRRFs: Today, Python is the most common programming language in use for
data science applied across multiple sectors (Github Staff 2024). In addition, it is open source
and thrives on an active, global community of engineers, software developers, and scientists.
Due to its popularity, commercial simulator software vendors active in the wastewater sector
provide and support interfaces with their simulation packages. At the same time, Python
supports interfacing with both legacy and modern data acquisition and control systems (e.g.,
SCADA systems, ROS) and database technologies, although security and variations in SCADA
software provider standards require unique interfacing practices per utility (examples described
below). Overall, we conclude that Python is the correct platform to develop WRRF specific tools
for data mining, soft-sensing, and adaptive control, while maintaining access to the latest
developments in artificial intelligence, machine learning, and model predictive control.

SCADA systems are integral to WRRF operation and, therefore, the integration of Python and
SCADA is an important consideration. As an outcome of this project, two architectures are
proposed for integrating Python with SCADA systems and DCSs, modeled after Applications
demonstrated at DC Water, HRSD, and Metro. At DC Water, the Python script was deployed
directly to an edge device — a Raspberry Pi microcontroller. Once uploaded, the script received
input data locally, executed the model, and sent the resulting outputs back to the control
system. In contrast, HRSD and Metro implemented a centralized approach, running the Python
scripts on a central server. At HRSD, inputs were transmitted to the server using Open Platform
Communications Unified Architecture (OPC-UA), a common industrial communication standard.
The model’s outputs were then returned to the control system over the same OPC-UA channel.

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 25

At Metro, the models were deployed on a remote Windows server located outside the SCADA
firewall. The Python script used a basic request form (HTTP POST request) to both pull data
from and push predictions to Metro’s data historian using the historian’s built-in application
interface (API). By accessing data through the data historian, data from disparate sources can
be retrieved using the same protocol despite being stored in different databases (i.e., read-only
SCADA data and laboratory data). This method allowed Metro staff to view model outputs
through existing dashboards and reporting tools without needing to modify or directly access
the SCADA system. Because the server and laboratory database were positioned outside the
SCADA network, this architecture maintained a strong cybersecurity boundary while still
enabling integration of multiple data sources. These examples illustrate possible architectural
options. Each utility must evaluate key factors when selecting an appropriate implementation
strategy, such as whether the script requires only local inputs or signals from across the facility,
how the model will be updated, and what cybersecurity measures are needed. Of course, the
objective of the model/script itself will also affect a utility’s choice of architecture. DC Water
and Metro’s applications involved developing a soft sensor, while HRSD’s application included a
forecasting model. Consequently, interfacing Python with a utility’s SCADA system is possible,
and current differences in protocols across SCADA software companies mean that
implementation methods are likely to be distinct for each WRRF at this time.

Ease of Learning and Use: Python is easy to learn and use. Python’s syntax is relatively clean
and straightforward, making it accessible for beginners. This simplicity will allow ML Toolkit
users to focus on solving problems rather than getting bogged down by complex syntax. In the
instructions to Toolkit users, the project team emphasizes this aspect to reassure users that
Python is approachable, even for those with no prior programming experience. The subsequent
materials and tutorials on individual applications are designed to build confidence and
competence from the very beginning.

Extensive Libraries and Frameworks: Python boasts a rich ecosystem of libraries and
frameworks that simplify AI and ML development. Libraries like TensorFlow, Keras, and PyTorch
provide pre-built functions and algorithms, allowing the creation of sophisticated models with
minimal code. The project team conveys to ML Toolkit users how these libraries can accelerate
development and reduce the need for extensive coding from scratch. By demonstrating
practical examples through the project’s Applications with partner utilities, the project team
shows how these tools can be applied effectively in wastewater process engineering.

Community and Support: Python has a vast and active community. This means there are ample
tutorials, forums, and documentation available to help troubleshoot issues and learn new
concepts. The project team stresses to ML Toolkit users the importance of community support,
highlighting how it can serve as a valuable resource for continuous learning and problem-
solving. Users are encouraged to engage with the community to share experiences and gain
insights.

26 The Water Research Foundation

Versatility: Python is not only used in AI but also in web development, data analysis,
automation, and more. This versatility makes it a valuable skill across various domains. In the
project team’s messaging, the team highlights this versatility to underscore the long-term value
of learning Python. The team provides examples of how skills gained in this course can be
transferred to other areas of a user’s professional work, enhancing overall career development.

4.1.2 Essential Python Libraries for AI
In guiding ML Toolkit users through their AI journey, the ML Toolkit introduces them to
essential Python libraries for AI through each Application:

NumPy: A fundamental package for numerical computing in Python. It supports large, multi-
dimensional arrays and matrices, along with a collection of mathematical functions to operate
on them. The team explains how NumPy is crucial for handling large datasets typical in
wastewater process engineering.

Pandas: A powerful library for data manipulation and analysis. It provides data structures like
DataFrames, which are essential for handling and analyzing structured data. The team provides
practical examples of how Pandas can simplify data processing tasks, making it easier to
prepare data for analysis.

Matplotlib and Seaborn: Libraries for data visualization. They help in creating static, animated,
and interactive plots, which are crucial for data analysis and presenting results. The team shows
ML Toolkit users how to create visualizations that can communicate complex data insights
effectively to stakeholders.

Scikit-learn: A comprehensive library for ML. It includes simple and efficient tools for data
mining and data analysis, supporting various ML algorithms. The project team demonstrates
through each Application how Scikit-learn can be used to build predictive models that improve
decision-making processes in water management.

their Python environment:

 team encourages users to start with Google Colab due to its ease of use and accessibility.
• Jupyter Notebooks: An open-source web application that allows users to create and share
 documents that contain live code, equations, visualizations, and narrative text. Users can

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 27

Supporting Device Installs for advanced users:
• Anaconda: A distribution of Python and R for scientific computing and data science. It

 After installing Python, they can use pip, the package installer for Python, to install libraries
 like NumPy, Pandas, and TensorFlow. The project team provides step-by-step instructions to

4.2.2 Writing Python Script
To get users started, the project team provides a simple example of writing and running a
Python script for novice users:
1. Install Python (if not using Colab): The team guides users on how to install Python using
 Anaconda or directly from the official website.
2. Set Up Your Environment (if not using colab): The team explains how to open a terminal or
 command prompt, create a new directory for a project, and navigate to this directory.
3. Create a New Script (if not using Colab): The team shows how to open a text editor or an
 IDE (like VSCode or PyCharm) and create a new file named hello_ai.py.
4. Write the First Code (all users): The team provides a simple code example that directs
 Python to generate fake sensor data over time, which can be executed by a beginner
 without importing data:

 python

 import numpy as np

 # Generate fake time series data
 np.random.seed(42) # For reproducibility
       time = np.arange(0, 100, 1) # 100 time points
 sensor_data = np.sin(time / 10) + np.random.normal(0, 0.1, size=time.shape) # Sine
 wave with noise

 # Create a scatter plot
 plt.figure(figsize=(10, 6))
 plt.scatter(time, sensor_data, color='b', label='Sensor Data', alpha=0.6)

 # Add labels and title
 plt.xlabel('Time (s)')
 plt.ylabel('Sensor Reading')
 plt.title(‘Example Time Series Sensor Data')
 plt.legend()

5. Run the Script: The project team explains how to save the file and run it from the terminal
 or command prompt by typing python hello_ai.py. If users use notebooks, the play button
 can simply be pressed.

CHAPTER 5
Case Studies: Data-Driven Process Controls
This chapter presents details for five case studies of data-driven process controls in real full-
scale facilities for the five Applications that are the focus of this project. Collectively, these
Applications provide a whole plant approach for achieving substantial energy and resource
recovery benefits.

Each case study includes a Background section to set the stage for the challenge that is being
addressed by the data-driven process controls. Then the case study is organized by the steps
used in the ML Toolkit framework (e.g., workflow):
• Define Problem
• Get Data
• Prepare Data
• Train Model
• Deploy Model

The case studies demonstrate in detailed, practical terms how each step of the ML Toolkit
framework works such that users will have a better understanding of the workflow process and
how to develop and implement ML models at their facilities.

At the end of each case study, there is a Lessons Learned section which captures key learnings
and guidance to help others implementing ML models to avoid unexpected challenges or
reinventing the wheel.

For more information on each case study, users can visit the ML Toolkit website (see Chapter
1.4) for links to the code that was used (e.g., Google Colab notebook), short project overview
videos, code walk-through videos, and more.

The ML models created in this project were specifically developed for implementation by the
case study sites and their associated Applications; however, the approach (e.g., steps in the ML
Toolkit framework) and principles used are transferrable to other WRRFs. Additionally, some
relevant sections of code could potentially be utilized or incorporated into ML models at other
WRRFs by trained programmers.

5.1 Carbon Diversion: High-rate Contact Stabilization (Application 1)
5.1.1 Background (Set the Stage)
High-rate contact stabilization (HR-CS) is a promising high-rate bioprocess that can be used in
place of or in addition to primary clarification to maximize carbon capture and energy
generation. Substantial energy savings are achieved by minimizing aeration costs (minimizing
carbon oxidation) and maximizing energy recovery through sidestream anaerobic digestion
(methane production) (Meerburg et al. 2016). In addition to carbon and energy recovery,
signiﬁcant amounts of nitrogen and phosphorus (15 – 27% TN and 13 – 38% TP) are redirected
to solids treatment, resulting in potential for significant reductions in energy and cost for

30 The Water Research Foundation

mainstream nutrient removal and significantly enhanced sidestream nutrient recovery (Truong
et al. 2020). Overall, high-rate CS systems are estimated to be able to capture 29 – 55% of
influent total COD, depending on wastewater characteristics, solids retention time (SRT)
applied, and degree of feast famine conditions achieved (Rahman et al 2020).

DC Water’s Blue Plains Advanced Wastewater Treatment Plant (BP AWWTP) is the largest plant
of its kind, worldwide. On an average day, BP AWWTP treats close to 300 million gallons of
wastewater and is capable of treating over a billion gallons a day at peak flow. It handles
inflows from the District of Columbia, Montgomery and Prince George’s Counties in Maryland,
and Fairfax and Loudoun counties in Virginia.

BP AWWTP employs HR-CS for secondary treatment following chemically enhanced primary
treatment (CEPT). It precedes the tertiary treatment where nitrogen removal is performed. In
addition to the primary effluent, the HR-CS system receives two return flows that are
hypothesized to influence performance: (i) the solids processing building (SPB) line produced
during the thickening and dewatering processes, and (ii) waste nitrification sludge (WNS) from
the downstream tertiary nitrification process. At BP AWWTP, it was observed that bio-
augmenting the A-stage HR-CS process with WNS may positively influence settleability (i.e.,
ability of solids to settle out of suspension and form a sludge layer at the bottom) and overall
process performance, whereas supernatant return flow from the SPB may negatively influence
process stability (Van Winckel et al. 2019). Here, it is worth noting that the high-rate systems
operate at very short solids retention times where wastage rates are high. At Blue Blains,
wastage rate is at the maximum of the solids processing capacity. Accordingly, there is no room
to perform conventional return flow management and manipulate wastage and return rates.
Figure 5-1 shows the schematic location of the HR-CS system reactors at Blue Plains AWWTP
relative to the primary and secondary clarifiers, along with the location of various flow
measurements and laboratory samples taken for the system.

 Figure 5-1. Location of the HR-CS System Reactors at the Blue Plains AWWTP Relative to the Primary and
 Secondary Clarifiers. Q_WNS: Waste Nitrification Sludge (WNS) Return Flow, Q_SBP: Solids Processing Building
 (SPB) Return Flow, ESS: Effluent Suspended Solids.

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 31

5.1.2 Define the Problem
Process stability, particularly in biomass settling, is major concern in HR-CS. Carbon diversion
relies on biosorption of slowly biodegradable carbon to biomass and subsequent solids-liquid
separation via settling in a clarifier, with carbon-laden waste biomass routed to digestion. The
system is operated in a highly dynamic manner where high mass and volumetric loading rates
are applied. As a result, a key challenge in this and other A-stage processes is sludge
settleability and the associated maintenance of effluent quality, particularly effluent suspended
solids (ESS) (AlSayed et al. 2023). An A-stage process in wastewater treatment, also known as
the adsorption stage, is a biologically enhanced primary treatment that removes organic matter
by flocculation and sorption to sludge.

In an operational-related context at the BP AWWTP, even though SPB and WNS return flow
effects were anecdotally observed, their impacts were never quantified or transformed into a
control strategy for the return streams. For example, it is not clear what should be the flow or
loading rates of WNS and SPB in relation to desirable ESS, received influent conditions, and
ongoing operational and environmental conditions. This is particularly of operational
significance because such variables (WNS and SPB flows) are the key manipulated variables,
given that solids wastage in BP AWWTP is highly dependent on the capacity of the solids
processing facilities rather than process performance. As a result, HR-CS effluent quality (ESS)
exhibits a significant fluctuation as shown in Figure 5-2. In addition to its negative influence on
resource recovery, high ESS from the HR-CS was found to increase the energy requirements in
the nutrient’s removal process. In a study performed on the tertiary treatment in BP AWWTP, it
was reported that ESS above 25 mg/L results in a statistically significant higher energy
consumption for nutrients removal.

 Figure 5-2. Effluent Suspended Solids of the HR-CS System at Blue Plains AWWTP.
 The Horizontal Red Line Indicates 25 mg/L Effluent Concentration.

Unfortunately, current mechanistic modeling approaches are inadequate for robust prediction
of dynamic behavior in HR-CS (Ngo 2023). Therefore, the ultimate goal of this work is to
develop a data-driven tool to control the secondary treatment process to keep its ESS below 25
mg/L.

32 The Water Research Foundation

This goal can be achieved through a two-step process:
1. Objective 1: Develop a data-driven model that links HR-CS operational conditions and ESS.
 For this model to serve the goal of controlling the process ESS, the project team subjected
 model development to two specific constraints:
 - Successfully capture ESS daily dynamics.
 - Be sensitive to changes in the manipulated variables (SPB and WNS flow rates).
2. Objective 2: Based on the developed model, develop a decision-making tool to inform
 operators with the optimal SPB and WNS flow rates to control ESS below 25 mg/L.

5.1.3 Get Data
Data in this study derived from historical records (since the secondary treatment process
switched to HR-CS in 2020) coupled to a 16-week dedicated data collection campaign. The time
span for data collection (historical data and data collection campaign) represents 28 months (2
years and 4 months) of full-scale data from August 2020 to December 2022. For this period,
more than 50 input variables were routinely collected.

As shown in Figure 5-3, these variables were collected from multiple sources using different
methods and with different collection frequencies. The PCS-based data are collected from
online sensors and available with high frequency on a daily basis. This includes different flow
rates like SPB flow rate (SPB_Q) and WNS flow rate (WNS_Q), dissolved oxygen (DO)
concentration via online sensors, and mixed liquor suspended solids (MLSS) probe data. The
second group of the data is collected and measured manually by the DC Water Lab team, but is
available at a relatively high frequency (more than once a week). This includes parameters like
lab-based MLSS measurements, primary effluent total suspended solids (PE_TSS), and total
soluble phosphorus of the primary effluent (PE_TSP) and secondary effluent (SE_TSP). The last
group of data sources requires more complex experimental measurements and is performed by
the DC Water Research team, and is therefore available on a less frequent and a less systematic
manner (generally available once a week.) This includes parameters like threshold of
flocculation (TOF) (i.e., the minimum concentration of suspended particles that triggers the
transition from discrete settling to the formation of larger, flocculent particles that settle more
efficiently), sludge volume index (SVI), settling flux curves, and COD and nitrogen fractions for
the primary effluent and secondary effluent. To complement historical data, a tailored 16-week
experimental plan was designed and implemented to intentionally change the WNS and SPB
control points and measure its impact. The purpose of this data collection campaign was to
impose sufficient systematic variation in the WNS and SPB return flows to robustly assess their
influence on ESS, thereby aiding in robust model development. This experimental campaign
was completed at the end of November 2022. The complete list of features is in Table 5-1.

The parameters (features) listed in Table 5-1 include both raw measurements, such as
secondary influent flow and measured effluent suspended solids, and calculated parameters
(process related parameters) such as volumetric loading rates, specific loading rates, clarifier
surface overflow rate (SOR), clarifier sludge loading rate (SLR), and solids retention time (SRT).
A major challenge with the presented dataset is the fact that the A-stage processes, HR-CS
included, are more complex with more parameters influencing effluent quality including
settleability, bioflocculation, internal storage, and clarifier loadings (AlSayed et al. 2023). It
relies on the biosorption of the slowly biodegradable carbon to biomass, and subsequent solids-
liquid separation via settling in a clarifier. The former can be influenced by influent
characteristics, operational conditions, and environmental conditions, while the latter can be
affected by biomass characteristics, loading rates, and clarifier capacity (AlSayed et al. 2023). As
a result, prior mechanistic knowledge was not used to filter out features and resulted in
considering more than 120 features resulting in high dimensionality. Another challenge with
the data is the high degree of missingness and inconsistency in such a missingness. As shown in
Figure 5-4, data availability varies significantly from one feature to another. This challenge was
multiplied with the data where sampling frequency and routine varied significantly including
COVID periods.

 Figure 5-4. Missing Data in an Exemplary Part of the Dataset.
 Each Column Represents One Feature, and each Gray Bar Represents One Point of the Data. A White (Empty) Cell
 Indicates No Data for That Feature at That Point of Observation.

5.1.4 Prepare Data
Data cleaning included elimination of selected features or time periods due to sensor
malfunctions, process upsets, maintenance events, and very low variance. Following this, in

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 37

coordination with DC Water research team, a thorough data quality review was performed to
fix errors like negative values, typing errors, and uncommon extreme values.

For data imputation, a multi-step approach was adopted. In the first step, features were
imputed using linear interpolation. This was restricted to a maximum of 2 missing consecutive
days in a week (7 days). To validate the assumption that the weekly data follows a linear
pattern, the data was split in weeks and a linear model was fitted into each week and the
goodness of fit was evaluated to confirm its suitability. In the second step, a multivariate
imputation was used to leverage the high dimensionality and co-linearity in the dataset. First,
features were selected using maximum relevance minimum redundancy (mRMR) method. Next,
an optimized Huber regression model was trained using the existing points and the fitted model
was used to predict the missing values. Huber regression is a robust linear regression technique
used in statistics and machine learning for modeling the relationship between variables in the
presence of outliers and influential data points. A threshold of prediction accuracy (validation
R2 above 0.5) was set to avoid imputing features using inaccurate models. An example of the
results of the multivariate imputation is shown in Figure 5-5. The last step was using the moving
average for features expected to remain constant for a specific period of time. This period of
time was determined according to process knowledge. For primary effluent characteristics, no
imputation was performed at this step as there is no prior knowledge of changes in influent
characteristics. Seven days moving average were used to impute features related to SPB and
WNS streams as they are generated based on relatively slow processes, while 3 days moving
average were used for the HR-CS process conditions like sludge characteristics as this was the
average SRT of the system.

 Figure 5-5. Total Suspended Solids of the SPB Flow After Imputation.
 Red Dots Are Original Data and Blue Dots Are Imputed Data.

After data cleaning and manually eliminating extreme values, the decision was made not to
apply any statistical outlier removal methods. As the goal of the project is to capture and
prevent extreme events, it was suggested that keeping such high values is desirable. Given the
stochastic and dynamic nature of the ESS, it was smoothed by using a 7-day rolling average.
This fits well with the project objectives as the goal is to make weekly or bi-weekly decisions on
SPB and WNS flow rates. The dataset was normalized using a Min-Max scaler, to ensure all
values were between 0 and 1, which significantly improved model performance by ensuring
that all features contribute equally. During experimentation, other scaling techniques were

38 The Water Research Foundation

tested to mitigate the observed skewness like quantile transformer. However, Min-Max scaler
was found to be the most reliable scaling approach.

5.1.5 Train Model
This section covers the first objective of Application 1, which entails process modelling using
data-driven models, with the models including SPB and WNS flow rates and other features to
accurately model ESS. This was performed in two major steps as detailed in the following
sections. In brief, the project team used a minimal feature set (high-frequency features that
have at least 50% of the days available) at first to compare 10 different algorithms. Following,
the top performing algorithms were selected and were carried out through a rigorous feature
selection and hyperparameter optimization. Finally, one model was selected and re-trained on
the entire dataset to be used for deployment.

In all cases, nested cross-validation (nested CV) was used. Nested CV is an incremental learning
approach where learning happens at multiple levels or stages, building upon each other
incrementally. Nested CV was selected due to the small sample to features ratio in this dataset.
More than 120 features were candidates to be tested and only 866 instances (days) were
available with a very high missingness rate (exceeded 50% in many days). Another reason for
this selection was the stochastic nature of the data and inconsistent variation in ESS over the
examined period. Nested CV provides a robust technique that allows the use of the entire
dataset in training and testing while minimizing bias and overfitting. It employs two layers of
CV: an outer loop for model evaluation and an inner loop for feature selection and
hyperparameter tuning. In the outer loop, the dataset is partitioned into multiple folds (5 or 10
folds in this study), with each fold being held out in turn as a test set while the remaining folds
form the training set. Within this training set, the inner loop conducts another round of CV (5 or
10 folds in this study) to optimize hyperparameters, tune model architecture, and select
features. This process is repeated for each fold in the outer loop, ensuring a comprehensive and
unbiased evaluation.

In the first two steps, the inner loops (5-fold CV) were used for the selection of algorithms used
and feature selection. Subsequently, in the last step, selected models and their selected
features were used for hyperparameter optimization using the inner loops (10-fold CV) and
final models with different imbalance treatment methods were compared based on the testing
results from the outer loops (10-fold CV).

5.1.5.1 Preliminary Model Selection
To perform preliminary algorithm selection, a certain dataset with selected features needs to
be used. This was performed by selecting features with a missing ratio of more than 50% (at
least 50% of the days are available). The goal here was to define a starting point to decide on
the algorithms to be used. Using this dataset, ten different algorithms were compared to model
the HR-CS process. These algorithms vary in complexity, explainability, and computational
speed. A subset consisted of relatively simple and explainable algorithms, namely Linear
regression (LR), Huber regression, LASSO regression, and Ridge regression. Huber regression, as
explained before, is robust against outliers. LASSO and Ridge utilize L1 and L2 regularization to
improve generalization and reduce overfitting. Additionally, a group of slightly more complex

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 39

algorithms were employed including K-nearest neighbors (KNN) and Decision Tree. KNN is
simple and intuitive, and works by finding the majority class among the nearest neighbors.
However, it can be computationally expensive as it requires storing the entire training dataset
and calculating distances for each prediction. Decision Tree is a highly interpretable algorithm
that splits data into branches based on feature values. While easy to understand and visualize,
decision trees can easily overfit, capturing noise in the training data, which is why different
ensemble algorithms were also evaluated. Furthermore, ensemble algorithms tested included
Adaptive boosting (AdaBoost), Extreme gradient boosting (XGBoost), and random forest (RF).
AdaBoost is a boosting algorithm that combines the predictions of multiple weak learners
(typically decision trees) to form a strong predictor. It adjusts the weights of misclassified
instances to focus on hard-to-classify examples, improving accuracy but at the cost of increased
computational complexity. XGBoost is an advanced implementation of gradient boosting. It
optimizes both computational speed and model performance. It uses parallel processing and
regularization to handle large datasets and reduce overfitting, making it powerful but complex
to tune. RF is an ensemble method that creates multiple decision trees and merges their
predictions to improve accuracy and control overfitting. While more robust than a single
decision tree, it loses some interpretability due to the ensemble nature. Finally, a more
complex and less interpretable algorithm was used which is a support vector machine (SVM)
with radial function basis (rbf) kernel. SVM works by finding the optimal hyperplane that
minimizes the error, and the rbf kernel allows it to handle complex, non-linear relationships by
mapping input features into a higher-dimensional space. It is effective for high-dimensional
spaces, but it can be computationally intensive, especially with large datasets.

A preliminary space of hyperparameters was defined and Grid search optimization with 5-fold
cross-validation was performed to evaluate different algorithms. These models are trained to
predict ESS based on the pre-selected high-frequency features. The models’ goodness of fit was
evaluated with root mean squared error (RMSE) and R2, as shown in Figure 5-6. Also, the extent
of overfitting is considered which was determined based on the percent increase in training R2
in comparison with testing. It can be seen that all simple and interpretable models including LR,
Huber, LASSO, and Ridge are underfitting. As anticipated, Decision Tree obviously overfitted the
data exhibiting the highest overfit above 50%. An attempt to highly regularize the algorithm
was made by restricting hyperparameters like tree maximum depth and minimum split
thresholds but it resulted in severe underfitting. KNN and XGBoost showed good performance
as validation R2 was above 0.65 in both algorithms; however, achieving R2 of 1 in training across
all splits makes these models very prone to overfitting in the future. Only AdaBoost, RF, and
SVM showed good performance with validation R2 values equal to 0.6 or higher with acceptable
ranges of overfitting around 25%.

40 The Water Research Foundation

  Figure 5-6. Performance of 10 Different Algorithms Using Features with at Least 50% of Instances Available.
 The Horizontal Red Line is at R2 = 0.5.
 LR: Linear Regression, Huber: Huber Regression, LASSO(L1): LASSO Regression, Ridge(L2): Ridge Regression, KNN:
 K-Nearest Neighbour, DT: Decision Tree, AdaBoost: Adaptive Boosting, XGBoost: Extreme Gradient Boosting, RF:

5.1.5.2 Feature selection
Pre-Selected Features and Features Elimination
Feature selection was performed in multiple steps. As shown in Figure 5-7, the first step is
selecting features with missing ratio equal or less than 50%. Such features were used in
preliminary model selection. Then, the artificial contrasts with ensembles (ACE) method was
used for the selection of the key features from the features with a missing ratio below 50%. ACE
is unsupervised and does not require a pre-selected number of features or pre-determined
stopping criteria. It works by first estimating feature importance using an algorithm of
ensemble of trees (RF was used). Then, for each feature in the dataset, an artificial random
variable (called “artificial contrast variable”) is created from the same distribution as its
corresponding feature but is independent of the target. A feature is eliminated if it has lower
importance, that is artificial contrast (Tuv et al. 2006).

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 41

 Figure 5-7. Feature Selection Method.

Incremental Learning
Following the ACE step, an incremental learning method (a modified Wrapper Dominating
Hybrid Sequential Forward Floating Search) was used. In this method, features were added to
the dataset in a sequential manner. Every time a feature is added, model performance is
evaluated. If improved, permutation feature importance is calculated and the feature with least
importance is dropped. If performance improved, this feature is dropped, and permutation
feature importance is determined again. This process is repeated until the performance is not
improving. If performance did not improve by dropping features, the added feature is selected
without dropping any features. The incremental learning process for one of the iterations is
shown in Figure 5-8. This process continues until the samples to features ratio (S/F ratio) is less
than 10. While there is no clear threshold for the S/F ratio, it is widely reported that at least S/F
ratio of 10 is needed for reliable and generalizable results (Zhu et al. 2023).

42 The Water Research Foundation

 Figure 5-8. The Incremental Learning Process for One Iteration, as Used for Feature Selection.
 Features in Red were Selected for Inclusion in the Model.

The aforementioned feature selection process was repeated 5 times for the inner CV loop. This
was performed for the three selected models AdaBoost, RF, and SVM. For each algorithm, the
final selected features were determined using majority voting. Therefore, three algorithms with
their selected features were determined as shown in Table 5-2. Here, as seen in the table,
algorithms were forced to keep SPB and WNS flowrates in their feature set to ensure that the
manipulated variables were included in the model. Also, it can be seen from the table that the
number of features and the S/F ratio resulting varies significantly between the three
algorithms. Apart from the performance, higher S/F ratio and lower feature number is
preferable as it makes the algorithm less prone to overfitting, less complex, and lower in
computational cost. Interestingly, SVM, which selects the least number of features, relies on
parameters that are easy to measure and track like flow rates, temperature, solids of the return
streams.

Model Optimization and Imbalance treatment
In this stage, a more rigorous hyperparameter optimization (HPO) was performed along with
different methods for data imbalance treatment. A persistent observation across previous
experimentations was that models showed reasonable validation scores where R2 ranged
between 0.55 and 0.7, and RMSE was below 7 mg/L. The main limitation was the high degree of

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 43

overfitting observed, which exceeded 30% in many cases. In HPO, it was ensured that models
are regularized, and a variance bias trade-off can be reached.

Another observation was the failure of the models to predict high ESS values (>25 mg TSS/L).
This stems from the imbalanced dataset, which is a typical challenge with data obtained from
wastewater treatment. In most of the days, the process is performing well, and ESS is below 25
mg/L, as a result, the algorithm prioritizes predicting the common low values and ignores the
high values. To fix this issue, several imbalance treatment methods were tested on the training
dataset, including synthetic minority oversampling technique (SMOTE), adaptive synthetic
oversampling (ADASYN), weight-adjusted loss function, and quantile transformation of the
target. SMOTE is an oversampling method that creates synthetic samples using k-nearest
neighbors of the minority instances in the original dataset, thus addressing imbalances in the
target variable distribution. ADASYN is similar to SMOTE, but instead of oversampling the
minority instances of the data, it generates more samples similar to those that are difficult to
learn. A weight-adjusted loss function assigns different weights to instances based on their
representation in the dataset, penalizing errors associated with the less represented instances
more heavily to improve model performance. This was performed by assigning weights equal to
the inverse of the probability density function of the training data or by assigning higher
weights to values beyond the interquartile range. Quantile transformation of the target
involves transforming the target variable to follow a uniform or normal distribution, stabilizing
variance and making the modeling process more robust to imbalanced data.

To evaluate performance, R2 was used. In addition, as naïve baseline model, we selected the
average ESS value. The RMSE reduction from the average model, referred to as RMSE
reduction, was also used to evaluate model performance. In all three algorithms, higher
performance was observed when weight-adjusted loss function was used. Similar performance
was observed when using the inverse of the probability distribution function or adjusting the
weights based on the data quantiles. As shown in Figure 5-9, random forest showed the worse
performance with Testing R2 of 0.71±0.07 and RMSE reduction of 47.20±6.50%. This was a
direct result of constraining the number of estimators and tree depth which could have led to
slightly higher performance but with severe overfitting beyond 30%. In the meantime, testing
results obtained from Adaboost and SVM were similar with testing R2 around 0.80 and RMSE
reduction of 55-57%. SVM selected for lower number of features and trained on almost double
the data. Therefore, it was selected as the model for deployment.

44 The Water Research Foundation

  Figure 5-9. Testing Results of the Optimal Models for Adaboost (left), Random Forest (middle), SVM (right).

5.1.6 Deploy Model
As mentioned earlier, our ultimate objective is to use the developed model as an offline
decision-making tool to inform operators about how to manage WNS and SPB flowrates to
reduce ESS or at least keep it below 25 mg/L. However, it can be implied from Figure 5-10 that
ESS is affected more by the temperatures and WNS total suspended solids (WNS TSS) than SPB
and WNS flow rates. This is based on the observation that in many instances temperature and
WNS TSS are the main predictors. To explain, Figure 5-10 is based on Shapley feature
explanations (SHAP), which is a model-agnostic method derived from cooperative game theory
to explain the contribution of each feature to a model's predictions. It calculates the average
marginal contribution of a feature across all possible combinations of features, ensuring a fair
distribution of importance. The average marginal contribution in this context refers to the
mean increase or decrease in a model's prediction when a specific feature is added to a subset
of other features.

 Figure 5-10. SHapley Additive exPlanations (SHAP) Values Demonstrating F Effect on ESS Prediction.

Therefore, the developed tool shall answer two questions for the operators. The first question
is “under the existing conditions, are the existing WNS and SPB flowrates contributing to the
observed ESS”, while the second question is “under the existing conditions, how will ESS change
if WNS and SPB flowrates change”.

The suggested deployment tool will rely on the pre-trained model. This model can be re-trained
on a monthly or bi-weekly basis according to the data collection. To answer the first question,

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 45

Shapley values can be used to identify the current contribution of each feature, as shown in
Figure 5-11. For Day 2 in the figure as an example, the model prediction for ESS was 34.46 mg
TSS/L, the shift of this value from the base value (24.06 mg TSS/L, which is the average) was
predicted to be most significantly influenced by the WNS flow rate (W_WNS_Q), followed by
SPB flow rate and temperature. In contrast, in Day 1, WNS TSS mainly contributed to increasing
ESS to 28.06 mg TSS/L, while WNS and SPB flow rates contributed to lowering it back to 26.25
mg TSS/L. The challenge is that this works only for fitted or trained data. Hence, this can be
implemented either by re-training the model on current conditions, or by finding the most
similar point in the training dataset using k-nearest neighbor.

 Day 1

 Day 2

 Figure 5-11. Contribution of Each Feature to ESS Prediction of Two Different Days.

In addition, the model can be fed the current conditions (the input features) as a new instance
in addition to all possible flow rates for WNS and SPB, and the algorithm will generate different
predictions according the WNS and SPB values. These values can be plotted in a heat map
similar to the one in Figure 5-12. The center of the heatmap can be adjusted based on the
current ESS. This heat map will answer both questions; if all values are similar to the existing
ESS, this means WNS and SPB return flows are not the main contributors to ESS. For the second
question, the heat map will identify the zones of operation where low ESS is expected under
the existing conditions.

46 The Water Research Foundation

 Figure 5-12. Heat Map Showing the Effect of the SPB and WNS Flow on ESS.
 The Coloring is Centered Around 25 mg/L as the Threshold Suggested by DC Water.

5.1.7 Lessons Learned
• Offline model-based deployment was developed using support vector machine (SVM)
 model.
• Addressing high dimensionality (120+ features) and inconsistent missingness required multi-
 step imputation and rigorous pre-processing.
• Nested incremental learning feature selection selected 6 features in the final SVM model.
• Careful model selection is crucial; simple models like linear regression underfitted, while
 some complex models like ensembles and neural networks overfitted. Model optimization
 for data imbalance was performed.
• Deployment tool allows retraining on current data and visualization via heatmaps for
 dynamic operational guidance in terms of optimal return flows for each treatment trai

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 47

5.2 BNR: ABAC / AvN + PdNA (Application 2)
Water Resource Recovery Facilities (WRRFs) employing Ammonia-Based Aeration Control
(ABAC) can sometimes be burdened with outdated unstable control systems that struggle from
disturbances occurring in conjunction with long delays. The ammonia control may fail to be
precise, forcing staff to overcompensate with large safety factors, potentially leading to
inefficiencies that manifest as wasted operating costs. The research at HRSD explores a
promising solution: implementing data-driven solutions into the controllers. These tools,
including digital twins (DTs) and data-driven models (DDMs), could be a game-changer within
existing ABAC frameworks. However, it should still be recognized that there is no such thing as
a one-size-fits-all approach.

To find the optimal balance between effectiveness and complexity, HRSD tested three different
control strategies at three different HRSD WRRFs. The most challenging scenario occurred at
the Nansemond Treatment Plant (NTP), which involved rather unpredictable influent loads and
plug flow reactor orientation. Here, HRSD experimented with a feedforward-feedback
controller that included ML. The NTP case study is presented in this section (Section 5.2). At the
HRSD Army Base Treatment Plant (ABTP), a classic feedback Proportional-Integral (PI) controller
was employed and proved effective, likely due to a favorable residence time distribution (RTD)
resembling something close to a continuous stirred tank reactor (CSTR). The third facility was
the Virginia Initiative Plant (VIP), where influent load was rather predictable, but the RTD was
more plug flow. This warranted a more complex feedforward-feedback approach. These latter
two control strategies are not featured in this report.

5.2.1 Background (Set the Stage)
HRSD employs a 5-stage Bardenpho at their NTP and leverages Ammonia Based Aeration
Control (ABAC) to try and maintain a constant ammonia (NHx) concentration leaving the
aerobic zone (Figure 5-13). In the figure, flow enters the biological process from the Primary
Clarifiers (PCs) and then exits the biological process to then enter the Secondary Clarifiers (SCs).
In between the PCs and the SCs are the 5 stages of the Bardenpho, including the Anaerobic
(ANA), 1st Anoxic (1 ANX), Aerobic (AER Zones 1-3), 2nd Anoxic (2 ANX), and Re-Aeration (RE-
AER) zones.

48 The Water Research Foundation

 Figure 5-13. 5-Stage Bardenpho at the HRSD Nansemond Treatment Plant.

If the objective of ABAC can be achieved, there are numerous benefits to the facility. First, the
facility would only expend the energy required to nitrify influent NHx down to the aerobic zone
effluent NHx setpoint, minimizing electrical costs associated with blower energy. Allowing some
NHx to slip through the biological process to chlorine disinfection also aids in chloramine
formation, stabilizing the chlorine residual at the end of the chlorine contact tank, and saving
on sodium hypochlorite costs. Therefore, the benefits here are two-fold and they come in the
form of reduced energy and chemical costs.

These energy and chemical costs can be reduced even further if ABAC is modified to become
Ammonia-versus-NOx (AvN) control ahead of a process known as mainstream partial
denitrification anammox (PdNA) in the second stage anoxic zone. For PdNA, far less nitrification
is required and since the NOx produced from nitrification is only partially denitrified, external
carbon savings can also be realized. Understanding the possibilities, HRSD NTP is beginning the
retrofit of their second stage anoxic zones in the summer of 2024 to start performing
mainstream PdNA. It should be noted that for this technology to be successful, the facility must
employ effective upstream aeration control to achieve the AvN setpoint.

5.2.2 Define the Problem
Historically, the HRSD NTP has used a feedback proportional-integral-derivative (PID) controller
for ABAC. This was a cascade controller, consisting of an NHx controller that produces a
dissolved oxygen setpoint (DOSP), DO controller that produces an air flow setpoint (Air QSP), and
an Air Q controller that produces an aeration control valve (ACV) position output (Figure 5-14).

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 49

The DO setpoints from the NHx controller were applied the same to both Aerobic Z2 and
Aerobic Z3. The location of the NHx measurement (yellow star in Figure 5-13) was not ideal
given that the biological process ahead of the NHx measurement is approximately 11
continuous stirred tank reactors (CSTRs) in-series based on a dye study.

For the dye study, dye was injected at the inlet of Aerobic Z2, and samples were subsequently
collected at the outlet of Aerobic Z3. Preliminary analysis suggested that the combined
hydraulic behavior of Aerobic Z2 and Z3 could be approximated as a series of five CSTRs. To
validate this assumption, a modelling study was performed. The model was created in
Dynamita’s Sumo software and included five CSTRs equivalent in total volume to Aerobic Z2
and Z3. The modelling exercise consisted of a dynamic simulation in which a metal tracer was
introduced in the influent of the first CSTR under flow conditions aligning with those from the
day of the dye study. The mass of metal tracer injected in the model also matched the mass of
dye introduced during the hands-on experiment. The results from this dye study and the
modelling evaluation are displayed in Figure 5-15.

The physical layout of the three zones upstream of the first common aerated channel
comprises six CSTRs in-series. Altogether, it can be reasonably surmised that the biological

50 The Water Research Foundation

process consists of 11 CSTRs in-series upstream of the NHx measurement taken at the Aerobic
Z3 outlet (6 + 5 CSTRs for Aerobic Z2 & Z3). In addition to the predominantly plug flow hydraulic
behavior, the influent NHx load entering Aerobic Z2 is characterized by significant variability. As
illustrated in Figure 5-16, diurnal fluctuations in NHx concentration display inconsistent
magnitudes and patterns. The presence or absence of a shoulder on the diurnal peak adds to
this irregularity. Furthermore, extreme (rain) events, such as the pronounced peak depicted in
the middle of the figure, exacerbate the unpredictable nature of the influent load. Given this
complex and dynamic pattern, establishing a direct correlation between time-of-day and the
DOSP proves challenging at the HRSD NTP.

Åmand (2014), supported by operational experiences at the HRSD NTP, highlights the
detrimental effects of locating the NHx measurement in the final aerated zone of similarly
configured biological processes. These destabilizing delays hinder controller performance, as
illustrated in Figure 5-17, displaying the performance of the baseline feedback-only ABAC
controller and its inability to maintain Aerobic Z3 effluent NHx concentrations at setpoint. To
address these shortcomings, the feedback approach can be expanded to include a feedforward
component. This requires placing an NHx sensor at the reactor inlet (red star in Figure 5-13) and
employing a model to determine the DOSP. Vrečko et al. (2006) demonstrated this strategy
using Activated Sludge Model No. 1 (ASM1), which incorporates a single-step nitrification
process from NHx to NO3- and a unified nitrifier population, hereafter referred to as NITO
(Equation 5-1).
