 −1

Equation 5-1 incorporates several key variables. These include the Aeration Tank (AT) volume
(V), AT effluent NHx setpoint (NHxSP), total AT influent flow rate (Q), and the AT influent NHx
concentration (NHx0 – red star in Figure 5-13). Nitrifier kinetic parameters are also included.
The nitrifier specific growth rate (μNITO), the nitrifier half saturation coefficient for oxygen (KO-
NITO), and the nitrifier half saturation coefficient for NHx (KNHx-NITO), adjusted for the current
temperature, help inform the DOSP. The nitrifier concentration (XNITO) and nitrifier yield (YNITO)
make up the remaining variables in the DOSP calculation.

While Vrečko et al. (2006) showcased the potential of mechanistic models, specifically ASM1,
for feedforward NHx control, their inherent limitations in isolation are acknowledged. A
significant stride towards addressing these limitations is the development of hybrid models,
which combine mechanistic and data-driven components. These models offer the potential to
overcome simplifying assumptions that degrade mechanistic model performance, thereby
enhancing their forecasting capabilities in real-world operational scenarios. For these reasons,
due to expected superior extrapolation accuracy, and to allow building on Vrečko’s work, a
hybrid model was used in this case.

For nearly a decade, identical feedback ABAC and feedforward-feedback methanol dosing
controllers have been employed in parallel process trains. Yet, the HRSD NTP consistently
observes substantial differences in air and methanol demands between these trains. These

52 The Water Research Foundation

disparities suggest underlying variations between the parallel reactors, possibly due to uneven
load distribution. Furthermore, inconsistencies between handheld and in-situ DO
measurements question the accuracy of the latter in representing overall zone conditions.
These challenges highlight the limitations of a mechanistic model that relies on assumptions of
equal load distribution, which would affect parallel train-to-train model performance, and
representative DO data, which would affect the whole-plant model performance. In reality,
numerous factors likely contribute to mechanistic model inaccuracies, and these hypothesized
issues represent only two. The other contributing factors are simply unknown.

Figure 5-18 through Figure 5-20 illustrate the performance of the whole-plant mechanistic
model and its limitations, likely due to unrepresentative DO data and other unknown factors.
The figures compare average modeled Aerobic Z3 effluent NHx concentrations to
corresponding measured values for three distinct 4-day periods in October 2023. The model
was calibrated via Nelder-Mead optimization, which yielded an optimal µmax-NITO value of 0.41.
This estimate was based on measured (observed) influent Total Kjeldahl Nitrogen (TKN),
influent flow rates, recycle flow rates, waste flow rates, temperatures, and DO concentrations
for the entire month of October. This optimized value outperformed the default 0.90 in
minimizing the mean error between modelled and observed effluent NHx concentrations under
the given conditions. However, the figures clearly show that the model's accuracy fluctuates,
with periods of overprediction, underprediction, and acceptable performance. This
inconsistency suggests that a constant µmax-NITO for an entire month may hinder model
performance. Therefore, a more dynamic approach, such as daily µmax-NITO optimization using a
relatively short sliding window, might be necessary to improve model accuracy.

While rapid and frequent adjustments to µmax-NITO could potentially improve model accuracy,
such an approach is inconsistent with the established understanding of nitrifier kinetics. Absent
a major, unexpected inhibitory event, changes in nitrifier behavior typically occur over longer
timescales rather than daily fluctuations. An alternative approach involves less frequent
updates to µmax-NITO combined with a machine learning component that allows for mechanistic
model error (errmech) corrections. By integrating a Data-driven model’s (DDM’s) mechanistic
model error forecasts into Equation 5-1, we can refine the DOSP calculation. Incorporating these
forecasted errors into the ammonia removal term's denominator, as detailed in Equation 5-2,

54 The Water Research Foundation

allows the DOSP to adjust accordingly based on the error's magnitude and direction. In this case,
an error is positive if the mechanistic model NHx is greater than the observed NHx, and
negative if the mechanistic model NHx is lower. Therefore, the error forecast is added to the
NHxSP so, for example, if the forecasted error is negative, then the hybrid model will see this as
additional NHx to be removed beyond (NHx0 – NHxSP).

 𝐾𝐾𝑂𝑂−𝑁𝑁𝑁𝑁𝑁𝑁𝑁𝑁
    𝐷𝐷𝐷𝐷𝑆𝑆𝑆𝑆 =                                                                                                         (5-2)
 −1

Note that the only modification made to Equation 5-1 is the inclusion of a mechanistic model
error term. Importantly, it is the DDM’s error forecast for one Hydraulic Residence Time (HRT)
in the future.

5.2.3 Get Data
The HRSD NTP leverages its online digital twin (DT) to forecast the mechanistic model error
based on historical data. This DT is a digital replica of the plant’s biological process, constructed
using the Sumo software by Dynamita (Sigale, France). It receives real-time data from the
facility's control system via the Open Platform Communications (OPC) protocol. This data
includes a variety of parameters such as the influent flow rate, recycle flow rates, waste flow
rate, methanol flow rates to the second stage anoxic zones, DO concentrations for all aerobic
zones, and the raw influent temperature. The influent TKN concentration from the laboratory is
also ingested by the DT.

Every minute, the DT generates a new modelled NHx value, which is then compared to the
corresponding measured value from the NHx analyzer. The difference between these two
values constitutes the mechanistic model error. While the DT can produce a new modelled NHx
every minute, the NHx analysis requires 20 minutes. Consequently, the time step interval
chosen for the proof-of-concept modelling that is described in the following two sections was a
20-minute interval to align with the analyzer measurement frequency.

5.2.4 Prepare Data
While technically a multivariate model, the DDM forecasting errmech relies solely on historical
errmech data. Specifically, the model incorporates the current errmech value, its recent change
between t-1 and t, and its rolling 24-hour statistical information (median, maximum, minimum).
These features were selected based on their importance for overall model performance and,
crucially, for accurately forecasting errmech peaks and valleys. Precisely forecasting these
extremes is essential for calculating DOSPs that manage diurnal TKN load fluctuations and
minimize controller errors. Note that given the low number of features, it was quite easy to
surpass the recommended sample-to-feature ratio of 10-to-1.

October 2023 errmech data were leveraged to establish the proof-of-concept. After a preliminary
visual inspection to identify anomalies, the data underwent a smoothing process using a
Savitzky-Golay filter, known for its wide application for instrument signal smoothing. Figure 5-
21 illustrates the raw and filtered data for one of the online tanks during this time,

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 55

demonstrating the filter's effectiveness in mitigating the step-like trends and residual anomalies
(see arrows). After filtering, the previously described features were calculated from the
smoothed errmech data. To align the model outputs with the features, the smoothed data were
shifted seven time steps, equivalent to the aeration tank’s average HRT of two hours and
twenty minutes (given the 20-minute time step interval). Figure 5-22 and Table 5-3 summarize
the approach to prepare the time series data for modelling.

5.2.5 Train Model
To rigorously evaluate model performance, October 2023 data were sourced from three
separate ATs. To prevent data leakage and ensure robust model testing, the models were
trained on only two of these ATs and the third was reserved for testing. Furthermore, the
training and testing data were split temporally, using the first 80% of data for model training
and the remaining 20% for testing. This approach ensured that the model was tested on both a
different AT and a distinct timeframe compared to the training data.

To enhance the robustness of this evaluation, a second test was conducted on a completely
different AT, entirely separate from those used for initial training and testing. Importantly, the
data for this second test was sourced from April 2024, providing an independent dataset for
model assessment.

A variety of DDMs were developed to forecast errmech. These included a simple average model,
a multivariate linear regression model, two eXtreme Gradient (XG) Boosted models, and two
long short-term memory (LSTM) neural network (NN) models. To optimize hyperparameters for
one of the XG Boost and LSTM models, grid search and Hyperband tuning were employed,
respectively. The models are detailed in the list below:
1. An “average” model, that is simply based on the average errmech from the training set.
2. A multivariate linear regression model.
3. A “simple” XG Boost model that uses a tree depth of 3, number of estimators of 50, and the
 default learning rate.
4. A tuned XG Boost model. In this case, grid search is used to determine the ideal tree depth,
 number of estimators, and learning rate that minimizes the training set error.
5. A “simple” LSTM NN model. This one has two layers with only one neuron in each layer and
 uses the default learning rate.
6. A tuned LSTM NN model. For this model, a Hyperband tuner was used to determine the
 ideal number of neurons in each hidden layer and ideal learning rate that minimizes the
 training set error.
Table 5-4 presents the performance of the explored models across both test sets. Based on the
results, the multivariate linear regression model excelled in Test 1, while the Simple LSTM NN
model demonstrated superior performance in Test 2. Given their strong performances and
simplicity, an ensemble model combining these two models was chosen as the final forecasting

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 57

solution. This approach offers a balance of accuracy and efficiency for predicting errmech one
HRT into the future.
 Table 5-4. Model Performance of Various DDMs Explored for Forecasting errmech.
 # of Test 1 Error Test 2 Error
 Model Parameters October 2023 April 2024
                          Average             -            6.97            2.31
                          Multi LR            6            0.17            0.71
                      Simple XG Boost       396            1.89            0.68
                      Tuned XG Boost        144            1.77            0.62
                      Simple LSTM NN         59            1.42            0.55
                      Tuned LSTM NN        3,533           0.67            0.65

Figure 5-23 illustrates the performance of the standalone mechanistic model, using an
optimized µmax-NITO, in forecasting Aerobic Z3 effluent NHx concentrations for the same
timeframe as the test set. Figure 5-24 presents the performance of the hybrid model, where
the error forecasts from the DDM ensemble have been subtracted from the mechanistic
model's predictions. Notably, the hybrid model residuals continued to exhibit periodicity even
after applying the DDM ensemble. Recognizing this pattern, a fast Fourier transform (FFT)
model was employed to capture and correct for this periodicity. A Fourier transform is a
mathematical algorithm that transforms a signal from the time domain to the frequency
domain, revealing its constituent frequencies. Essentially, it decomposes a complex signal into
its fundamental sinusoidal components, allowing for analysis of its frequency content. Here,
the FFT model uses a 1-day sliding window and the 10 most dominant frequencies within that
window to make its forecasts. The final hybrid model, incorporating all data-driven
components, including the FFT, can be evaluated in Figure 5-25.

5.2.6 Deploy Model
The feedforward controller, as defined in Equation 5-2, was implemented exclusively for
Aerobic Z2. It was deployed for real-time active control, eliminating the need for a human in the
loop. Figure 5-26 provides a visual representation of this controller, where uFF denotes the DOSP
output. To address potential inaccuracies in the feedforward controller, a feedback PID
component was incorporated. This feedback mechanism generates a correction factor, or trim,
which is multiplied by uFF to produce a modified DOSP (uFF,tr). This adjusted DOSP can be either
higher or lower than the original uFF, depending on the deviation of the measured NHx (y) from
the setpoint (r). The correction factor is designed to update gradually.

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 59

A distinct feedback controller architecture was employed for Aerobic Z3, as illustrated in Figure
5-27. Unlike Z2, this controller lacks a feedforward component and maintains the baseline strict
feedback-only PID architecture. However, key differences exist. Firstly, the DOSP for Z3 (uFB) is
independent from that of Z2 (uFF,tr). Secondly, due to Z3's reduced size and its position within
the AT where NHx measurements are taken, the controller was tuned aggressively to create a
“trimming” or “polishing” zone.

The updated controllers for both zones were integrated into AT4. Figure 5-28 compares the
NHx concentrations at the end of AT4 with those of AT6, which continued to operate with the
baseline controller. Prior to the advanced controller implementation, controller errors in both
ATs were comparable. A controlled experiment was conducted in AT4 by fixing the feedback
correction factor for Z2 at 1.0 and maintaining the original slow tuning for the Z3 controller.
Despite these constraints, Figure 5-28 demonstrates a significant reduction in peak NHx
concentrations in AT4 compared to AT6.

By allowing the correction factor for the Z2 controller to fluctuate and optimizing the Z3
controller tuning, as shown in Figure 5-29, the controller mean absolute error (MAE) was
ultimately reduced from 0.81 mg/L to 0.20 mg/L.

This shows that this project was successful in leveraging these cutting-edge tools to achieve a
superior level of aeration/NHx control in advance of mainstream PdNA. In this way, the project
was relatively successful; however, there were many failures and lessons learned along the
way.

5.2.7 Lessons Learned
Three of the key lessons are highlighted below:
1. Additional parameters in the model do not necessarily result in a higher level of
 performance.
2. The practical side to implementation and operationalizing the model should garner as much
 or more consideration as the modelling itself.
3. Controller deployment should leverage feedback, if possible, in addition to feedforward.
 Relying on feedforward control alone, even when there is high confidence in the model
 prior to deployment, is not recommended.
4. In the case of time series sensor data, univariate signal pattern recognition and time series
 forecasting may be more critical to improved controller performance than a multivariate
 DDM based on instruments scattered across the facility.

62 The Water Research Foundation

Meeting Targets
Table 1-1 lists 20% aeration energy savings and/or 20% methanol savings as targets for
Application 2. While this study didn't directly prove these exact savings, it demonstrated the
level of control necessary for effective PdNA can be achieved. It is through PdNA that these
savings are expected to be realized. However, this study primarily focused on upstream control,
rather than PdNA itself.

5.3 Disinfection: PAA (Application 3)
5.3.1 Background (Set the Stage)
As WRRFs look to upgrade old gaseous chlorine or liquid sodium hypochlorite-based
disinfection processes, PAA disinfection has become an attractive retrofit option that produces
fewer carcinogenic disinfection byproducts (DBPs) than chlorine-based disinfection. However,
PAA is significantly more expensive than conventional sodium hypochlorite. Thus, precision
dose control is key to minimizing cost and ecological impacts. Unfortunately, PAA is difficult to
model at full-scale. For example, the Metro Water Recovery Robert W. Hite Treatment Facility
(RWHTF) has found both the monitoring and optimization of PAA difficult; such that excessive
disinfectant dosing is common. Data-driven models are needed to better understand and
quantify real-time disinfection performance and realize chemical reductions.

5.3.2 Define the Problem
PAA dosing is not as straightforward as using a set initial PAA concentration to achieve a
required log removal of E. coli. Rather, PAA is currently controlled based on the estimated CT,
which can be calculated by integrating PAA concentration over hydraulic retention time. PAA
concentration in secondary treated wastewaters follows a first order decay model. The
required CT is then determined by a second model (double exponential mechanistic model)
which explains the relationship between CT and E. coli log inactivation. Finally, the required log
inactivation is calculated from the influent E. coli concentration and the discharge limit.
However, there are complications to how the estimations made at each step influence the final
PAA dose calculation. This leads to significant error and results in a very large safety factor
needing to be applied in order to guarantee the E. coli discharge limit is met.

Influent E. coli is measured using a grab sample immediately upstream of the PAA dosing point
and requires 24-hours for laboratory analysis. To estimate the current influent E. coli, Metro
uses a running 3-day average of previous influent E. coli measurements. The calculated log
removal is then used with historical CT data to fit a mechanistic model, but it appears that CT is
not the only factor that influences E. coli log removal (Figure 5-30).

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 63

Figure 5-30. E. Coli Log Inactivation as a Function of Integrated Ct at RWHTF. The Red Line Represents a Double
 Exponential Model Fit to All Samples.

Once the required CT is established, PAA dose can be calculated using previously measured
first-order decay parameters and an online analyzer. A PAA analyzer located approximately 10
minutes downstream of the dosing point during average flow conditions adjusts for the initial
oxidative demand of PAA (i.e., difference between initial PAA dose and the actual initial
concentration) and the first-order decay rate (k) is measured quarterly in the lab. However, k is
not as constant as previously assumed (Figure 5-31).

 Figure 5-31. (Left) PAA Concentration as a Function of Hydraulic Retention Time (HRT) in the Disinfection Basin
 for Different Days and Different Times During the Day. The Black Dots Are PAA Measurements and the Blue
 Lines Are First-Order Decay Fits for Each Individual Profile. (Right) The Calculated First-Order Decay Rate (K) Is
 Calculated for Each Profile and the Range of Values Summarized in the Boxplot.

To address these drawbacks, the following control schema is proposed (Figure 5-32). In order to
best integrate with the existing decision-making and control strategy, models will be used to
more accurately represent each step. This includes predicting influent (e.g., initial or pre-

64 The Water Research Foundation

disinfection) E. coli, CT given the required log inactivation, and the actual CT being applied
which accounts for the changing decay term k.

 Figure 5-32. Proposed Control Strategy for PAA Dose Based on Influent E. Coli. Individual Models Will Operate
Independently to Predict Influent E. Coli (Model 1), CT for a Required Log Inactivation (Model 2), and Real-Time
 CT (Model 3).

5.3.3 Get Data
A total of 1,236 PAA and E. coli samples were taken throughout the length of the disinfection
basin (Figure 5-33) over 126 sampling events between August 2021 and June 2022, specifically
for this study. Data that is normally collected by the facility for regulatory or process purposes
was also utilized; 12 in-line sensors and analyzers (primarily flows and concentrations measured
in the activated sludge system) and 10 laboratory analytes (primarily daily regulatory
concentrations measured in the final effluent) that measured water quantity and quality up-
and down-stream of the disinfection system. A complete list of variables can be found in Table
5-5. Instrumentation data was aggregated to an hourly time-weighted average when being
exported from Metro’s data management system (OSIsoft PI).

 Figure 5-33. Sampling Locations for PAA And E. Coli Along the Disinfection Basin at RWHTF.

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 65

One additional data source was available for a portion of the PAA and E. coli data collection
effort: a novel E. coli analyzer (Coliminder™) that was being piloted by RWHTF. This data was
included as instrumentation data for a separate model training analysis, due to the substantial
reduction in sample size when subset to just the analyzer’s timeframe (Figure 5-34).

 Figure 5-34. Daily Data Availability by Feature. A Dot Indicates that at Least One Observation Is Available for a

5.3.4 Prepare Data
PAA concentration measurements for each sampling event (one sample at each location taken
within 1-2 minutes) were used to fit an exponential decay curve. Thus, preparation of PAA data
required consideration for how it would affect the exponential curve fit. PAA measurements
that were below detection limit were removed. The alternative approach, of replacing
laboratory measurements below detection limits with either “0” or the detection limit, resulted
in a skewed exponential model fit. Additionally, if the difference between two subsequent PAA
measurements was an increase of 0.3 mg/L, (i.e., PAA concentration immediately downstream
of an upstream concentration measurement increases by more than 0.3 mg/L), the upstream
sample is removed. The upstream sample was selected to be removed after multiple instances
suggested that non-ideal flow conditions (e.g., short circuiting) in the initial sampling locations
lead to non-representative PAA concentrations. Exponential decay curves were fit to the final
subset of PAA concentrations (901 observations) for each sampling event. From this curve and a
calculated hydraulic retention time based on flow, the integrated CT (iCT) was calculated. iCT
representative of the time and active concentration that pathogens are in contact with PAA,
and has a known mechanistic relationship with E. coli log removal.

E. coli data was transformed to log removal based on the E. coli concentration immediately
prior to PAA dosing taken with each sampling event. To perform the log removal calculation, E.
coli concentrations, both before and within the disinfection basin had to be within detection
limits. This filter resulted in a total subset of 1,223 E. coli observations.

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 67

Both laboratory and instrumentation data was interpolated to the latest PAA and E. coli
collection time using the last observation carried forward method, in order to prevent future
information that would not be available during a true forecast from being used for model
training.

Kernel density estimation (KDE) was used to identify outliers for each variable (feature). KDE fits
a probability density curve to the dataset; the flexibility of the curve is determined by its
bandwidth. Bandwidth estimators were initially used, but demonstrated overfitting behavior.
Instead, a bandwidth was selected for each feature that is 1/5th the range of values (similar to
default histogram behavior). This, combined with an alpha value of 0.90, resulted in a two-sided
confidence interval of ‘normal’ values. Values above or below the confidence interval were
considered outliers. This was validated against several known outliers in features such as TSS
and OP.

Finally, E. coli were log10 transformed to normalize the distribution, as it was heavily skewed.
This ensured that during model fitting that the model was not overly sensitive to large values
with correspondingly large errors.

5.3.5 Train Model
5.3.5.1 Modeling approach
Linear regression, adaptive lasso with diurnal model features, and XGBoost were used to
predict iCT, E. coli concentrations, and E. coli removal. Linear regression represented a
simplified baseline condition. Adaptive lasso is a linear regression method with built-in feature
selection, and in this case sine and cosine terms were added as features to represent common
diurnal trends in water and wastewater treatment. This was primarily to test potential time-
dependent behavior that was observed in some water quality sensors. The ML approach that
was explored was XGBoost, a tree-based approach in which each subsequent regression
decision tree fit to the total error (residuals) of the previous trees. As a tree-based approach,
XGBoost has been shown to be effective with (1) laboratory data that may include some
measurement variability or noisy sensor data and (2) significantly different model output
(response) behavior depending on the range of values.

Model training included both random and time-based 5-fold cross validation of 80% of the data
for model training and hyper-parameter tuning, with the remaining 20% for testing (Figure 5-
35). The most common practice of splitting training and testing data involves random sampling.
However, because of the high autocorrelation of the E coli measurements, a time-series
approach was deemed necessary. When a random train/test split was used with XGBoost,
training and testing accuracy exceeded 91%. This was significantly reduced when the time-
series approach was implemented suggesting that data leakage may have occurred during the
random selection. The poor performance of models using the time-based split was also likely
influenced by the small sample size (and lack of process variability) of the initial training
windows. To remedy this, a second train-test split approach was explored.

68 The Water Research Foundation

 Figure 5-35. Example of One CV Iteration of the Train-Test Time-Based Split in Which Only the Newest
 Observations Are Used in Testing.

A cumulative distribution function (CDF)-based method was explored as an alternative train-
test split approach. The goal of using a CDF split was to maximize the sample size used during
training, minimize autocorrelation between samples, and maximize the range of values for an
individual variable. A random sample from a uniform distribution was used to select training
observations from an empirical CDF of all response variable observations; this ensured a wide
range of values in the training set. The same 80%/20% train-test split was maintained.

5.3.5.2 Modeling results
The inclusion of the E. coli analyzer provided sufficient information to overcome any sample
size limitation and perform comparably to the same modeling approach with a larger sample
size (Figure 5-36). However, the E. coli analyzer was not essential to the performance of the
model. Due to the high cost of the system, it was decided to continue deployment without the
additional analyzer. XGBoost using all available features (no feature selection) and a CDF
approach to training and testing was selected for deployment.

 Figure 5-36. Training and Testing Log RMSE For Each Influent E. Coli Model and Dataset Permutation.

The log removal predictions were much less variable (Figure 5-37, left). XGBoost models
performed best and much better than the existing approach to log removal estimations (Figure
5-30), reducing the standard error four-fold. This is a clear indicator that log inactivation is not
solely determined by disinfectant dose, and the majority of the variation from the mechanistic
approach can be explained using available water quality data (Figure 5-37, right). An
examination of the features important to the XGBoost model illustrate that nitrogen species are

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 69

highly-correlated to log removal; suggesting that microbial ecology of the upstream activated
sludge process influences disinfection kinetics.

 Figure 5-37. (Left) Prediction of Log Removal Using an XGBoost Model and CDF Train-test Splits. (Right) SHAP
 Values Illustrate Most Important Predictors in the Log Removal Model.

The iCT model was the best performing model: low errors (testing RMSE of ~2.0 mg/L min) and
these errors are normally distributed throughout the range of observed values (Figure 5-38).
Due to the large training dataset, high correlation to dose, and availability of downstream
analyzer, it is expected that the iCT model would perform well. In previous work, the model
without the online analyzer had twice the RMSE as the model with the online analyzer
(Newhart et al. 2021). However, if that was the case, then the improvement over the existing
control strategy is all the greater.

 Figure 5-38. Prediction of iCT Using an XGBoost Model and CDF Train-test Splits.

70 The Water Research Foundation

5.3.6 Deploy Model
To deploy the model, a new Windows virtual machine was initialized on the Hite network. This
server has read and write access to the data historian. To operationalize the models, the
following additional cases were considered:
• Only some of the data collected during the initial sampling campaign would continue to be
 collected. Specifically, the PAA concentration measurements in the disinfection basin would
 not be continued. The pre-disinfection and post-disinfection E. coli would continue to be
 measured once per day as regulatory values. Therefore, the E. coli model would require
 regular updating, while the CT model would not be regularly updated.
• Updating would occur every two weeks, or when model inputs change.
• In the event that a variable does not have a new observation in the last 7 days, the model
 would be updated and exclude that variable in re-training.
The deployment stage requires significant time if a pipeline has not been previously established
to connect with the data historian previously. Since this was the first case study for Metro to
connect to the historian and execute the retraining and backfilling functions, this consumed the
majority of the time in the project.

“Deployment” for this Application was the automation of the predictions as a digital shadow—
observed but not actively engaging with the control strategy. Due to the data quality issues and
model changes described above, the model and errors are still under observation. Once
deployed, performance for the influent E. coli differed significantly from the simulation study
previously performed. The pre-disinfection E. coli model was unable to anticipate high
concentration events (Figure 5-39). This failure, while representing a small number of days
relative to all predictions, was a requirement for operations to take up the model as a new
component of their control strategy. Without the ability to predict high concentration events,
the complexity of the model is not worth the uncertainty. However, the diurnal trend observed
in the prediction is of significant interest to Metro and has inspired a 24-hour E. coli sampling
campaign to validate this data artifact.

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 71

Figure 5-39. Pre-disinfection E. coli as a Function of Time During the Digital Shadow Period. The Predicted Values
are Made by the XGBoost Model. The Actual Values are Measured in the Lab. The Persistence Values are a 3-day
 Rolling Average, Shifted by 24 hours to Simulate the Availability of Laboratory Data.

The low pre-disinfection E. coli model accuracy could be due to missing process variables which
are responsible or are correlated to these large E. coli events, or due to the limited range of
values in the intensive PAA sampling period. To test the later hypothesis, the influent E. coli
model was therefore modified to include all historical influent E. coli data for 3 years (Figure 5-
40).

Figure 5-40. Density of Pre-disinfection E. coli Measurements During the Initial Data Collection Period (Blue) and
 All Available Historical Data (Red).

The model inputs also required the addition of more historical data, as during deployment the
secondary process and final effluent quality began to move outside of the ranges originally
observed during the sampling period (Figure 5-41).

72 The Water Research Foundation

  Figure 5-41. Density of Various Pre-disinfection E. coli Model Inputs During the Initial Data Collection Period
 (blue) and all Available Historical Data (red).

Utilizing 3 years of historical data posed an additional problem as the data historian would
“time out” with such a large data request each time the model downloaded data into Python,
as some instruments observations are retained at an extremely high frequency. Moving
forward, data storage for high frequency variables would be more carefully considered, but the
past data collection could not be changed. Thus, some model inputs were dropped; this in
addition to those dropped due to inconsistent data collection (i.e., Lab Effluent TKN, TN, TIN).
With fewer variables and a larger data space, the XGBoost model did not perform better than
the existing control strategy of using a lagged 3-day rolling average (Figure 5-42).

  Figure 5-42. Deployed Pre-disinfection E. coli Concentrations Compared to the Existing Estimation Strategy.

Based on the observed log removal, the actual CT applied can be approximated by using a
double exponential mechanistic model (Figure 5-43). This mechanistic model was fit using
actual CT profiles and log removal values from the training data. XGBoost predictions of CT
were found to be slightly more accurate than the current CT approximation overall (24.7 vs 27.4
mean absolute error, respectively), but when outliers are weighted more heavily the

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 73

improvement diminished (33.7 vs 33.5 root mean squared error, respectively). This
investigation has uncovered that the current approach to estimating CT (constant decay) is
inaccurate. Despite operations slowly increasing their CT setpoint during the study period, post
disinfection E. coli also steadily increased; supporting the finding that the current CT estimation
has no relationship to post-disinfection E. coli and thereby E. coli removal. The XGBoost model
prediction may be a more accurate representation of the PAA disinfection kinetics and
integrated CT; however, adopting the XGBoost model would require an entirely new
operational framework as there is a significant difference in how the CT is estimated and used.
The operational, control, and sampling requirements to successfully implement this shift are
not currently worth the small estimated improvement observed in real-time (10%). However,
these findings will inform future disinfection upgrades as more information is collected on how
to best represent and control PAA dose.

  Figure 5-43. Measured Log Removal (LR) as a Function of CT. A Double Exponential Mechanistic Model Was Fit
 from the Original Data Collection Campaign (Red). Metro Assumes a Constant K and Maintains a CT Setpoint by
Adjusting Dose (Light blue). The XGboost Predictions (Dark blue) Utilize Online and Offline Water Quality Data to
 Predict Real-Time CT.

The combination of the CT and pre-disinfection E. coli results illustrate that there are water
quality changes that are not currently being captured in either model which directly affect PAA
disinfection kinetics. Additional features are required that represent water quality or
operational changes that result in these high pre-disinfection E. coli events.

74 The Water Research Foundation

5.3.7 Lessons Learned
• Due to the limited nature of these environmental laboratory datasets relative to sensor-
 only datasets (e.g., hundreds of observations in environmental datasets vs millions of
 observations in conventional ML applications), overfitting may be unavoidable using ML
 models. Even a small amount of overfitting in simulation led to a worse outcome than
 expected during deployment. Therefore, the estimated improvement in simulation should
 be significant in order to account for some amount of performance degradation during
 deployment when any amount of overfitting is observed.
• When creating a model in simulation, all historical data is available for model building.
 When creating a model for deployment, the most recent data may not include the same
 variables or observations as in simulation. This can occur for a variety of reasons including
 the sampling point being moved, a delay in validating measurements to be uploaded to the
 data historian, or a change in process that fundamentally shifts variables higher or lower
 than previously observed. These items directly impact model performance and should be
 evaluated at the beginning of modeling building as opposed to waiting until the deployment
 phase of the project.
• For high-frequency or autocorrelated data, it is important to utilize a time-based approach
 to model training as randomized training approaches may fit to the highly correlated
 observations immediately before or after a testing observation. This is a data leakage
 problem that leads to overfitting and artificially low testing errors. Oftentimes when this
 type of overfit model is deployed, the model error is significantly worse than the status quo
 approach.
• Breaking down a large or complex control problem into individual decision-making
 components allows for separate predictive models to be built for each step. This improves
 interpretability and likelihood of overall success, as opposed to attempting to directly
 predict the control variable.
• Model retraining is triggered by multiple cases: if sufficient time has passed since the last
 training or if data availability changes. In this case, 2 weeks was considered sufficient time
 for new operational conditions to be incorporated. Data availability was found to be a
 challenge during deployment where individual analytes were intermittently available (e.g.,
 delay in sample validation).

5.4 P-Recovery: MagPrex® (Application 4)
5.4.1 Background (Set the Stage)
Metro Water Recovery’s (Metro’s) Robert W. Hite Treatment Facility (RWHTF) located in
Denver, CO, serves a population equivalent of 2.2 million people and is rated for 220 MGD. The
facility has two secondary processeswith both utilizing an enhanced biological phosphorus
removal (EBPR) process to remove phosphorus present in their main treatment stream. During
subsequent anaerobic digestion (AD) of the sludge, a substantial portion of the biomass-bound
phosphorus is re-solubilized and released into the liquid stream. This high concentration of
released phosphorus (>400 mg/L-P) combined with the high concentrations of ammonia (>1000
mg/L-N) and sufficient magnesium levels lead to inadvertent struvite precipitation. Struvite
(NH MgPO •6H O), also known as MAP, is a crystalline solid that forms in the presence of
 4 4 2

magnesium, ammonium, and phosphate. Unwanted struvite precipitation scales critical

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 75

infrastructure and results in operational shutdowns, reduced digestor capacity, and increased
maintenance costs (e.g., see Figure 5-44). Furthermore, the remaining soluble phosphorus is
recirculated back into the secondary treatment process. This recirculation forms a loop, where
phosphorus continually re-enters the system until the EBPR process is overwhelmed.
Consequently, high concentrations of phosphorus are discharged into the final effluent, posing
a risk of permit violations.

 Figure 5-44. MagPrex® Images of Struvite Scaling on Pipes at Metro’s Facility.

To address these problems and ensure compliance with effluent discharge limits, Metro
installed North America’s largest MagPrex® system in 2020 designed by Centrisys/CNP
(Kenosha, WI, USA). The phosphorous recovery system expediates the chemical precipitation of
struvite by adding the limiting reagent magnesium as magnesium chloride (MgCl). Digester
effluent with a high phosphorus concentration enters the air upflow reactor continuously where
soluble phosphorus is then converted to a particulate state with an average particle size <100
µm. Particles >400 µm settle to the bottom of the reactor where they are captured and
beneficially reused in compost. The MagPrex® effluent is then sent to a dewatering process
where >90% of the phosphorus is retained in the biosolids and eventually land applied at
Metro’s farm, Metro Grow. Figure 5-45 shows a diagram of the MagPrex® system installed at
Metro.

76 The Water Research Foundation

 Figure 5-45. Diagram of the MagPrex® System Installed at Metro. Key Control Variables are Boxed with Dashed
 Lines. X’s indicate sample locations for laboratory measurements.

5.4.2 Define the Problem
Currently, Metro is adjusting the reactor’s MgCl dosage based on a 1.3:1 Mg-P molar ratio with
weekly orthophosphate (OP) measurements from the AD effluent. This extended time between
measurements results in missed opportunities to adjust the dosing set point to account for
dynamic changes in OP concentrations. While it might seem straightforward to simply overdose
the required MgCl to address these challenges, Metro’s current practice, this approach has two
significant drawbacks. Overdosing not only increases chemical costs and necessitates more
frequent deliveries but also leads to residual magnesium that can precipitate as struvite
downstream in the centrate system. This creates maintenance challenges for crews, introducing
additional operational burdens. There is no commercially available OP sensor that measures in
the complex matrix of the AD effluent due to the high solids concentration >1% and OP
concentration >300 mg/L-P. Furthermore, increasing laboratory measurement frequency is not
currently operationally feasible for the facility. To address these limitations, an investigation
into leveraging a variety of statistical and ML methods is therefore undertaken to provide daily
predictions for the influent OP into the Magprex® reactor, the effluent OP out of the reactor,
and OP removal occurring within the reactor. While Metro currently uses only influent OP
measurements to determine setpoint adjustments, the additional effluent OP and OP removal
predictions are tested to see if they can better determine MgCl setpoint (MgCl SP)
recommendations that lead to Metro’s ideal reactor effluent OP concentration of 30 mg P/L.

5.4.3 Get Data
Over 15 months, Metro collected a dataset spanning 95 different variables including a
combination of offline laboratory (e.g., Digester Effluent Mg and OP concentrations) and online
sensor measurements (e.g., Centrate pH and MgCl Discharge Flow Rate). All measurements are
pulled from Metro’s internal data historian system and into separate Microsoft Excel
(Microsoft, Redmond, WA) files with the online data exported as time-weighted, daily averages.

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 77

Using Python (v3.11.5), these Excel files are merged and matched up by timestamp. OP removal
values which equal the difference between the influent OP and the effluent OP concentrations
are added to the dataset as an additional variable.

5.4.4 Prepare Data
Days where the system was offline or lacked data on reactor influent OP and effluent OP
concentrations are removed from the dataset. Additional variables are removed from the
dataset that have a vast majority of their observations missing or constant.

Through exploratory data analysis and consultation with Metro’s process engineers, known
outliers in the dataset are manually identified. Additional automated outlier identification is
done through the use of kernel density estimation (KDE) where probability density curves are fit
to each individual variable’s data. The bandwidth and significance level of the KDE is iteratively
adjusted until the known outlier points are identified. As a result, an observation from each
variable is identified as an outlier when it has a log probability less than the 10th percentile.

If a variable’s observation was missing for a specific day or was identified as an outlier,
imputation is performed to fill in the unavailable data. The last-observation-carried-forward
technique, which assumes data on days with missing observations is equal to the data of the
last measured observation, is used first. For any remaining missing observations, data is
backfilled with the first recorded measurement. Once imputation is finished, a final dataset of
86 variables and 158 observations is obtained.

5.4.5 Train Model
Six modeling types are evaluated on predicting three different objective variable
concentrations: influent OP into the reactor, effluent OP out of the reactor, and the OP removal
within the reactor. To prevent data leakage by providing extra information that could cause
over-optimistic predictions, the objective variables and other OP-related data are dropped from
the independent variable set. Four different types of statistical regression models, including
ordinary linear regression (LR), partial least squares (PLS), ridge, and lasso regression, are
evaluated first. XGBoost regression is then also evaluated to determine whether more
computationally complex ML models provide any additional predictive value. A baseline model
utilizing the mean values of the influent OP, effluent OP, and OP removal data is used as a point
of comparison.

A repeated k-fold cross validation with five folds and 3 repeats is used to split up the processed
dataset and help determine each model’s ability to predict a variety of unseen data. The RMSE
between the actual measured data and the predicted data from each of the cross-validation
splits is calculated and averaged to provide a score for all models.

Given the low S/F ratio, a variety of variable selection methods are investigated to reduce
model complexity and filter noise. A comparison between four different techniques including
Spearman’s correlation (ρ), principal component analysis (PCA), maximum Relevance-Minimum
Redundancy (mRMR), and XGBoost variable importance is undertaken. ρ is a nonparametric
variation measuring the strength and direction of monotonic relationships. The correlations of
all variables to the objective variables are found using ρ and the lowest correlated variables are

78 The Water Research Foundation

removed. PCA involves creating linear combinations of existing variables to sequentially capture
the most variation in the data. Across these linear combinations (i.e., principal components),
those variables with the lowest contributions to the variation are removed. For mRMR, a
ranking based on F-scores and correlation coefficients is created and the highest scoring
variables are kept. For the XGBoost variable importance method, XGBoost models are
developed for all objective variables. Like other decision tree models, XGBoost quantifies the
relative importance of each independent variable for its predictions. Those variables with the
highest importance values as identified by the models are kept.

To identify the best performing variable selection method and ideal number of variables to use,
the six modeling approaches are trained and tested on different numbers of variables. Training
occurs on the first five variables selected and then is repeated for five additional variables until
all are selected, this process is repeated for each of the four methods outlined.

The left-hand side of Figure 5-46 shows variable selection results for each modeling objective.
For the influent OP predictions, selecting 60 variables with the XGBoost variable importance
method and using the ridge regression model results in a minimum RMSE of 19.8 mg-P/L
representing a 39.6% improvement over the mean model. For the effluent OP predictions,
selecting 45 variables with ρ and using the XGBoost model results in a minimum RMSE of 10.4
mg-P/L representing a 60.1% improvement over the mean model. For the OP removal
predictions, selecting 65 variables with the XGBoost variable importance method and using the
lasso regression model results in a minimum RMSE of 22.0 mg-P/L representing a 36.1%
improvement over the mean model.

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 79

Figure 5-46. a) (Left) Plot of RMSE Values of Influent OP Predictions vs. the Number of Variables Selected Using
 the XGBoost Variable Importance Method, (Right) Plot of the Best Performing Model’s Predicted Influent OP
Values vs. the Actual Influent OP Values. b) (Left) Plot of RMSE Values of Effluent OP Predictions vs. the Number
 of Variables Selected Using Ρ, (Right) Plot of the Best Performing Model’s Predicted Effluent OP Values vs. the
Actual Effluent OP Values. c) (Left) Plot of RMSE Values of OP Removal Predictions vs. the Number of Variables
Selected Using the XGboost Variable Importance Method, (Right) Plot of the Best Performing Model’s Predicted
 OP Removal Values vs. the Actual OP Removal Values.

These improvements over the mean justify the use of data-driven modeling for predicting the
objective variables and indicate Metro can reduce the number of monitored variables without
impacting accuracy. Across the variable selection methods and when considering all modeling
types except LR, there is little difference in RMSE values after the selection of 20 or more
variables. This suggests the type of variable selection technique used is most important when
selecting a relatively small number of variables to keep. As shown in Table 5-6, the mRMR
method results in the lowest average RMSE values across all model types when considering the
first 5-15 variables selected. Table 5-7 shows the top 15 variables selected for influent OP and
effluent OP models using the mRMR technique.
