In terms of model types, regardless of modeling objective and variable selection technique
used, the XGBoost model consistently has the lowest RMSE values when under 30 variables are
selected. Interestingly though, the statistical ridge and lasso regression models had similar or
better RMSE values than the XGBoost model for the Influent OP and OP removal predictions
after 30 variables were selected. It should be noted that all the models perform worse at high
OP concentrations as illustrated through the increased distance between the predicted and
actual values shown on the right-hand side of Figure 5-46.

During model development and training, Metro opened a new solids processing facility causing
some variable’s monitoring locations to change and some to stop being monitored entirely. To
adjust the models for the eventual deployment, new datasets were resampled to include data
up until May 2024. Following the same procedures, the new data was processed and tested
with the mRMR variable selection method and the ridge, lasso, and XGBoost models. For all
three modeling objectives, XGBoost results in the lowest RMSE values of 24.3 mg-P/L, 10.6 mg-
P/L, and 24.9 mg-P/L for the influent OP, effluent OP, and OP removal predictions respectively.

MgCl SP predictions can be obtained using the influent OP predictions and applying Metro’s
typical 1.3:1 Mg-P molar ratio setpoint. However, considering the model’s accuracy and
dynamic reactor and water quality conditions, this may not result in Metro’s ideal effluent OP

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 81

concentration of 30 mg-P/L. To improve upon this, the effluent OP predictions are leveraged to
help identify the system’s distance from ideal conditions. This distance from the ideal is then
multiplied by the 1.3:1 molar ratio to determine an MgCl SP adjustment recommendation.
Figure 5-47 shows results of the MgCl SP adjustment recommendations from effluent OP
predictions using the new dataset through May 2024. The predictions successfully recommend
an adjustment that corrected for an over- or underdose 88% of the time. This means that when
an overdose happens (i.e., the OP concentration is lower than 30 mg-P/L) a lower MgCl SP is
recommended and vice versa for an underdose. Additionally, as the effluent OP gets further
away from the ideal, the magnitude of the recommended MgCl SP adjustment generally
increases as well.

Figure 5-47. MgCl Recommendation Results from the Effluent OP Model Predictions Using 45 Variables Selected
 through mRMR, the XGBoost model, and with Data Up Until May 2024. Correct regions refer to
 recommendations that correctly identify and account for a day where MgCl was over- or underdosed.

The potential chemical and cost savings from these recommendation results are rather modest
with MgCl consumption being theoretically reduced by 100 gal/d or 5% when considering data
from May 2023-May 2024. This is likely due to the limitations of using the 1.3:1 Mg-P molar
ratio to calculate the MgCl SP recommendations. The potential savings in this case are limited
by an assumed linear relationship between effluent OP and the MgCl SP. In practice, it is likely
this relationship is more dynamic and non-linear depending on reactor and water quality
conditions. To maximize potential savings, future work will need to be done to tease out this
dose response relationship more thoroughly. Additionally, avoided costs related to decreased
maintenance and downtime could be factored into future savings considerations.

5.4.6 Deploy Model
Deployment involves operationalizing the model code and integrating it with Metro’s internal
data historian system. Python scripts were created to pull new data every day from the
historian, integrate it with the historical raw data, and process it following the same procedures

82 The Water Research Foundation

used during development and training. Code for predicting the three modeling objectives using
XGBoost and 45 variables previously identified using mRMR was also created. While the use of
45 variables with only 288 days worth of sample data results in an S/F ratio less than 10, model
training results indicate little impact to model error and including slightly more variables can
help overcome issues due to the infrequency of laboratory measurements. To sufficiently
capture changes in data while conserving computing power, it was determined that model
retraining on any new data would occur every two weeks. Prediction outputs, including MgCl SP
recommendations, are set up to publish results back to the historian and be shown to Metro
engineers and operators.
Deployed predictions were analyzed from April 2024 - December 2024. Out of the 222 days of
monitored deployment predictions were only available for 180 days due to a variety of code or
server errors that occurred in the early stages of deployment. Predictions were compared
against measured laboratory values which were available for 86 of the monitored days. As
shown in the left side of Figure 5-48, predictions for influent OP, effluent OP, and OP removal
generally followed the rising and lowering trends of the actual measured data. Despite the
models’ ability to predict relative trends in concentration changes over time, the overall
performance, as measured by RMSE, of the deployed influent OP and OP removal models was
worse than during model training and development. This difference is likely due to the high OP
concentrations that occurred during this period. As was known during model training, the
models perform worse at the extremes of the concentration spectrum due to these events not
being well represented in the training data. Predictions of influent OP and OP removal were
found to have similar levels of accuracy to the originally trained models when concentrations
were below 450 and 400 mg P/L respectively. The additional sampling demand at a wider range
of operating conditions illustrates that data collection for ML methods which utilize laboratory
data may require multiple years to fully capture the range of influent and operational
conditions. The deployed effluent OP predictions were found to be as accurate as the
previously trained models but had similar issues with inaccurate predictions at the extremes.

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 83

  Figure 5-48. a) (Left) Timeseries Plot of Influent OP Predictions and Actual Measurements (Right) Predicted
 Influent OP vs. Actual Influent OP values. b) (Left) Timeseries Plot of Effluent OP Predictions and Actual
 Measurements (Right) Predicted Effluent OP vs. Actual Effluent OP Values. c) (Left) Timeseries Plot of OP
 Removal Predictions and Actual Measurements (Right) Predicted OP Removal vs. Actual OP Removal Values.

The deployment platform for the digital shadow (a virtual machine on the Metro internal
network, extensive python code) was found to be inconsistent. There were times when the
server would shutdown (e.g., CrowdStrike outage) or the python code would not successfully
run (e.g., errors would occur after ingesting uncommon or mislabeled data or confronting
issues not anticipated during training). This project was the first attempt to use existing internal
Metro systems for real-time, data-driven modeling. After identifying these weaknesses, Metro
has recognized the need for a cloud-based data-driven workflow. No previous use cases had
overcome cybersecurity concerns, but as this project has demonstrated, deployment of real-
time data driven models that utilize laboratory data will require a stable, cloud-based solution.

84 The Water Research Foundation

5.4.7 Lessons Learned
• Dynamic Data Environment: Infrastructure upgrades, laboratory availability/capacity, and
 sensor maintenance significantly impact data collection patterns. The MagPrex®
 implementation demonstrated that data sources, locations, and collection frequencies can
 change substantially during deployment, as evidenced by the opening of Metro's new solids
 processing facility. Regular model retraining and performance re-evaluation proved
 essential to maintain prediction accuracy in this dynamic environment.
• Cybersecurity and Access Challenges: Utilities' cybersecurity protocols can significantly
 impact project timelines when external consultants require access to data systems. At
 Metro, these restrictions affected model development, deployment, and evaluation
 schedules. Organizations should either establish secure data access protocols for external
 partners early in the project or ensure internal staff are trained to support modeling
 activities. This is particularly crucial for systems like the MagPrex® where real-time data
 access is vital for optimization.
• Model Accuracy Expectations: Data-driven models, while powerful, have inherent
 limitations in prediction accuracy. For the MagPrex® system, model performance varied
 across different OP concentration ranges, with higher concentrations showing increased
 prediction error. Utilities must define acceptable accuracy thresholds based on operational
 requirements and risk tolerance. In Metro's case, the models achieved 88% accuracy in
 recommending correct MgCl dosing adjustments, demonstrating that even imperfect
 predictions can provide operational value.
• Cost-Benefit Considerations: While the initial chemical savings were modest, the true value
 of automated prediction systems extends beyond direct chemical costs. Future
 implementations should consider broader benefits including reduced maintenance
 requirements, decreased downtime from struvite scaling, and improved operational
 efficiency when evaluating system performance.

5.5 Holistic Biosolids Optimization (Application 5)
5.5.1 Background (Set the Stage)
The DC Water Blue Plains Advanced Wastewater Treatment Plant (AWTP) contains a side
stream process that takes the solids from the mainstream processes and converts them to
biogas and a biosolids product to be used as a fertilizer. Figure 5-49 shows a diagram of DC
Water Blue Plains AWTP side stream process to create a Class A biosolids product. This process
requires the accurate setting of dilution water at multiple locations including the pulper
feedline (pre-dewatering management or before thermal hydrolysis process (THP) feed
targeted 16.5% TS), digester feedline (10.5% TS), and final dewatering feedline (3.5% TS).
Especially, the final dewatering process needs polymer dosage to maintain the correct total
solids concentration when entering the belt filter press, preventing polymer overdosing for
energy reduction in polymer manufacturing and polymer underdosing resulting in machine
failures that require significant operator cleaning time.

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 85

 Figure 5-49. DC Water Blue Plains AWTP Diagram of Side Stream Process to Create a Class A Biosolids Product.

5.5.2 Define the Problem
To optimize this side stream process in the DC Water Plant, the measurement of total solids is
the most essential piece of information that is necessary to identify dilution water and polymer
dosage and is typically conducted using time-intensive laboratory processes. Using a non-
contact acoustic soft sensor, we are able to detect the total solids concentration within a pipe
without requiring operator sensor maintenance using ML. Other total solids sensors are placed
on the inside of the pipes and experience fouling very quickly. ML is necessary to achieve this
total solids estimation because the acoustic non-contact sensors do not provide a direct output
of total solids that may be calculated but are known to be correlated with total solids.

5.5.3 Get Data
Acoustic measurements, flow measurements, and total solids laboratory samples were taken
from the digester in the treatment plant. The acoustic measurements are recorded using a
passive acoustic sensor (type 4534-B-001 of the Brüel & Kjær company) glued using industrial
adhesive directly to a pipe in the full-scale system (see Figure 5-50). The flow measurements are
already a plant data stream recorded with a sensor. Total solids samples were taken weekly
under normal operating conditions at ~9 am from September 2021 to May 2023. Samples were
then taken at the digester under operator induced experimental conditions over the summer of
2023 where the range of total solids values was pushed to the edge of safe conditions over a
period of ~6 hours.

86 The Water Research Foundation

 Figure 5-50. 4534-B-001 Brüel & Kjær Company Acoustic Sensor Glued to a Pipe in the Blue Plains Facility.

After collecting all of the total solids data to be used to train and test the future model, the
histogram of the data must be plotted to ensure that there is a sufficient range and distribution
of samples. Under normal operating conditions, the range of total solids measurements was 7-
12% total solids. This range excluded any extreme conditions occurring at the digester.

ML algorithms are known to perform poorly outside of the range of their training data meaning
the final model would not be able to accurately interpret observed extreme conditions during
deployment if trained exclusively on normal conditions. The digester is also a controlled
process, meaning normal operating conditions exist on a very small range and the standard
deviation of the distribution of samples is close to the goal error of the model and the error in
the laboratory measures. This means a model is likely to just predict the mean of the data, not
an underlying pattern. Since the goal of many ML models is the predictions of aberrations in
underlying conditions, the training data should include these conditions as well.

An experiment was conducted to combat this issue by increasing the range of total solids
measurements observed and ensuring there was an even distribution of normal operating
condition measurements and extreme measurements. Essentially, the project team ran the
process across a wide range of conditions to ensure that the training data would reflect the
types of conditions the team would also like to predict.

This may not be feasible for many other applications, since taking other processes outside of
their control regime may lead to unintended consequences. If one intends to use ML for a
project, make sure the data are “interesting.” In other words, if the parameter is tightly
controlled, you may not have enough data to predict anything outside of that range.

The final dataset of total solids and flow measurements are plotted in Python before
proceeding with data processing to ensure the new dataset from the operator experiments
meets the criteria of a greater standard deviation than goal model error and error in laboratory
measurements. Figure 5-51 shows histograms of data measured during the experiment's total
solids, flow, and smoothed flow.

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 87

Figure 5-51. Histograms of Data Measured During the Experiment's Total Solids, Flow, and Smoothed Flow (Left
 to Right).

5.5.4 Prepare Data
The acoustic data is recorded in its raw form as a sound wave for 0.45s. To reduce the number
of features, the samples are projected into the frequency domain by taking the fast Fourier
transform that results in a dataset containing the amplitude for 5946 frequencies from 12 Hz to
13,100Hz.

Three data streams are included in the collection of the data, and thus these streams must be
merged into one dataset. The acoustic data are recorded in triplicate every 5 minutes during the
experimental measurements, the flow is always recorded every minute, and total solids values
are measured every 10 minutes, 2.5 minutes after the most recent acoustic measurement
during the experimental period.

To merge the total solids values with the acoustic measurements, the sample measurement
time for the acoustic measurement is compared with the set of total solids measurement times,
and if there is a total solids measurement within 5 minutes, the total solids measurement is
linked to that acoustic measurement. Based on the timing of this experiment, there are two
acoustic measurements linked to each total solids measurement because the acoustic
measurements are equidistant to the total solids measurement time.

The triplicates of the acoustic measurements will be averaged into one sample per
measurement time. This averaging of triplicates occurs after the merging of the acoustic
measurements and total solids measurements to reduce computation time. The triplicates may
also be used for some outlier removal, but in this limited dataset, there were no dramatic
outliers therefore none were removed.

The reduced dataset as processed above is then merged with the flow data that is collected
every 1 minute at the digester. Each acoustic/total solids sample is assigned the closest flow
measurement in time within a range of 30 seconds. A moving average has been conducted on
the raw flow dataset and merged with the acoustic/total solids dataset as well to remove some
noise in the flow measurement.

88 The Water Research Foundation

After merging the entire dataset into this final form containing acoustic fast Fourier transform
profiles, total solids laboratory measurements, raw flow data, and smoothed flow data, the
acoustic data is smoothed to remove noise. This smoothing process is conducted last to limit
the number of samples that must be smoothed, therefore reducing computational time. The
smoothing is conducted using a moving average on the fast Fourier transform with a width of
46Hz to best remove noise without removing local minimums and maximums of interest. Figure
5-52 shows the fast Fourier transform of acoustic signals measured at the digester before and
after moving average smoothing.

 Figure 5-52. Fast Fourier Transform of Acoustic Signals Measured at the Digester Shown Before (Left) and After
 (Right) Moving Average Smoothing.

5.5.5 Train Model
Further feature reduction is required as a part of this model because the number of features,
5946, is much greater than the number of samples. The method of feature reduction that was
determined to be the most effective for this Application is principal component analysis. This
method along with other feature reduction techniques is used to remove the portions of a
measurement that are either irrelevant or redundant. In doing so, one is left with only the most
essential pieces of information and as little noise as possible. This results in a model that
predicts the testing data points with a similar level of accuracy to the training data points
meaning the model is effectively describing the data.

5.5.5.6 Establishing a baseline
An average model is created as a comparison point for the final root mean squared error to be
able to evaluate if the advanced models perform better than guessing the mean of the training
dataset (see Figure 5-53). The root mean squared error of this model is 2.73. A root mean
squared error for the average model that is similar to the goal final root mean squared error is a
good indicator that the dataset used has too little variability to be an effective model.

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 89

 Figure 5-53. The Predicted Versus Measured Testing and Training Data When Trained on a Model Always
 Predicting the Average of the Training Data.

Before moving on to advanced ML algorithms, it is important to exhaust the simplest models'
performance. With principal component analysis conducted, linear regression performs well
with a root mean squared error of 1.30 and visually is not overfitting the data (see Figure 5-54).
This is a reasonable model and the benefit of linear regression is it is able to be extrapolated
outside of the training dataset range. However, cross-validation remains to be conducted to
compare it to advanced ML algorithms.

 Figure 5-54. The Predicted Versus Measured Testing and Training Data When Trained on a Linear Regression
 Model.

5.5.5.7 Increasing model complexity
Multiple ML algorithms were compared to identify the best performing algorithm, XGBoost,
including partial least squares, gradient boosted regression, light gradient boosted regression,
and random forest. By comparing many algorithms, widths of moving average smoothing, and
number of principal components with cross-validation, XGBoost was identified to have the
lowest root mean squared error of 1.63 with a small standard deviation when combined with a
moving average smoothing algorithm and using 20 principal components (see Figure 5-55).

90 The Water Research Foundation

 Figure 5-55. The Predicted Versus Measured Testing and Training Data When Trained on an Extreme Gradient
 Boosted Regression Model.

Cross-validation is an essential test of model performance as it limits the effect of the random
choice of training-testing splitting of the data. 50 iterations of cross-validation were conducted
here where in each iteration the data was re-split into training and testing sets and the models
were retrained. The resulting bar graph (Figure 5-56) shows that the XGBoost model reduces
the overall root mean squared error by about 1% total solids and has a much smaller standard
deviation of root mean squared error than linear regression does. These results show that
XGBoost is a better choice than linear regression despite the increase in complexity of the
model and the inability to extrapolate effectively.

 Figure 5-56. A Bar Graph of the Mean Root Mean Squared Error and Standard Deviation of the Average, Linear
 Regression, and Extreme Gradient Boosted Regression Models After Cross Validation.

Finally, a last check of the XGBoost model’s performance is a graph of all of the testing data sets
over the 50 iterations of cross validation (Figure 5-57). By only graphing the testing data points
that were predicted using the 50 differently split and trained models, we are visually able to
verify that the model is predicting the extremes of the data as well as the center of the dataset.
This is in stark contrast to the average model as plotted above. Since this is the case, we are
able to be confident in the root mean squared error of the final model as an accurate
description of the error in the model.

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 91

Figure 5-57. The Predicted Value Compared to the Measured Value of Each Testing Point from Each Iteration of
 Cross Validation.

5.5.6 Deploy Model
The model, developed on digester 3, was validated at digester 1 and 3 feedline location. This is
a go decision to deploy the model to predict online TS of digester feedline for dilution decision
making. The machine learning model must be deployed safely and securely, meaning
maintaining secure Wi-Fi networks and communicating with operators to have a clear
understanding of the use cases of these tools. To achieve this goal, an internal Wi-Fi network
was constructed that only the acoustic sensors are placed on and these sensors report to one
computer housed within the plant. This computer then processes the data using the techniques
contained in the data preparation section and runs it through the model chosen in the model
training section. The computer is able to post data to the operator dashboard without having a
return pathway meaning there is no security risk imposed. The data is also posted to a PowerBI
dashboard for process engineers to monitor.

After deployment, the model may be retrained if samples continue to be taken and the model
begins to perform at a lower quality level. The total solids behavior often changes on the multi-
month scale, so model retraining may be discerned by humans and done infrequently. This
retraining also means that operators can have confidence in the model while in the model’s
operating range because each model has been analyzed by an engineer before redeployment.

5.5.7 Lessons Learned
• It is crucial to evaluate the true value of the data-driven model (e.g., predicting the mean or
 comparing it with the baseline scenario depending on specific case scenarios). In the
 preliminary model development, it was clear that the models developed were not better
 than guessing the mean TS value referenced as the average model for pulper feedline (or
 THP feedline), digester feedline, and final dewatering feedline. This validation towards a
 control model is often neglected in other studies, but it is important to evaluate the true
 value of developed data-driven tools. The main reason for the “good” performance of
 “guessing the mean” model was that the full-scale system was a controlled system and did
 not allow for much TS variability to take place in the system. We identified this as the main
 challenge of developing this TS prediction model in our case study, but also as a general
 challenge for other data-driven tools developed based on full-scale systems.

92 The Water Research Foundation

• To address the challenges of full-scale operational boundaries, it is essential to ensure
 sufficient data variability to capture extreme conditions for data-driven model
 development. In our case study, we conducted an experimental testing campaign to expand
 the variability of the training dataset for the digester feedline after the lesson learned from
 preliminary model development. Specifically, the TS range was increased from 9–11% under
 normal operating conditions to 5–14% to include extreme conditions. As a result, we
 successfully developed a robust TS prediction model for the digester feedline. The model
 provided a confirmation of using an acoustic sensor to predict online digested TS % and
 provide input to dilution decision making.
• Creating TS variability in the full-scale system is not easy and was only feasible during short
 periods to avoid significant impacts on process outcomes and compliance. In our case study,
 we were not able to conduct the testing campaign to expand the TS variability in the pulper
 feed line and the final dewatering feedline. Note that, we intend to use the model to set
 dilution water upstream in the pulper feedline (pre-dewatering management or before the
 THP feedline) and the final dewatering feedline.
• Overall, a theoretical approach for LCA is worth considering in this study for the potential
 benefits of reducing labor for TS measurement at the pulper feedline and achieving more
 stable final dewatering performance as well as saving polymer dose demand. However, with
 the online TS measurement now available at the digester, DC Water can explore how to
 leverage it for calculating TS at the final dewatering stage or upstream at the pulper
 feedline.
• Assessing the global applicability of the data-driven ML Toolkit is crucial to transfer the ML
 Toolkit development to other full-scale implementations.

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 93

CHAPTER 6
Life-Cycle Assessment and Techno-Economic Analysis
To better quantify the benefits realized through the Applications in this project, both a life cycle
assessment (LCA) and a techno-economic analysis (TEA) were conducted. LCA and TEA
methodologies are important methods for comparison because they provide standardized
metrics with which to compare different scenarios. LCA describes relevant sustainability metrics
while TEA describes an economic metric, both spanning over the whole life cycle of a product or
process. Multiple impact categories in the LCA were investigated including global warming
potential, cumulative energy demand, marine and freshwater eutrophication, as well as water
deprivation. These impacts were calculated using ReCiPe 2016, Cumulative Energy Demand,
and AWARE water footprint calculation methods. All these impact categories as well as the
CAPEX and OPEX costs of the TEA were levelized per m3 of treated water to provide a standard
basis between Applications.

The LCA and TEA were updated throughout the project based on a combination of historical
data, data collected during this project, as well as inputs from the Ecoinvent and USLCI
databases. This section details the major highlights of the LCA/TEA for each Application as well
as a summary of the benefits realized from implementation of each Application compared to a
relevant conventional baseline scenario. A more detailed analysis for each Application can be
found in Appendix A.

6.1 Methodologies
An attributional system model was implemented for this life cycle analysis. In an attributional
system model, the environmental impacts that can be attributed to a process are summed to
provide an overall impact score. To calculate this score, the inputs and outputs and
emissions/wastes were determined for each system process flow for a specified functional unit.
The functional unit in this case was 1 m3 of treated wastewater for each relevant system. These
impacts were summed based on physical flows within the system (i.e., chemical dosage or
electricity use in operation).

The ReCiPe 2016 method was chosen as the main calculation method for its broad range of
indicators and its acceptance as one of the best life cycle impact assessment (LCIA) methods.
This method calculates impacts on the global scale. There are two levels, a midpoint level and
an endpoint level. Midpoint indicators were selected for this analysis for interpretability, as
they have a more physical meaning to the system analyzed. For this analysis, we focused on
two indicators from this method: global warming and eutrophication. Global warming potential
was included as it is an extremely pertinent metric to overall plant sustainability. Eutrophication
was also selected for its relevance to nutrient removal in water treatment specifically in
Applications such as Application 2, biological nutrient removal through AvN + PdNA. For
eutrophication potential, the ReCiPe method separates out freshwater and marine
eutrophication in units of kg-P eq and kg-N eq respectively.

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 95

The cumulative energy demand LCIA method calculates the total energy used in the life cycle of
a product, both direct and indirect uses (i.e., indirect being embedded energy in chemicals, etc.
like PAA). Because many of the Applications in this project impact parameters such as chemical
dosing, embedded energies are important to consider. While this information is also captured
largely in the global warming potential impact category, having a kwh representation of this
value was deemed valuable to the idea of a levelized energy impact.

The AWARE method is one of the most recent and highly endorsed water foot printing methods
by WULCA. This method is based on “available” water, focusing on water “deprivation”. As
stated by Pfister et al. 2016b, “the physical interpretation is given as the amount of water
downstream users are lacking as a function of water consumption.” The reported water
footprint information here should be taken with caution when interpreting this value. The
reported water footprint here is not the same thing simply as a m3 of consumed water. The
value presented by the AWARE method “is not meant to be taken as true value, but it
represents a more damage-oriented method to combine emissions and water consumption
impacts (Pfister et al. 2016a).” The unit for this method is one aggregated term of m3 of water
compared to a world equivalent of 1 (PRé Sustainability 2022).

6.2 High-Rate Contact Stabilization (Application 1)
Historical data was used in an LCA/TEA to compare the efficacy of the high-rate contact
stabilization (HR-CS) system during 2020-2021 to the previously operated high-rate step feed
(HR-SF) system before 2020 at DC Water’s Blue Plains advanced wastewater treatment plant.
Both full scale experimentation and pilot scale work were conducted during this project,
however this LCA/TEA focuses on the full-scale implementation. Because the optimization of
the HR-CS was not implemented at full-scale, there is not sufficient evidence to conclude the
impacts through the LCA/TEA of HR-CS optimization. With the fully optimized system,
additional ferric chloride savings, aeration energy savings, and concrete savings due to
increased capacity were theorized. Shown in this summary is just the LCA/TEA comparison of
the HR-SF baseline system compared to the HR-CS system.

A decrease in ferric chloride use was exhibited between the HR-SF and HR-CS periods in this
analysis. It is hypothesized that this was due in part to greater P removal in the HR-CS
configuration, however further analysis would have been needed to fully confirm this cause of
the ferric reduction. There was not a significant decrease exhibited between power
requirements of the HR-SF and HR-CS periods. This was most likely due in part to limitations on
the blower system. The savings from FeCl3 alone in the HR-CS system compared to the HR-SF
system represented a 2-6% decrease across impact categories (with the highest decrease
exhibited in water footprint, and the lowest in cumulative energy demand). A decrease of 6% in
OPEX savings related to FeCl3 was also observed in the TEA.

6.3 BNR: ABAC / AvN + PdNA (Application 2)
This analysis compared AvN + PdNA with a more conventional biological process of
nitrification/denitrification. These processes were compared because advanced biological
processes such as AvN + PdNA in the mainstream necessitate implementation of advanced

96 The Water Research Foundation

process controls. The ability to benefit from advanced AvN + PdNA can therefore be attributed
in part to advanced controls, which were the focus of this Application. Because the expansion
of the plant has not fully occurred, the analysis conducted for HRSD’s Nansemond plant in this
LCA was partially theoretical. Energy and carbon demands were calculated by determining the
COD and oxygen requirements of both biological pathways. The energy was scaled based on the
Nansemond plant’s recent electricity usage data. The carbon MeOH dose, while theoretical for
the Nansemond BNR system, was further contextualized by scaling the current carbon usage to
the future flows used in this analysis. This can be seen in the full LCA/TEA report in Appendix A
for Application 2.

Significant reductions in global warming potential, cumulative energy demand, and water
footprint were demonstrated in this LCA with AvN + PdNA operation compared to ABAC and
conventional nitrification/denitrification. This was driven mainly by reductions in carbon and
electricity usage. The impacts associated with potential capacity savings (and therefore
concrete savings) were minimal. The eutrophication impacts were also driven almost entirely by
effluent nutrient concentrations which were assumed to meet the same effluent targets
despite the biological pathway analyzed. The TEA in this analysis demonstrated both potential
CAPEX and OPEX savings.

6.4 Disinfection: PAA (Application 3)
During the period of the study, an increase in PAA demand related to the system influent water
quality at Denver Metro’s Robert W. Hite Treatment Facility (RWHTF) was observed.
Additionally, the data driven PAA dosing scheme was never able to be actually implemented in
the PAA dosing control due to the limiting step of predicting the influent E coli with the data
available better than the current method. Because of these extenuating circumstances, it is
difficult to provide an accurate reflection of the improved PAA system for the current period of
the LCA and TEA, as these were mainly driven by PAA dose actually applied. Benefits of the
work accomplished through this project (including the better prediction of CT) were observed
more in process reliability than chemical savings. Results from the preliminary LCA/TEA and
goal PAA dosing compared to the baseline dosing were included in the summary tables in this
section, however, actual improvements based on data collected during this study were
impossible to quantify. The full LCA/TEA for the preliminary results in this Application can be
seen in Appendix A.

6.5 P-Recovery: MagPrex® (Application 4)
The solids treatment system at Denver Metro’s RWHTF was compared before MagPrex®
(historical data 2015-2020), with MagPrex® initially implemented (2020-2021), and during the
first two months of MagPrex® optimization with the advanced control online. In almost all
environmental impact categories analyzed in the LCA, there was at least a small decrease in
impact from the baseline scenario without MagPrex® to the initial operational scenario with
MagPrex® online. Optimization of MagPrex® decreased each impact category further,
particularly for water deprivation.

It should be noted that the optimized MagPrex® scenario operational data was from a limited
data set during the first two months of optimized control implementation. It should also be

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 97

noted that in addition to the advanced data driven process controls optimized as a part of this
project, other traditional optimization efforts were also implemented for MagPrex® during the
time period of this study. Reductions in dewatering polymer, MgCl2 use in the MagPrex®
system, and increase in struvite recovered were included in this analysis. Controlled
precipitation of struvite through MagPrex® also reduces harmful nuisance struvite build-up in
RWHTF infrastructure (Wisdom et al. 2017). The associated impact reduction of infrastructure
repair and maintenance were not captured in this analysis but are an important consideration
for the overarching benefits of MagPrex®.

Of all the optimized parameters, the savings in MgCl2 were the most directly related to the
optimization through data driven process controls. The other optimized parameters were still
included in this analysis for contextualization of the impacts of the MagPrex® system. The
reduction in MgCl2 specifically lead to savings of around $49,000 annually in the TEA, or
$0.0003/m3 product water normalized to mainstream treatment flow. The OPEX savings
associated with MgCl2 made up 7% of the total OPEX reductions with MagPrex® optimization
compared to the initial MagPrex® OPEX TEA. It also made up to about 8% of the reductions in
the LCA impact categories (the highest proportion in the water deprivation impact, and lowest
in the eutrophication impacts). The full LCA/TEA can be seen in the Application 4 MagPrex®
report in Appendix A.

6.6 Holistic Biosolids Optimization (Application 5)
Better prediction of TS% through the solids treatment train at DC Water’s Blue Plains advanced
wastewater treatment plant was hypothesized to lead to more accurate polymer dosing at the
final dewatering step, improving both polymer dosing and/or biosolid hauling impacts. During
this project, TS% could be predicted at the pre-digestion step in the treatment train but could
not to be predicted at the final dewatering step. This was largely due to limited ability to test
different conditions and collect a robust training data set for the model at this step, as slight
changes to the TS% at this step would risk disrupting the entire final dewatering process.
Consequently, the benefits of this work were not able to be quantified in the LCA model, as it
was centered around the benefits of being able to predict the TS% at this final step. Benefits of
this work mainly centered around reduced labor and better process reliability as will be
discussed in chapter 7. Future work outside of the scope of this project may extrapolate TS%
prediction that was successful at pre-digestion step to be able to predict TS% downstream at
this critical final dewatering step. Results from the preliminary LCA/TEA and goal polymer
dosing compared to the manual dosing were included in the summary tables in this section,
however, actual improvements based on data collected during this study were impossible to
quantify. The full LCA/TEA for the preliminary results in this Application can be seen in
Appendix A.

CHAPTER 7
Summary Results, Key Achievements, Lessons Learned,
and Future Work
This chapter presents the summary results, in terms of model performance, model deployment,
and net achievements, along with the lessons learned for the five case studies of data-driven
process controls. These case studies were implemented in real full-scale facilities for the five
promising Applications that are the focus of this project, namely:
1. Carbon diversion: high-rate contact stabilization
2. BNR: ABAC / AvN + PdNA
3. Disinfection: PAA
4. P-recovery: MagPrex®
5. Holistic biosolids optimization
Collectively, these Applications provide a whole plant approach for achieving substantial energy
and resource recovery benefits. Each case study followed the steps used in the ML Toolkit
framework (e.g., workflow) process, specifically:
• Define Problem
• Get Data
• Prepare Data
• Train Model
• Deploy Model
The case studies provide important examples of how each step of the ML Toolkit framework
works such that users will have a better understanding of the workflow process, lessons
learned, and how to develop and implement ML models at their facilities.

For more details on each case study, users can visit the ML Toolkit website for links to the code
that was used (e.g., Google Colab notebook), short project overview videos, code walk-through
videos, and more. The ML Toolkit website can be accessed via the WRF project 5141 page.

Table 7-1 provides the summary results along with the lessons learned for each of the five case
study Applications. The summary results are presented in terms of model performance, model
deployment, as well as net achievements related to improvements in capacity, process
reliability, energy, chemical savings, nutrient recovery, OPEX and CAPEX costs, etc. These
summary results supplement the summary results of the LCA and TEA provided in Chapter 6.6.
The lessons learned will guide those considering embarking on a data-driven controls approach
at their utility or in their business so they can avoid unexpected challenges or reinventing the
wheel.

Through meetings and information exchanges regarding the individual case study Applications,
the project team also identified the following cross-cutting general themes, considerations, and
lessons learned from the project overall:
• Simpler Can Be Better:
 Given the promise and hype surrounding AI and ML approaches, some users may be
 predisposed to choosing an ML solution for solving a wastewater process challenges like a
 hammer looking for a nail. However, users should first carefully consider the reasons an ML
 solution may be necessary and the right approach for a particular challenge. For example, if
 we do not have a good understanding of the dynamics or mechanistics of a system, ML
 tools may be very useful. Users should also consider if a simpler approach is suitable before
 electing to use a more complex ML model. In some cases, simpler statistical or
 mathematical techniques may be able to provide similar or better results to a more complex
 and resource intensive ML modeling approach.
• The Importance of Problem Definition:
 For developing solutions using ML approaches, it is important to take time to first clearly
 articulate and define the problem that needs to be solved. Users need to make sure they
 are solving the right problem. Then, a challenge will be for users to keep the main problem
 the focus of any subsequent efforts. Otherwise, a user risk veering off on a problem-solving
 tangent or not solving the correct problem to begin with.
• The Art of Outlier Removal:
 Correctly identifying and removing outliers from datasets can be challenging, and to users it
 may seem like a combination of art and science. What is the best way to handle outliers?
 Users need to negotiate the tension between capturing the 5% edge/extreme data points
 versus justifying their removal as outliers. Users will need weigh the value of the data points
 to a particular dataset, as well as understand their reasonableness for a given situation.
 Users will also need to determine when outlier removal may be automated versus when
 human engagement may be necessary.
• Experimental Design and Collecting Wide-Ranging Data:
 In developing experimental designs to collect data for ML solutions, users should consider
 when and where to collect samples so that one captures “interesting” data. In particular, it
 is important to collect wide-ranging data that describes the system fully and catches any
 anomalies/perturbations that may take place. If the process is too tightly/narrowly
 controlled, users may not catch potential anomalies/perturbations. Collecting wide-ranging
 data can be challenging for some situations at WRRFs, though. For example, plant operators
 would not want to risk potential permit violations to gather anomalies/perturbations.
 Getting wide ranging data near failure points may be difficult to justify for full-scale systems
 and warrants considering pilot-scale studies that push treatment trains and allow for
 perturbation and "interesting" data to be collected safely. Additionally, users may want to
 program in check-points in a sample collection regime to modify the experimental design if
 needed based on the data collected. It can also be challenging determining how much data
 is needed (e.g., the granularity of data). Sometimes users spend a lot of time collecting data
 which may or may not ultimately be needed. It is important to determine where to draw
 the line in data collection such that a sufficient amount of data is collected but not too
 much such that resources of time and money are expended unnecessarily.

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 105

• Mechanistic Models:
 For wastewater process challenges, users may want to consider the balance between using
 a complex mechanistic model versus a simple, black box ML model. When is the simple,
 black box ML model sufficient? How complex should the mechanistic model be? It may
 depend on the problem being solved. A user will likely want to have the model be as
 complex as needed, but no more than that. They will also need to have the necessary data
 for the solution approach being used. Hybrid mechanistic-ML modeling approaches can also
 be used, as was demonstrated in the Application 2 case study at HRSD’s Nansemond
 Treatment Plant.
• Computer Scientists & the Water Sector:
 In order to implement ML solutions at WRRFs more widely in the future, the water sector
 needs to consider how to attract more computer scientists, data scientists, statisticians,
 etc., as well as how to compete for this workforce against high-tech firms which can often
 offer higher-paying positions. Perhaps organizations in the water sector can tout some of
 the benefits and advantages that may be unique to the water sector or different from high-
 tech firms such as working for mission driven water organizations focused on improving
 public health and the environment, work/family balance, more complex or interesting
 projects involving physics, chemistry, biology, and other disciplines, relatively large and
 stable work sector/environment, etc. Alternatively, the water sector can work to train
 wastewater process engineers in these disciplines. The ML Toolkit and final report from this
 project have been developed to specifically help support the professional growth,
 development, and training of wastewater process engineers and others to enable more
 broad-scale implementation of these solutions.
• Operator Engagement & Training:
 During the course of this project, operations staff at multiple facilities were interviewed for
 their understanding of control systems and trust in ML. The spectrum of trust in ML was
 found to directly correlate to (1) operators general understanding of control systems, (2)
 historical and regular engagement with the engineering team as control upgrades are made,
 and (3) trust in the process rather than trust in an individual model or ML technology
 broadly. Where operations staff felt siloed in their roles and responsibilities, they tended to
 distrust new systems. Where operations staff were integrated into training, decision
 making, and problem solving, operators trusted that as a multidisciplinary team they would
 find a way to success.
• Challenges of Real-World Deployment:
 Developing the model is only part of the challenge of integrating data-driven process
 controls at WRRFs. The deployment of these models within a real utility environment
 results in a variety of additional challenges and barriers to success. Some of these can be
 generalized across WRRFs including how to deal with data quality issues from things like
 sensor drift and process anomalies or retraining models in the face of dynamic data
 environments (e.g., process upgrades, seasonality effects). Some are WRRF-specific issues
 include dealing with organizational cybersecurity systems, integrating model outcomes into
 SCADA systems, and ensuring regulatory approval and permit compliance. WRRFs will need

106 The Water Research Foundation

 to strategize beyond the model development stages to ensure these efforts can provide
 long-term, sustainable value.
In conclusion, the key benefits and outcomes from the project include:
• Developed an ML Toolkit framework (i.e., workflow) to guide users in the steps or workflow
 needed for implementation of ML solutions.
• Using the ML Toolkit framework, developed and demonstrated data-driven process controls
 in real full-scale facilities for five promising Applications (WRRF process technologies).
• Conducted an LCA and TEA to quantify the benefits realized from implementation of each
 Application in this project compared to a relevant conventional baseline scenario.
• Developed an ML Toolkit (i.e., website) to support the training of wastewater process
 engineers and others to enable more broad-scale implementation of ML solutions. The ML
 Toolkit website can be accessed via the WRF project 5141 page.
• Summarized ML implementation considerations and lessons learned for each of the five
 case study Applications as well as the project overall.
Future research is needed to continue to support the development and deployment of ML
models, including hybrid models, at full-scale facilities. In particular, there is a need for further
research on additional case study examples for different types of applications, developing the
business case for various data driven process controls, and developing and sustaining the
workforce necessary to support the implementation of these solutions in the water sector.

Lastly, in support of project outreach and technology transfer, the project team conducted a
workshop at the 2023 WEF/IWA Innovations in Process Engineering Conference, a workshop at
WEFTEC 2024, a WRF webcast in 2025, along with numerous other conference papers,
presentations, and publications. A complete list of the project outreach and technology transfer
activities is provided in Appendix B.

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 107

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 109

Both full scale experimentation and pilot scale work were conducted for the secondary
treatment of DC Water’s Blue Plains advanced wastewater treatment plant during this project,
however this LCA/TEA focuses on the full-scale implementation. The work from this project
focused specifically on optimizing the high rate contact stabilization (HR-CS) with data driven
process controls for SRT optimization. This step however was not implemented at full scale and
so there is not sufficient evidence to conclude the impacts through the LCA/TEA of HR-CS
optimization. Nevertheless, historical data was used in an LCA/TEA to compare the efficacy of
the high-rate contact stabilization (HR-CS) system during 2020-2021 to the previously operated
high-rate step feed (HR-SF) system before 2020.

A significant decrease in ferric chloride (FeCl₃) use was exhibited between the HR-SF and HR-CS
periods. It is hypothesized that this was due in part to greater P removal in the HR-CS
configuration, however further analysis would have been needed to fully confirm this cause of
the FeCl₃ reduction. There was not a significant decrease exhibited between power
requirements of the HR-SF and HR-CS periods. This was most likely due in part to limitations on
the blower system.

The largest contributing process to global warming potential, cumulative energy demand, and
eutrophication potential in this study was the electricity used for aeration in the activated
sludge system. The savings from FeCl₃ alone in the HR-CS system compared to the HR-SF system
represented a 2-6% decrease across impact categories (with the highest decrease exhibited in
water footprint, and the lowest in cumulative energy demand).

A.1.2 Goal and Scope

This project focuses on carbon diversion with high rate contact stabilization, as a part of the
broader DOE project: Integration of data-driven process control for maximization of energy and
resource efficiency in advanced water resource recovery facilities (WRRFs).

Substantial operational improvements are possible in activated sludge systems through the
implementation of contact stabilization (HR-CS) as compared to step feed (HR-SF) activated
sludge, which was previously in operation at Blue Plains. These improvements have the
potential for high energy savings, capacity savings, as well as decreased chemical consumption.
The environmental sustainability implications of three different scenarios for the high rate
activated sludge system (HR-AS) at Blue Plains advanced wastewater treatment plant were
analyzed through LCA.

Baseline high rate step feed (~before 2020):
The first baseline scenario in this analysis focused on historical data under the step feed (SF)
configuration for DC Water’s HR-AS system. In this configuration, primary effluent (PE) to be
treated is fed into the system at multiple points along the reactor basin, including at the very
beginning of the HR-AS system. A diagram illustrating this configuration is shown in Figure A-2,
where each pass of the AS system is shown as a reactor. A substantial amount of energy is used
to add oxygen at each pass in order to oxidize and remove COD as CO₂.

110 The Water Research Foundation

In addition to oxidation, COD is also removed through redirection, primarily through
biosorption into sludge which can then be harvested in the WAS stream by settling in the
downstream clarifier. This produces biogas energy in the downstream anaerobic digester (AD).

Compared to conventional activated sludge systems, HR-AS systems including the HR-SF
configuration are extremely productive in this regard. The shorter solids retention time (SRT)
reduces the energy required to oxidize the carbon within the system and also means more
carbon can potentially be harvested by the downstream anaerobic digester.

Unfortunately, HR-AS operation also can lead to worsening and more fluctuating secondary
effluent quality. Effluent quality regulations as well as the solids handling system limitations
also constrict the extent to which the SRT can be reduced.

 Figure A-2. Influent PE Is Added at Each Pass (Reactor Unit) of the HR-AS System Under HR-SF Operation.

Current high rate contact stabilization (~2020-2021):
Contact stabilization (CS) is a HR-AS configuration that introduces primary effluent starting at
the second pass of the system. By not feeding the primary effluent into the first pass of the
reactor, a “famine” stabilizing zone is created as is shown in Figure A-3. This feast-famine
operation forces a quick increase in extracellular polymeric substance formation (EPS), which
binds carbon constituents and in turn has shown to improve flocculation (Rahman et al. 2017).

Better bioflocculation has the potential to reduce the energy requirements of the HR-AS
system. It may improve the oxygen transfer efficiency in the system. The improved
bioflocculation also reduces the necessary SRT and therefore requires less energy to support
the biomass that oxidizes the COD to CO₂. As previously discussed, improved carbon capture
may have the potential for more energy generation in the downstream anaerobic digester.
Further, it is hypothesized that this feast famine response leads to greater phosphorus removal.
Secondary effluent quality improvements could potentially reduce secondary effluent ferric
chloride (FeCl₃) requirements and downstream biological nutrient removal energy
requirements as well.

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 111

 Figure A-3. Influent PE Is Added in the Last 3 Passes (Contact Units) of The HR-AS System. A “Famine Zone” Is
 Created in the First Pass.

Contact stabilization with improved control (theoretical):
Due to constraints on the solids handling system, the WAS flow and therefore the SRT has little
room for testing at full-scale. The work from this project focused specifically on optimizing the
high rate contact stabilization (HR-CS) with data driven process controls for SRT optimization.
This step was not implemented at full scale due to these limitations for SRT reduction. There is
not sufficient evidence to conclude the impacts through the LCA/TEA of HR-CS optimization.
The main focus of this LCA then is the differences between the HR-SF and HR-CS systems.

Objectives:
• To assess the comparative environmental impact of the HR-SF (before 2020) and HR-CS
 (2020-2021) configurations of DC Water’s HR-AS system.
• To identify which life cycle stages of the treatment process have the largest environmental
 impacts to see where additional optimizations to the system are possible.

various treatment capacities, the results were normalized by the volume treated in the system.
Therefore, the functional basis for this LCA was one cubic meter of wastewater treated by the
HR-AS system. Presenting these results per cubic meter of treated water also provided a more
interpretable metric relevant to plant operation.

Impacts were calculated for 50 years of operation, as this is a typical lifespan for capital
investments such as the increased reactor capacity. These results were then divided by the
volume of water treated at the plant in this timespan.

112 The Water Research Foundation

Construction impacts associated with the required increase in HR-AS system capacity factored
into this analysis as the total amount of concrete needed in the expanded basins. The focus of
this LCA was on the operational phase of the system, focusing on processes impacted by the
improvements in HR-AS performance. It also included the material acquisition of the affected
chemicals including their transportation and packaging.

The relevant processes are outlined for DC Water in Figure A-4. Main inputs into the system
were FeCl₃, transportation of the FeCl₃, plant electricity use, and concrete. While anaerobic
digestion is not included within the bounds of the analyzed system, a portion of the biogas
energy was included in this analysis due to a possible increase in biogas formation under
optimized conditions. This was factored in as an energy offset. Emissions associated with
methane losses in the biogas combustion were still attributed for this portion. Downstream
nutrient removal was also not included within the boundaries of the HR-AS system. As a
consequence, any N and P removal benefits were not included as an effluent improvement.

  Figure A-4. System Boundary for the LCA Of DC Water’s HR-AS System Includes Chemical Acquisition, Energy
  Consumption, and Construction Impacts for the New Reactor. Downstream Biological Nutrient Removal and
 Solids Handling Not Included Within the System Boundary.

A.1.3 Inventory Analysis

The majority of this analysis was conducted based on information provided by DC Water on the
operation of their plant. Transportation distances were determined using the information
provided by DC Water and the locations of Kemira chemical facilities for the production of
FeCl₃. The study on phosphorus removal by Sena et al. 2021 was used for biogas emission
information. Information from Masłoń, 2020, was used to determine the energy production in

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 113

the biogas produced. Finally, the main database for all of the relevant processes in Simapro was
the Ecoinvent database.

TEA/LCA Inputs:
Both a TEA and LCA were conducted in parallel to analyze the system's economic and
environmental sustainability impacts. Much of the input data for the TEA model overlapped
with the necessary information for the LCA model. Input information for the foreground system
for this analysis can be divided into four main categories: treatment basis, energy basis,
chemical basis, and transportation. Figure A-5 shows the two models working in parallel to take
in the foreground inputs and generate the environmental and economic impact areas of focus.

 Figure A-5. Data Flow Overview of Main Parameters with Parallel LCA and TEA Models.

The following table shows unit cost information for relevant TEA inputs, including an estimate
for capital costs associated with increased capacity. This input information was relayed to the
National Energy Technology Laboratory (NETL) for inclusion in the WaterTAP3 model.

Treatment Basis:
Overall plant flow rate information provided by DC Water is outlined in Table A-3 (including
flow to both the East and West sides). Results in this analysis were calculated for 50 years of

treated in this timespan. Concrete needed under the plant expansion was included in both the
HR-SF and current HR-CS scenarios.

 Parameter Value Unit
 Plant flow rate 300 MGD
 Timespan 50 years
 New reactor capacity 384 MGD
 Concrete for new reactor 5,063 m3

Because of the famine zone in the CS configuration, less phosphorus is expected to be present
in the secondary effluent from the HR-AS system. Nutrient effluent quality was not directly
factored into this analysis, including both nitrogen and phosphorus removal. This is because the
downstream nitrogen and phosphorus removal systems will be operated to maintain plant
effluent below stringent nutrient regulations, regardless of the nitrogen and phosphorus load
received in the secondary effluent.

Chemical Use:
A significant decrease in HR-AS secondary FeCl₃ use between the HR-SF and HR-CS periods was
exhibited. It was hypothesized that improved secondary effluent quality, including less
phosphorus, was the cause of this reduced secondary FeCl₃. Annual FeCl₃ use was calculated
using an overall dose (both East and West side combined) provided by DC Water for the HR-SF
and HR-CS periods of 3.58±0.72 mg-Fe/L and 3.22±0.64 mg-Fe/L respectively.

The relevant Ecoinvent process for FeCl₃ was for a 40% w/w solution. This is consistent with
provided SDS information by DC water. FeCl₃ values were input based on the active agent mass
(FeCl₃), not based on the overall solution mass, as this is how Ecoinvent chemicals are reported
(Weidema et al. 2013). The overall solution mass was used as well for FeCl₃ transportation
calculations.

Energy Basis:
The electricity generation mix information provided by Pepco (2020), DC Water’s electricity
provider, is outlined in Appendix A.1. Relevant processes from Ecoinvent for each electricity
source in the SERC power grid were input into a DC Water energy mix process in Simapro.

Daily averages of power consumption for both the East and West side of the DC Water HR-AS
system were provided for the periods of HR-SF and HR-CS configuration. This came out to an
average annual energy use of 3.501E+07 kWh/yr and 3.496E+07 kWh/yr for the HR-SF and HR-
CS periods respectively. Even though COD redirection improved between the two periods, no
significant decrease in HR-AS COD oxidation energy arose. This was theorized to be the
consequence of two things. There are limitations on the minimum air that can be supplied with
some of the facility's larger blowers. If aeration savings are not large enough to warrant a
switch from the larger to smaller blowers, potential aeration savings may not be fully realized.

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 115

Additionally, full optimization of the SRT was not implemented at full scale to reduce the
carbon oxidation.

Downstream of the HR-AS system, potential energy savings were hypothesized to occur due to
lower aeration requirements in the enhanced biological nutrient removal (ENR) process as well.
Between the two periods analyzed, no significant reduction in ENR aeration energy occurred.
There was, however, a trend of less ENR aeration energy (normalized to the nitrogen mass
loading) when analyzed against the secondary TSS parameter. As TSS from the HR-AS system
becomes consistently lower, the projected energy savings may therefore be exhibited.
Regardless, the downstream energy was outside the boundaries of the immediately analyzed
system.

Another energy efficiency consideration is the amount of biogas that can be produced as a
response to increased carbon capture. The exact nature of the relationship between improved
carbon capture and anaerobic digestion biogas production is difficult to determine. No clear
trend between the improved carbon redirection and the anaerobic energy production has yet
been determined between the two periods. The biogas energy therefore is currently set as an
offset of ⅓ of electricity use, as this is approximately the portion of total energy the biogas
energy is able to offset for internal use within DC Water.

Biogas emissions associated with the anaerobic digester energy offset are outlined in the Table
A-4. These emission factors were sourced from Sena et al. 2021, which based relevant emission
factors on the work done by Carnevale and Lombardi (2015). The methane (CH₄) emissions
from Carnevale’s method were calculated for 1% CH₄ slip, assuming around 60% of the biogas is
CH₄ by volume. While the remaining portion of the biogas is mainly CO₂, biogenic CO₂ emissions
do not contribute to global warming potential in standard LCA methods. The same applies for
CO₂ emission occurring in the activated sludge unit from the oxidation of the COD. Additionally,
only CH₄ was relevant to our analysis of the emissions listed in the following table. CH₄ had a
relevant emission factor to global warming potential.

DC Water’s FeCl₃ manufacturer is Kemira Chemicals. A transportation distance of 1,000 km was
assumed based on DC Water’s FeCl₃ supplier in Atlanta, GA. From this, and from FeCl₃ mass
needed each year, transportation values were calculated. FeCl₃ is shipped in reusable tanker

116 The Water Research Foundation

trailers, and therefore no packaging associations were included. Transportation values were
input for this process as the mass of load carried times the distance traveled for a unit of
ton*km = tkm. A portion of the return trip was inherently accounted for in the Ecoinvent
process methodology (Spielmann et al. 2007), as discussed in the overall LCA framework
document.

A.1.4 Impact Assessment
LCA Results:
Figures A-6 through A-8 show the environmental impact of each HR-AS scenario broken up by
process contribution and normalized per m3 of treated water. Energy here includes the HR-AS
energy use and the biogas offset as well. In all impact categories, a slight decrease between the
HR-SF and HR-CS scenarios was exhibited. Almost all of the decrease was due to the reduction
in FeCl₃ and FeCl₃ packaging (between 93 to 99% of the total decrease in different impact
categories). For the 50 year lifespan, the new reactor concrete impact was almost negligible in
all impact categories.

In all impact categories, the dominating contributing process was the energy use except in the
water footprint. As can be seen in Figure A-8, the largest contributing process to the water
footprint was the FeCl₃ addition. The high contribution of aeration energy in most categories
indicates that reducing this factor should be a priority for sustainable operation.

Figure A-6. LCA Results Broken Down by Process for the Baseline HR-SF Configuration and the Current HR-CS for
 Global Warming Potential (Left) and Cumulative Energy Demand (Right), Normalized Per m3 of Treated Water.

scenarios. The CAPEX cost of the basin was normalized by dividing by the water treated in the
basin’s expected lifetime of 50 years. OPEX savings can be seen between the periods of HR-SF
and HR-CS operation.

A.2 Application 2 - Biological Nutrient Removal - AvN + PdNA LCA
A.2.1 Overview of Application
Goal: To compare the coupled environmental impact of two different control schemes and
biological nutrient removal pathways during the expansion of HRSD’s Nansemond plant:
ammonia-based aeration control (ABAC) + conventional nitrification-denitrification (DN) &
ammonia to nitrate aeration control (AvN) + partial denitrification with anammox (PdNA).

Figure A-11. Environmental Impacts for ABAC + DN vs. AvN + PdNA. Results Normalized Per m3 of Treated Water
 and Presented as a Percentage of the Baseline Impact in Each Category.

 Table A-11. Overall Environmental Impacts Per m3 of Treated Water in Each Scenario.
 Impact category Method Unit ABAC + DN AVN + PDNA

 Global Warming ReCiPe kg CO2 eq 0.1217 0.1025

 Freshwater Eutrophication ReCiPe kg P eq 1.70E-05 1.49E-05

 Marine Eutrophication ReCiPe kg N eq 7.45E-04 7.45E-04

             Total CED                     CED               Kwh              1.1309                0.9282

 Water Footprint AWARE m3 0.0288 0.0249

While the focus of this project is optimized controls for AvN + PdNA, this LCA compared AvN +
PdNA with more conventional biological processes such as conventional
nitrification/denitrification. This was because this more advanced biological process is only
made feasible once robust/advanced control is in place to accurately control the BNR system.
The ability to benefit from advanced AvN + PdNA BNR can therefore be attributed to the
advanced control that makes it possible. Because the expansion of the plant has not yet
occurred, the analysis conducted in this LCA was partially theoretical. Energy and carbon

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 125

demands were calculated by determining the COD and oxygen requirements of both biological
pathways. The energy was scaled based on the Nansemond plant recent electricity usage data.
The carbon MEOH dose, while theoretical, was contextualized by scaling the current carbon
usage to the future flows used in this analysis. Significant reductions in global warming
potential, cumulative energy demand, and water footprint were demonstrated in this LCA with
AvN + PdNA operation compared to ABAC and conventional nitrification/denitrification. This
driven mainly by reductions in carbon and electricity usage. The impacts associated with
capacity savings (and therefore concrete savings) were minimal. The eutrophication impacts
were also driven almost entirely by effluent nutrient concentrations which were assumed to
meet the same effluent targets despite the biological pathway analyzed. The TEA demonstrated
both CAPEX and OPEX savings from a total of $0.048/m3 product water, to $0.031/m3 water.

A.2.2 Goal and Scope

This project is a component of application #2 - biological nutrient removal through ABAC/AvN +
PdNA as a part of the broader DOE project: Integration of data-driven process control for
maximization of energy and resource efficiency in advanced water resource recovery facilities
(WRRFs).

HRSD’s Nansemond WWTP is currently undergoing an expansion in capacity from 30 MGD to 50
MGD. During this expansion, the biological nutrient removal system at the plant will be
converted from an ammonia based aeration control (ABAC) + conventional nitrification and full
denitrification system (referred to here as DN), to a more advanced ammonia to nitrate control
(AvN) + partial denitrification with anammox (PdNA) system.

PdNA has many potential environmental benefits, however, this new technology is only feasible
with improved aeration and carbon control. Therefore, advanced control strategies such as AvN
not only improve plant operation, but also pave the way for new technologies such as PdNA to
be employed. As such, the scenario with the advanced control mechanism AvN was coupled in
this analysis with the advanced biological pathway PdNA. This was compared to the coupled
scenario of ABAC aeration control and conventional DN that is currently implemented at the
Nansemond plant. An LCA was employed to illuminate the sustainability implications associated
with this transition.

Baseline ABAC + DN system:
HRSD’s Nansemond plant currently has the equivalent of five parallel biological nutrient
removal reactors (four large tanks and three smaller tanks with a capacity ~equivalent to the
capacity of one large tank) each designed as a 5-stage Bardenpho process as shown in Figure A-
12. This system currently operates with conventional nitrification-denitrification to remove
incoming organic nitrogen to nitrogen gas (N2). In conventional nitrification-denitrification, A
high amount of energy is used for aeration in the aerobic zone to convert all of the ammonia
(NH3) to nitrate (NO3). In the following anoxic zone, an external carbon source is added to
convert this NO3 to nitrite (NO2), and then fully to N2 gas.

126 The Water Research Foundation

Aeration to convert NH3 to NO3 in the aerobic zone is currently controlled based on the NH3
concentration in a strategy called ABAC. While this is an advanced control strategy, there is still
a loss of treatment capacity due to the low dissolved oxygen required in ABAC operation. The
baseline case investigated the scenario in which the currently operated ABAC + DN system
continues to be operated after the plant expansion.

  Figure A-12. Nansemond Plant Schematic Operated Under Conventional Full Nitrification in the Aerobic Zone
 and Full Denitrification in the Following Anoxic Zone. Aeration Is Controlled Based on an Ammonia NH3
 Concentration (ABAC). Parameters that Will Change with AvN + PdNA Control Are Designated in Blue.

Proposed AvN + PdNA system:
After the expansion, the Nansemond plant’s biological nutrient removal system will continue to
be operated as a 5-stage Bardenpho system. The control scheme and biological mechanism
however will be transitioned during the expansion into an AvN + PdNA system as shown in
Figure A-13. In the AvN + PdNA scenario, a moving integrated fixed film activated sludge (IFAS)
2nd anoxic zone will be implemented to support the PdNA biofilm on plastic moving media
chips. In PdNA, only a portion of the NH3 is converted to NO3 through aeration in the aerobic
zone. Some of the NH3 is retained for use later on in the following anoxic zone. In the anoxic
zone, NO3 is converted to NO2 with the addition of COD. Instead of full conversion of the NO2 to
N2 gas using COD, the NO2 is converted to N2 gas by the NH3 retained in the water through
anammox. This advanced pathway will allow for less aeration energy because less of the NH3 is
converted to NO3. It also requires less supplemental COD required to fully denitrify the NO2 to
N2 gas.

Aeration to convert NH3 to NO3 in the aerobic zone in this scenario will be controlled based on
the NH3:NOx ratio in a strategy denoted AvN. AvN aeration control allows for more efficient
COD use and aeration energy supplied. Maintaining a consistent AvN ratio is also crucial for
optimized partial denitrification, where the ratio of NH3 and NO3 plays a large role in the
cultivation of the microbial community. Capacity benefits will also be realized in the system due
to the more efficient aeration control.

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 127

It should be noted that the construction of a new reactor will occur at this plant regardless of
the capacity savings realized. This is due to construction constraints on the plant expansion. The
capacity savings were still added to this analysis, as a portion of the existing tanks will be
repurposed for other functions.
