Objectives:
● To assess the comparative environmental impact of HRSD’s AvN + PdNA system at the
 Nansemond WTP and the currently operated ABAC + DN system after plant expansion.
● To identify which components of the biological nutrient removal system have the largest
 environmental impact to see where additional optimizations can be made.

various treatment capacities, the results were normalized by the volume treated in the system.
Therefore, the functional basis for this LCA was one cubic meter of wastewater treated by the
biological nutrient removal system. Presenting these results per cubic meter of treated water
also provided a more interpretable metric relevant to plant operation. Impacts were calculated
assuming a 10-year lifespan for the media in the IFAS system, and a 50-year lifespan for the
reactor expansion. Results were then normalized by the volume of water treated at the plant
during this time.

This analysis included the operational phase of the system, specifically focusing on electricity
and carbon supplemented as methanol (MeOH). It also included the material acquisition of the
relevant chemicals, including their transportation. Because of the focus of this system on

128 The Water Research Foundation

nutrient removal, effluent ammonia and nitrate were also within the system boundaries. The
construction phases for the expansion of the plant in each scenario was also a factor in this
analysis. In the case of ABAC, this included the reactor expansion. In the AvN scenario, this was
the media used in the second anoxic zone.

The demolition and maintenance phases of the biological nutrient removal system were not a
factor in the analysis. More efficient use of carbon in PdNA has the potential to increase the
amount of incoming COD that can be harvested in the primary clarifier for anaerobic digestion
without compromising the performance of the system (McCullough et al. 2022). However, a
trade-off exists between the capacity benefits and the COD harvesting benefits. In the case of
this expansion, HRSD chose to implement the capacity benefits in their design. Therefore the
potential downstream effects of improved COD harvesting were not a factor in this analysis. No
other processes upstream and downstream of the biological nutrient removal system were
considered affected by changes in this analysis. The system diagram indicating the processes
within the system boundary can be seen in Figure A-14.

 Figure A-14. System Diagram for LCA of the Nansemond WWTP Biological Nutrient Removal System.

A.2.3 Inventory Analysis

Because the expansion of the plant has not yet occurred, the analysis conducted in this LCA was
partially theoretical. Much of the information came from HRSD for the current operation of the
Nansemond plant over the past year. Some of it however was based on BioWin modeling done
for the future system. Transportation distances were determined using the information
provided by HRSD and assumed chemical manufacturers. Energy mix information was sourced
from HRSD’s energy supplier, Dominion Energy. Ecoinvent was the main database used for
relevant background processes in Simapro. The supplemental tables and figures section A.2.5
contains a figure with all relevant data sources listed.

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 129

TEA/LCA Inputs:
Both a TEA and LCA were conducted in parallel to analyze the system's economic and
environmental sustainability impacts. Much of the input data for the TEA model overlapped
with the necessary information for the LCA model. Input information for the foreground system
of this analysis was divided into four main categories: treatment basis, energy use, chemical
use, and construction. Figure A-15 shows the two models working in parallel to take in the
foreground inputs and generate the environmental and economic impact areas of focus.

The additional concrete for two aerobic tanks would be needed in the Nansemond plant
expansion under ABAC + DN. In the case of AvN + PdNA, only one of these additional reactors
would be needed. Three smaller tanks which are used currently when wet weather events
occur would be able to be repurposed in this scenario as well. HRSD indicated that this would
be the equivalent capacity as if the two new reactors were both avoided. Another important
thing to note is that even though only one additional tank is needed in the plant expansion
under AvN + PdNA, both aerobic tanks are planned to be built regardless. Linear scaling of costs
and impacts do not extend well to capital costs on large construction projects. The difference
between the cost for HRSD to implement one versus two additional tanks was negligible
compared to the overall cost of the plant expansion. Because of this, HRSD opted to have both
reactors built at once, as the second one would most likely need to be built at a later date. The
concrete needed for both tanks was still included in this LCA. While there are constraints on the
actual reactor construction savings, these savings are still pertinent to capturing the
sustainability benefits of the capacity increase achieved with this technology. As is discussed
later in our results, however, the added impact associated with the reactor construction was
comparatively negligible, rendering this assumption somewhat inconsequential. The additional
reactors are outlined in red in Figure A-16. The existing four large aeration tanks are outlined in
blue. The small tanks equivalent to one large tank are outlined in green.

Although no reactor expansion concrete impacts were associated with the AvN + PdNA
scenario, media use for the IFAS system must be taken into account. Plastic media was included
in the LCA for suspension of the biofilm in the AvN + PdNA system. This media will be added at
max ~60% fill of the first ⅓ of each of the five 2nd anoxic tanks.

Chemical Use:
The major chemical used in the biological nutrient removal of nitrogen is MeOH as an external
source of carbon COD. MeOH is used in the denitrification of NO3 to NO2, and in the case of full
denitrification, to also fully convert NO3 to N2 gas. These processes occur in the anoxic zones of
the 5-stage Bardenpho process.

Currently, MeOH is dosed to the BNR system both in the first reactor and in the second reactor.
Figure A-17 shows current MeOH doses as well as flow rates within the reactor under current
operating conditions in green. This was informed by data over the last 12 months of operation
of the plant. Figure A-17 also shows flows and MeOH COD addition scaled to the new annual
average incoming flow rate of 37 MGD. Flows within the system as well as MeOH addition were
assumed to scale linearly with the increased influent flow rate.

132 The Water Research Foundation

 Figure A-17. Pre-Expansion and Post-Expansion Flow Rates and MeOH Addition, Assumed to Scale
 Linearly with Incoming Flow Rate.

Because the AvN system and expansion will not be implemented within the span of this project,
the LCA conducted for the future scenario was partially theoretical. In order to provide a
baseline consistent with this theoretical scenario, the ABAC + DN scenario was also analyzed as
a theoretical scenario using consistent methods with the analysis of AvN + PdNA. It therefore
makes sense to discuss the AvN + PdNA scenario and then the ABAC+DN scenario to follow the
logical progression of the steps taken in this LCA.

The main basis for this analysis was the MeOH required per gram of NO3 oxidized through each
respective pathway in the 2nd anoxic zone. The COD consumption of MeOH per gram of
incoming NO3 for partial denitrification is 1.92 g COD/g NO3 and for full denitrification is 4.8 g
COD/g NO3. While most of the denitrification in the 2nd anoxic zone in the PdNA scenario will be
partial denitrification, some full denitrification will still occur at the higher COD consumption
rate. The partial denitrification PDN “efficiency” (the fraction of total denitrification occurring
that is only partial) ranges typically from 50-75%. For the purposes of this initial LCA, 60% PDN
efficiency was assumed for COD demand calculations. Assuming a PDN efficiency of 60%, the
COD demand for PdNA then becomes:


To determine the theoretical COD required in the second anoxic zone, a simple nitrogen mass
balance was employed as is shown in Figure A-18. The amount of TN removed in the 2nd zone,
the amount of TN removed through influent COD, and the overall TN removed in the system
was calculated. This mass balance also determined the TINRO parameter used later for scaling
the current energy use between conventional nitrification-denitrification and the PdNA
scenario. TINRO as described by Kester McCullough is the amount of TN removed from influent
COD as a fraction of the total TN removed (McCullough et al. 2022).

Influent TN concentrations for the system were sourced from MPOR data for the Nansemond
plant. All of the incoming TN was assumed to be NH3. Effluent TN concentrations were assumed
from NH3 and NO3 setpoints leaving the system. This was kept the same between both

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 133

scenarios, as effluent quality goals will not change during this expansion. NO3 and NH3
concentrations entering the second anoxic zone in the PdNA scenario were sourced from
BioWin modeling conducted by Hazen and Sawyer for the annual average future flows (current
flows + BH flows), giving both the NH3 concentration and the AvN ratio NH3:NOx entering this
zone. All of the NOx coming into this second anoxic zone was assumed to be NO3. Summer
conditions at 28oC with four of the five tanks in use were assumed to be representative of the
average conditions of the system.

All of the TN not reduced in the 2nd anoxic zone was assumed to be reduced by the influent
COD. While supplemental MeOH is currently added in the 1st anoxic zone, HRSD indicated that
this would be eliminated in their expanded system and was therefore not included in the
theoretical calculations. This 1st anoxic MeOH accounted for about 18.6% of the total MeOH
currently dosed at the Nansemond plant. The full nitrogen mass balance table for these three
major zones can be found in the supplemental tables section A.2.5.

 Figure A-18. Mass Balance of TN in the AvN + PdNA BNR System at Influent, Effluent, and Second Anoxic
 Zone Mass Flow Rates. TINRO Calculated as the Fraction of TN Removed Before the Second Anoxic Zone Out of
 the Total TN Removed in the System. Qe Denotes the Flow Entering into the Second Anoxic Zone.

For the ABAC + DN scenario, A similar method was employed. NH3 and NO3 concentrations
entering the 2nd anoxic zone were calculated based on the AvN BioWin modeled
concentrations, but adjusted to have an ammonia setpoint leaving the aerobic zone of 1 mg-
N/L to be consistent with ABAC control. To achieve this, 3.3 mg-N/L more of the NO3 in the AvN
scenario was assumed to be oxidized in the aerobic zone. After oxidation, some of this NO3
would get sent back however through the IMLR stream. This portion would therefore not enter
the 2nd anoxic zone. The amount of NO3 going into the 2nd anoxic zone taking into account the
portion of additional oxidized NO3 that gets recycled is shown below. This assumes all NO3 that
gets sent back will be reduced in the 1st anoxic zone.

The mass balance discussed for the ABAC scenario is shown in Figure A-19 Again, the TINRO
parameter is calculated here as well. The NO3 removed in the second anoxic zone and therefore
the COD required in this scenario was calculated based on the relevant COD consumption rate
for DN (4.8 g COD/g-N NO3).

 Zone Mass Flow Rates. TINRO Calculated as the Fraction of TN Removed Before the Second Anoxic Zone Out of

Many biological nutrient removal systems require supplemental alkalinity as well. In addition to
decreasing external carbon and electricity use, PdNA reduces the amount of alkalinity that must
be added to the BNR system (McCullough et al. 2022). HRSD’s Nansemond plant, however, has
sufficient alkalinity in the influent water. Supplemental alkalinity as NaOH is not added at the
Nansemond plant. Environmental impacts associated with alkalinity were therefore not a factor
in this analysis.

Energy:
The average daily energy input information is outlined in Table A-14 for the unscaled current
ABAC + DN system, the scaled ABAC + DN system, and the future AvN + PdNA system. This
information was sourced as an average over the last 12 months. Both aeration energy and
pumping energy were scaled to the new flow rate assuming that aeration and pumping scales
linearly with increased flow.

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 135

      Utilized aeration energy           12,007             27,922              24,623           kwh/day

         Pumping energy                   1,727              4,016              4,016            kwh/day

A portion of the aeration energy used at the Nansemond plant is blown off and wasted due to
the size of the blowers. HRSD estimates that about 25% of the energy used between 4am and
9am every morning is the portion of air that is wasted, equating to about 577 kwh/day on
average. It was indicated that this blowing energy inefficiency would be eliminated during the
plant expansion. This portion was therefore not included in the scaled energy in the system.
Pumping energy was assumed to stay constant between each scenario, as similar flows were
assumed between each scenario.

Energy was scaled between current ABAC and AvN scenarios using the mass balances created
by McCullough, that relate the O2 requirements per g of TN removed in the system to the TN
removed by COD in the influent wastewater (TINRO calculated previously). McCullough’s
method is based on a TN mass balance to determine how much of the TN removal can be
attributed to the specified pathway in the 2nd anoxic zone. This is a much more realistic
approach to determining the MeOH and energy reductions that can be achieved through PdNA,
so as not to overestimate the potential savings by assuming all TN is removed via the advanced
pathway. The g-O2/g-N removed for the 76% TINRO ABAC+DN scenario and the 67% TINRO AvN +
PdNA scenario were determined to be 2.14 and 1.89 respectively. The scaling factor was
therefore determined to be about 88% in the AvN + PdNA scenario. The AvN + PdNA aeration
energy use was scaled from the conventional pathway by the ratio of these energy
requirements, assuming linear O2 efficiencies. The scaled energies are seen in the table above.

The electricity generation mix information provided by Dominion, HRSD’s electricity provider is
outlined in the supplemental tables section A.2.5, Representation in Simapro (Dominion Energy
2020). Relevant processes from Ecoinvent for each electricity source in the SERC power grid
were input into an HRSD energy mix process.

MeOH came from the Gulf Coast by freight (for liquid goods). The plastic media will be
imported from China by freight (for dry goods) as well. No packaging was assumed for either
component. Table A-15 shows the distances traveled by each component (NOAA, 2019). Total
transportation was calculated for each product as transported load * distance traveled.
 Table A-15. Transportation Data.

A.2.4 Impact Analysis
LCA Results:
Figures A-20 through A-22 show the impact in each category (global warming potential GWP,
cumulative energy demand CED, freshwater/marine eutrophication FE/ME, and water
footprint) normalized per m3 of treated water. The first column in each graph represents the
current ABAC+DN system with MeOH and energy scaled linearly to the plant expansion flow
rate. The second column indicates the more theoretical ABAC+DN scenario in which MeOH was
calculated using consistent methods to the AvN + PdNA scenario. The final column shows the
impact associated with the theoretical AvN + PdNA scenario. The impact in each is again broken
up by the contributing process. The scenario using actual MeOH information was added to
contextualize the comparison between the two somewhat theoretical comparisons. It will not
be analyzed thoroughly as it doesn’t provide a very relevant comparison to the theoretical AvN
+ PdNA scenario. It is useful however to see how much could be saved if MeOH addition in the
system is fully optimized to theoretical values.

In all scenarios, a ~12% and ~45% decrease in aeration energy and MeOH was calculated
between the ABAC+DN and AvN + PdNA scenarios. Energy for pumping and effluent TN
concentrations stayed the same between each scenario. Changes in construction impact
(concrete and media/media shipping) changed between each scenario, exhibiting an increase in
all categories. The overall difference in impact between each scenario therefore depended on
the relative impact between each category. For global warming potential, a 16% overall
decrease was exhibited between the ABAC+DN and AvN + PdNA scenarios. New reactor
concrete impacts were ~25% of the comparative media and media shipping impacts. For
cumulative energy demand this was an 18% decrease in overall impact. Concrete amounted to
3% of the media and shipping impact.

  Figure A-20. LCA Results for Global Warming Potential (GWP) and Cumulative Energy Demand (CED) in Each
 Scenario By Process Contribution, Normalized Per m3 of Treated Water. *Current Operation is ABAC + DN with
 Actual MeOH Use Instead of the More Comparative Theoretical MeOH Use.

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 137

Less than 0.1% decrease was exhibited in eutrophication impacts, mainly due to the dominating
impact of the plant TN effluent and TP effluent. It should be noted that in ReCiPe 2016
methodology, P is assumed to be limited in freshwaters while N is limited in marine waters
(Morelli et al. 2018). NO3 and NH3 effluent therefore only affect marine eutrophication impacts.
Marine eutrophication was almost completely (99.7%) dominated by effluent TN. Concrete
made up 11% and 12% of FE and ME media/media shipping impacts respectively. A 13%
decrease in water footprint was exhibited and concrete was about 17% of comparative
media/media shipping impacts.

Figure A-21. LCA Results for Freshwater Eutrophication (FE) and Marine Eutrophication (ME) in Each Scenario by
 Process Contribution, Normalized Per m3 of Treated Water.

138 The Water Research Foundation

 Figure A-22. LCA Results for Water Footprint (WF) in Each Scenario by Process Contribution, Normalized Per m3
 of Treated Water.

In the other four impact categories, energy for aeration was the dominant contributing process.
Because of this, the overall percent decrease in environmental impact was closer to the 12%
decrease exhibited by energy savings than the 45% decrease exhibited by MeOH. Impacts
associated with construction in the AvN + PdNA scenario (media and media shipping) were
higher than those in the ABAC+DN scenario (concrete for the reactor expansion) across the
board. This was heavily outweighed however by the reduction in operational impacts
associated with energy and MeOH savings exhibited through AvN + PdNA. In all scenarios,
concrete made up less than 1% of the total impact and media made up less than 5%, and media
shipping made up less than 0.3%. Plastic media has a density of around 130 kg/m3. This is less
than the 250 kg/m3 threshold and is therefore considered volume limited transport. Because of
the ton*km input of Ecoinvent shipping factors, this parameter therefore may be slightly
underestimated. It was assumed that because the media only gets shipped every ~10 years by
sea freight that this difference would make a negligible difference. Because the shipping made
up such a small fraction of the overall impact of the system, this assumption is valid.

Figure A-23 shows the breakdown of the current ABAC+DN system scaled to the future flow for
each environmental impact broken up by process contribution. Again, in all but eutrophication
impacts, aeration energy was the dominant contributor. While relatively minor, 1st anoxic
MeOH still contributed 6 to 8% of the total impact in the remaining four categories. Concrete
impacts were <1% across all impact categories.

TEA Results:
A simple TEA was also conducted based on OPEX and CAPEX cost associated with both
scenarios. Savings associated with potential capacity reductions was the largest difference
between the two scenarios. OPEX savings associated with MeOH and Electricity were also
substantial. The CAPEX cost of the basin was normalized by dividing by the water treated in the
basin’s expected lifetime of 50 years.

A.3 Application 3 – PAA Disinfection
A.3.1 Overview of Application
Goal: To assess the environmental impact of PAA (peracetic acid) disinfection at Metro Water
Recovery’s Robert W. Hite Treatment Facility (RWHTF) with flow-based control (2018-2019) and
CT based control (2020-2021). To also compare this with the project end goal PAA disinfection
target.

  Figure A-26. Environmental Impacts for the Baseline Flow Based PAA Dosing, with CT Based PAA Dosing, and
 the Project Goal Dose. Results Are Presented as a Percentage of The Maximum Impact in Each Category.

 Table A-26. Overall Environmental Impacts Per m3 of Treated Water in Each Scenario.
 Impact Category Method Unit PAA with FB PAA with CT Goal PAA
                                                              (18/19)          (20/21)
           Global Warming              ReCiPe    kg CO2       0.0142           0.0164            0.0114
 eq
 Freshwater Eutrophication ReCiPe kg P eq 7.36E-06 8.52E-06 5.90E-06
 Marine Eutrophication ReCiPe kg N eq 5.71E-07 6.61E-07 4.58E-07
              Total CED                CED        Kwh         0.0847            0.0981          0.0680
 Water Footprint AWARE m3
                                                              0.0174            0.0202          0.0140

The lowest impact across all impact categories was the goal PAA scenario followed by the flow
based PAA dosing scenario period. Many factors influence disinfectant dosing beyond the
dosing control scheme. Metro Water Recovery (Metro) hypothesized for example a possible
worsening in water quality between the period of flow based (2018/2019) and CT based
(2020/2021) control scenarios. Further, PAA is desirable for many other reasons beyond the
sustainability implications presented here; The most prominent of these benefits is that PAA is
safer to handle than alternative disinfection chemicals and it reduces the formation of harmful

150 The Water Research Foundation

disinfection by-products. More advanced dosing mechanisms are imperative to feasibly and
reliably transition to this newer disinfectant option.

A.3.2 Goal and Scope

This project is Application #3 - Peracetic acid (PAA) disinfection as a part of the broader DOE
project: Integration of data-driven process control for maximization of energy and resource
efficiency in advanced water resource recovery facilities (WRRFs).

Disinfection with peracetic acid (PAA) is a relatively novel WRRF alternative that reduces the
potential for harmful disinfection byproduct (DBP) formation. Metro is one of the first utilities
in the U.S. to adopt this technology and study it at full scale at RWHTF. PAA disinfection kinetics
however are harder to monitor and optimize at full scale. This often leads to excessive dosing to
ensure effluent quality goals are met. This is a major consideration in WRRF sustainability, as
there are many environmental impacts associated with the manufacturing and transportation
of large quantities of chemicals such as PAA. The environmental sustainability implications of
the scenarios were analyzed through LCA for Metro’s RWHTF plant disinfection system.

Flow-Based PAA disinfection:
This scenario investigated the period (2018-2019) during which flow-based modeling was used
to inform PAA dosing decisions at RWHTF. In flow-based modeling, dosing was adjusted based
on fluctuations in the incoming flow and a PAA setpoint was controlled by the operator.

CT Based PAA disinfection:
This scenario investigated the period (2020-2021) during which CT-based dosing was first
implemented at RWHTF. CT can be most simply understood as the product of the residual
concentration in the tank times the contact time of the residual in the tank. Since the residual
in the tank decays over time, this equates to the integral of the decay curve over the contact
time. In the original CT dosing, the system calculated the dose required based on decay rate lab
tests, an online PAA analyzer reading to calculate demand, and the total time in the tank. It
then adjusted the dose of PAA based on these parameters to meet an operator set CT setpoint.

Goal PAA disinfection:
In this project, Metro was working to incorporate better controls through data-driven models
that offer more precise and real-time control of PAA dosing. This scenario investigated the goal
PAA dose as indicated in the project proposal. PAA dose is affected by a large number of factors
beyond the dosing control scheme. Changes in water quality for example between each period
play a large role in the PAA dose required. The goal is more so to reliably and accurately dose
PAA relative to the PAA demand. An actual decrease in PAA use within the periods analyzed
may be more dependent on the incoming water quality.

LCA Objectives:
● To assess the comparative environmental impact of Metro’s RWHTF PAA disinfection
 system with the original flow-based and current CT-based dosing.

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 151

● To identify which life cycle stages of the disinfection process have the largest environmental
 impact to see where additional optimizations to the system could be made.

various treatment capacities, the results were normalized by the volume treated in the system.
Therefore, the functional basis for this LCA was one cubic meter of wastewater treated by the
plant. Presenting these results per cubic meter of treated water also provided a more
interpretable metric relevant to plant operation. Impacts were calculated for a year of
operation. These results were then divided by the volume of water treated in this timespan.

Construction was not a factor within the system boundaries for this application. Although
sensors are used during operation, their construction is not considered in the LCA. This analysis
included the operational phase, focusing on processes impacted by the improvements in PAA
dosing. It also included the material acquisition of the affected chemicals including their
transportation and packaging. Operation of the chemical factories and associated
heat/electricity use in the disinfectant manufacturing was a factor in our analysis. The system
was cut off at the infrastructure of the chemical plant itself.

The system diagram showing the unit processes included in the boundary of the system can be
seen in Figure A-27 for PAA disinfection. The system boundary including all of the upstream
impacts for the manufacturing, packaging, and transportation of PAA to the Metro facility.

 Figure A-27. System Boundary for LCA of the RWHTF PAA Disinfection System.

152 The Water Research Foundation

A.3.3 Inventory Analysis

Data was collected from Metro on RWHTF operation. The main metrics for this analysis were
PAA dose and flow rate in each scenario. The chemical composition of the 15% PAA solution
was calculated based on the SDS safety sheet provided by Metro for PAA. A similar LCA
conducted on the comparative use of NaOCl, PAA, and PFA by Elena Penkarski Rodon of McGill
University, Montreal was used to confirm solution inputs as described in Figure A-36 of section
A.3.5, Supplemental Tables and Figures (Rodon 2017). The catalyst and energy input for PAA in
this analysis were also sourced from Rodon’s study.

Material inputs and processes used in constructing the 330 gallon intermediate bulk container
(IBC) transport totes that hold the PAA solution were sourced from an EY LCA on reconditioned
vs. newly constructed industrial packaging (Rietveld and Hegger 2014). Background processes
were sourced from the Ecoinvent and on occasion USLCI databases.

TEA/LCA Data Inputs:
Both a TEA and LCA were conducted in parallel to analyze the economic and environmental
sustainability impacts of the system. Much of the input data for the TEA model overlapped with
the necessary information from the LCA model. Input information for the foreground system of
this analysis was divided into three main categories: treatment basis, chemical use, and
transportation. Figure A-28 shows the two models working in parallel to take in the foreground
inputs and generate the environmental and economic impact areas of focus.

The goal PAA dose scenario was analyzed at the overall 2018-2021 flow rate. Using the dose
and flow rate information, the annual disinfectant mass was calculated for PAA in each
scenario. The solution mass (including the weight of water/solvents) and solution volume were
calculated as well, based on the % w/w and density of each solution. These values can be found
in Table A-35 of section A.3.5, Supplemental Tables and Figures.

Residual PAA was not a factor in our analysis because PAA readily degrades in water and is not
expected to persist.

Because this application is primarily a chemical dosing analysis, a particular focus was given to
confirming dosing inputs. An independent check of the PAA annual mass was calculated to
ensure accurate and realistic results. This independent check was based on the most recent
RWHTF PAA delivery information. The annual mass of solution calculated from this delivery
information was within ~20% of the calculated annual mass based on the 2020-2021 average
dose and flow rate. This can be found in Table A-35 of section A.3.5, Supplemental Tables and
Figures.

It should be noted that many factors affect PAA dose beyond just the control scheme utilized to
dose the disinfectant. The dose required for PAA may be controlled by the variable water

154 The Water Research Foundation

quality in the plant influent that contributes to the PAA demand. Using annual averages to
inform differences between each scenario therefore may not reflect actual improvements
between control schemes. The instantaneous PAA demand of the wastewater entering the
disinfectant chamber was measured at RWHTF starting in 2020. Figure A-29 shows the variation
in instantaneous demand as 24 hour averages for each day analyzed over the past ~three years
of recorded data. Observations with a negative average daily demand were set to 0, as a
negative demand is not realistic (3 observations in each year).

It is difficult to ascertain how much of the actual variability during our periods of study can be
related to water quality fluctuation, as this data was not present for the same time period.
Various methods were used however to get an idea for how demand varied between each year
analyzed to see if demand between years is relatively consistent. The median PAA demand
between each year recorded shifted from ~0.97 mg/L in 2020, 0.87 mg/L in 2021, and 1.004
mg/L in 2022. Within each year, high variability can also be seen with an overall IQR of 0.321
mg/L. Unlike E Coli concentration, PAA demand does not appear to be highly correlated with
time. Therefore, while this is time-series data for the same measurement over time, a non-
parametric Kruskal-Wallis test was utilized. A test statistic (df = 2) of 87.108 and a p value of
1.2157e-19 was determined. This indicates that at least one of the analyzed years was different
enough to test as coming from a different population/scenario. Analyzing PAA between the
years in this study may therefore not be just dependent on the control scheme but variability in
actual PAA demand itself.

 Figure A-29. Instantaneous PAA Demand Reported as 24 Hour Averages for 2020, 2021 and 2022. The Median,
 IQR, Number of Observations, and Range Excluding Outliers Is Reported for Each Year. High Variability Is
Exhibited Between Each Year and Within Each Year Analyzed as Well. Note that Variation in Points Along X-Axis
 for Each Year Is for Visualization Only, Variation in Sample Points Is Shown Along Y-Axis Only.

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 155

As indicated by Metro, a full delivery includes 16 totes each with a capacity of 330 gallons. The
totes were assumed to be newly manufactured. The weight of each tote is 150 pounds. The
total delivery load for each trip for both the weight of the solution and the totes themselves is
therefore about 23.87 tons for PAA. Transportation values were input for this process as the
mass of load carried times the distance traveled for a unit of ton*km = tkm. Because
transportation was input on a load*distance basis and because the total load of each trip was
greater than the average load factor, a portion of the return trip of the empty container was
inherently accounted for in the Ecoinvent process methodology (Spielmann 2007). This average
loading was assumed to be consistent with our analysis even though only half of the totes (8
totes) in each delivery are attributed to the analyzed north treatment train.

Distance traveled by PAA was sourced from Envirotech, Metro’s PAA supplier located in Helena,
AK at 1740 km away. Trucking distance was based on delivery from this location.

LCA & TEA Results:

flow-based PAA dosing (period 18/19), the current scenario with CT based PAA dosing (period
20/21), and the goal scenario. In Figure A-30 through A-33, in all impact categories it can be
seen that the goal PAA dose is lowest, reflecting the potential reduction in PAA use. The CT
dose initial data show a higher impact in all categories compared to the flow based dose,
however WQ during each period should also be considered.

smallest contribution. Marine eutrophication had the highest percent contribution from the

manufactured instead of reused. Considering the relatively small contribution from these totes,
this assumption is minor. Using reusable containers would reduce impact especially in marine
eutrophication, but by a relatively small amount. Transportation was also relatively small
compared to the actual disinfection process in all categories as well.

Annual Calculations and Confirmation:
The annual mass of disinfectant required based on the dose and associated flow rates in each
scenario are shown below. PAA was input as 15% solutions by mass. The annual solution mass
is therefore calculated by dividing the disinfectant mass required in each scenario by 15%. An
example annual mass calculation for the 2020-2022 time period is below:

2.08 3
             ∗ 3785      ∗ 66.71𝑀𝑀𝑀𝑀𝑀𝑀 ∗ 365         ∗                        = 1.27𝐸𝐸6

Volume of solution in each case was determined from the respective annual solution masses
and densities. The density of the 15% PAA solution was calculated to be 4.314 kg/gallon. The
tkm of transport in each scenario was the distance traveled times the mass of solution needed
each year.

Metro’s most recent delivery schedule from January to April of 2022 equates to a delivery
frequency of 140 deliveries per year. Assuming 8 totes (330 gallons each) per delivery for PAA
to the North side treatment train at RWHTF, the mass of PAA solution was calculated based on
this information as a confirmation of our previously used method to calculate the annual
solution mass needed.
  140                         ∗8                  ∗ 330                ∗ 4.314               = 1.59𝐸𝐸6

This is about 20% higher than the 2020-2022 solution mass calculated using the method
outlined above. This was determined to be an acceptable difference to confirm the method
used in this study.

A.4 Application 4 - P-Recovery - MagPrex® LCA
A.4.1 Overview of Application
Goal: To assess the comparative environmental impact of the post-digestion solids handling
system at Metro Water Recovery (Metro) Robert W. Hite Treatment Facility (RWHTF) before
CNP’s MagPrex® P-recovery was online, with initial MagPrex® operation, and with optimized
MagPrex® operation.

Figure A-37. Environmental Impacts for the Baseline Scenario with MagPrex® Offline and the Current Scenario
with MagPrex® Online. Results Presented as a Percentage of the Baseline Impact in Each Category.

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 165

 Table A-37. Overall Environmental Impacts Per m3 of Treated Water in Each Scenario.
 Impact Category Method Unit Before MagPrex® Initial MagPrex® Optimized
                                                           (2015-2020)         (2021-2022)              MagPrex®
                                                                                                     (June-July 2024)

Freshwater Eutrophication ReCiPe kg P eq 9.47E-06 8.79E-06 7.04E-06

        Total CED             CED            Kwh             0.1181                0.1128                 0.0944
     Water Footprint         AWARE            m3             0.0064                0.0071                 0.0056

  The solids treatment system at Denver Metro Water Recovery’s (Metro’s) Robert W. Hite
  Treatment Facility (RWHTF) was compared before MagPrex® (historical data 2015-2020), with
  MagPrex® initially implemented (2020-2021), and during the first two months of MagPrex®
  optimization with the advanced control online. In nearly all environmental impact categories
  analyzed in the LCA, the transition from the baseline scenario (without MagPrex®) to the initial
  operational scenario (with MagPrex® online) resulted in modest reduction in all categories.
  Optimization of the MagPrex® process further reduced each impact category, particularly for
  water deprivation. It should be noted that the optimized MagPrex® scenario operational data
  was from a limited data set during the first two months of optimized control implementation. It
  should also be noted that in addition to the advanced data driven process controls optimized as
  a part of this project, other traditional optimization efforts were also implemented for
  MagPrex® during the time period of this study. These include reductions in dewatering
  polymer, MgCl2 use in the MagPrex® system, as well as increase in struvite recovery. Controlled
  precipitation of struvite through MagPrex® also reduces harmful nuisance struvite build-up in
  RWHTF infrastructure (Wisdom et al. 2017).

  Of all the optimized parameters, the savings in MgCl2 were the most directly related to the
  optimization through data driven process controls. The other optimized parameters were still
  included in this analysis for contextualization of the impacts of the MagPrex® system. The
  reduction in MgCl2 specifically lead to an approximate savings of $48,000 annually in the TEA,
  or $0.0003/m3 product water normalized to mainstream treatment flow. The OPEX savings
  associated with MgCl2 made up 7% of the total OPEX reductions with MagPrex® optimization
  compared to the initial MagPrex® OPEX TEA. It also made up to 8% of the reductions in the LCA
  impact categories (the highest proportion in the water deprivation impact, and lowest in the
  eutrophication impacts).

  A.4.2 Goal and Scope

  This project is application #4 - P recovery through MagPrex®, as a part of the broader DOE
  project: Integration of data-driven process control for maximization of energy and resource
  efficiency in advanced water resource recovery facilities (WRRFs). The environmental
  sustainability implications of post-digestion P-recovery through MagPrex® were analyzed
  through LCA.

  166 The Water Research Foundation

Baseline without MagPrex®:
In the baseline scenario analyzed here, the post-digestion solids handling system at RWHTF was
analyzed for the period when MagPrex® P recovery was not yet online (historical data from
2015-2020). The RWHTF has two secondary treatment process, each receiving approximately
50% of the flow. The north side of the facility operated in an MLE (modified ludzack-ettinger)
configuration with a sidestream anaerobic reactor. While the south side of the facility operated
in an anaerobic-anoxic-oxic (A2O) configuration. Both of these processes enabled enhanced
biological phosphorous removal (EBPR), which removed soluble phosphorus from the
secondary process. While EBPR is necessary for meeting stringent effluent quality goals, it has
had many detrimental effects on the solids system due to the release of phosphorus in
anaerobic conditions. These impacts include but are not limited to: the build up of damaging
nuisance struvite in RWHTF infrastructure, recycling of phosphorus in the system from
dewatering reject water, and hindrance of biosolid dewaterability (Wisdom et al. 2017). Thus,
intentionally precipitating phosphorus as struvite in a controlled reactor has the potential to
reduce or even prevent many of the common consequences associated with EBPR.

Current operation with MagPrex®:
In the current operational scenario, the solids handling system was analyzed for the initial
period with the MagPrex® unit online (recent operational data from 2021-2022). MagPrex®
precipitates phosphorus as struvite in a controlled setting through the addition of magnesium
chloride (limiting reagent) and an increase in pH via air stripping (increases reaction rate).
Struvite can then be harvested from the reactor or captured in the biosolids during dewatering.
Major impacts of the MagPrex® system on RWHTF are outlined in Figure A-38 below, with those
captured in our analysis highlighted in blue.

 Figure A-38. Effects of MagPrex® Captured in this Analysis Are Highlighted in Blue and Include Decreased
Polymer Demand, Decreased Wet Ton Cake Production, Increased Struvite P Recovery, and Decreased Recycle of

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 167

  OP Leading to Decreased MgCl2 Use. Other Impacts May Include Decreased Nuisance Struvite, Decreased OP
Reject, and Increased Dry Solids/Phosphorus in the Biosolid. Note that Only Relevant Processes Within the Plant
 Were Included in this Diagram for Simplicity.

A major benefit of MagPrex® operation is improved dewaterability of the biosolid, reducing the
polymer demanded per pound of TS. These effects are best explained by a reduction in soluble
orthophosphate which binds to and inhibits the dewatering cationic polymer’s ability to
stabilize and flocculate the sludge. Another explanation of this would be the added Mg2+ which
stabilizes the biosolid through the divalent cation bridging theory much like the cationic
polymer added for dewatering (Bergmans et al. 2014). In addition to a decrease in polymer
requirements, these effects also decrease the bound water within the biosolids and therefore
the mass of biosolid cake that needs to be transported from RWHTF each day. Reduction in
internally recycled phosphorus in the dewatering centrate also reduces the OP that recycles
back through the MagPrex® reactor. Improvements to P removal therefore decreases required
MgCl2 dose, which is consistently dosed in the MagPrex® reactor at a ratio of 1.3:1 Mg:P. Given
the discussed role of Mg²⁺ in enhancing cake dryness, future work could correlate residual Mg²⁺
concentration to reductions in dewatering polymer requirements. Additionally, an evaluation of
potential negative impacts, such as downstream struvite precipitation due to residual Mg²⁺, will
also need to be quantified. For this study, the 1.3:1 setpoint used at RHWTF was used.

Beyond operational benefits, MagPrex® also provides a value-added product from its operation.
The harvesting of struvite P separate from the biosolid cake provides a more “clean” and
valuable agricultural product.

LCA Objectives:
• To assess the environmental impact of P-recovery on the post-digestion solids handling
 system at RWHTF, utilizing historical and current data from before and after MagPrex®
 installation, as well as data from the initial period with optimized controls online.
• To assess the environmental impact between MagPrex® current and optimized operation to
 illuminate what sustainability benefits were achieved through optimization efforts.
• To identify which process contributions of the post-digestion solids handling process have
 the largest environmental impacts, enabling the identification of opportunities for further
 optimization.

various treatment capacities, the results were normalized by the volume of water treated in the
system. Therefore, the functional basis for this LCA was one cubic meter of treated water within
the plant. Presenting these results per cubic meter of treated water also provides a more
interpretable metric relevant to plant operation. Impacts were annualized to a year of
operation. These results were then divided by the volume of solids treated by RWHTF system in
a year.

Other applicable functional units could have included “m3 of solids handled” by the post-
digestion solids system, or “kg of struvite recovered”. The overall treated water unit was

168 The Water Research Foundation

selected to be consistent with the other applications on this project and for ease of
comparison. Results are however presented in Table A-44 of section A.4.5 Supplemental Tables
and Figures normalized both per m3 treated water and per m3 of treated post-digestion solids.
The functional unit of kg of struvite recovered was not selected because struvite recovery is an
added benefit rather than the main goal/product of this system. Most of the struvite that is
precipitated is also recovered through the biosolid rather than as a separate struvite product,
making this a difficult functional unit to interpret.

This analysis included the operational phase of the system, specifically focusing on processes
impacted by MagPrex®. It also included the material acquisition of relevant chemicals including
their transportation. The downstream dewatering process was included in the system
boundaries, as the MagPrex® unit had a significant impact on dewatering performance.

The construction, demolition, and maintenance phases of the MagPrex® unit were not a factor
in the analysis. Processes upstream of the MagPrex® system and downstream of the returned
centrate were not a factor as well. Impacts associated with the recovered struvite were
included as a credit in the system boundaries, stopping at the delivery to A1 Organics and use in
their products. Transportation impacts associated with biosolid hauling were included, but the
system boundary did not include the biosolid product as a fertilizer offset. The system diagram
showing the processes included in the boundary of the system can be seen in Figure A-39.

To focus solely on the MagPrex® operation itself between current and optimized performance,
a second case was analyzed cutting the system boundaries off at the downstream dewatering
process. This secondary analysis helped emphasize the environmental benefits of the MagPrex®
optimization itself. This secondary boundary can be seen in Figure A-39 as the dotted-dashed
purple line.

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 169

Figure A-39. The LCA System Boundary for RWHTF’s Post-Digestion Solids Handling System Focuses on Processes
Impacted by MagPrex® Installation. This Boundary Is Outlined with the Dashed Black Line. The System Boundary
  for the Secondary Analysis Focusing on Just the MagPrex® Reactor Is Outlined with the Purple Dotted-Dashed
 Line.

A.4.3 Inventory Analysis

The majority of this analysis was conducted based on information provided by Metro on the
operation of their plant before and after MagPrex® was installed in 2020. Chemical
transportation distances were determined using the information provided by Metro for their
chemical manufacturers. Product distances were determined from the location of Metro’s
METROGRO farm for the biosolid, and from the location of the A1 Organics facility for their
struvite product. Electricity generation information was sourced from the US EIA for the state of
Colorado. MgCl2 information was based on a study by LaLonde 2019 on MgCl2 as a de-icer, and
from the magnesium chloride producer sustainability report referenced in this study. Finally,
the main database for all background process impacts in Simapro was the Ecoinvent database.

TEA/LCA Data Inputs:
Both a TEA and LCA were conducted in parallel to analyze the system's economic and
environmental sustainability impacts. Much of the input data for the TEA model overlapped

170 The Water Research Foundation

with the necessary information for the LCA model. Input information for the foreground system
of this analysis was divided into four main categories: treatment basis, energy basis, chemical
basis, and transportation. Figure A-40 shows the two models working in parallel to take in the
foreground inputs and generate the environmental and economic impact areas of focus.

 Figure A-40. Data Flow Overview of Main Parameters with Parallel LCA and TEA Models.

Table A-38 shows unit cost information for relevant TEA inputs. Struvite is not currently sold yet
by Metro. The product is provided to A1 Organics, where it is integrated into their compost,
adding valuable nitrogen and phosphorus supplements. As such, struvite was not yet included
in the TEA. This input information was relayed to NETL for inclusion in the WaterTAP3
application.

Chemical and Treatment Basis:
Flow rate to the MagPrex® unit from anaerobic digestion fluctuates usually between 400-800
gpm with peaks at 1200 gpm. During the Pre-MagPrex® period, the average post-digestion flow
rate was 627 GPM and Post-MagPrex® was 606 GPM. The overall plant flow rate over the
analyzed period was 131 MGD.

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 171

 The MgCl2 input information provided by Metro is outlined in Table A-39. The annual
 magnesium dose required for the initial MagPrex® operation was calculated based on the
 average gallons per min fed to the MagPrex® system. Based on a ratio of the current influent
 OP and the optimized influent OP, MgCl2 flow rate was extrapolated. This ratio was used to
 determine MgCl2 flow rate because Mg2+ is currently dosed at a consistent 1.3:1 MG:P ratio in
 the MagPrex® reactor based on the MagPrex® influent OP.
 Table A-39. MgCl2 Information.
 Input Before MagPrex® Initial Optimized Unit
                                    (2015-2020)         MagPrex®           MagPrex®
                                                       (2021-2022)      (June-July 2024)
 MagPrex® influent OP N/A ~350 ~330 mg-P/L
 Mg Solution flow rate – 1.98 1.86 GPM

 MgCl2 was not a defined process in the Ecoinvent or USLCI databases. As such, the process was
 constructed using inputs from Compass Mineral’s 2016 sustainability report on MgCl2
 production (Compass Minerals, 2016). Compass Minerals sources their MgCl2 from the Great
 Salt Lake and uses similar production methods as Envirotech, the MgCl2 supplier for Metro
 (LaLonde 2019). Specific fuel and electricity use were sourced from this report as inputs for
 MgCl2. The direct emissions from MgCl2 production as well as the consumed (and not recycled)
 freshwater intensity were included as well. Other P-recovery studies often use proxies to MgCl2,
 such as the study by Sena et al. 2021 which inputs the MgCl2 process based on the
 carbochlorination of MgO and water content as described by Igos et al. 2017. This is a much
 more intensive process than the mining of MgCl2 from the Great Salt Lake which relies primarily
 on solar evaporation for the drying of their products. A comparison of MgCl2 options is
 described in Figure A-56 of section A.4.5 Supplemental Tables and Figures.

 The polymer information provided by Metro is outlined below. The average daily solids load for
 the period between 2021-2022 was not estimated using traditional TS measurements. The
 traditional TS method was found to vaporize the crystallized water and NH4 in the struvite solid.
 Because RWHTF solids contain a large amount of struvite, the traditional TS did not capture all
 of the cake total solids (Avila et al. 2021). A lower temperature measurement was therefore
 used to more accurately estimate the TS, and a correction was applied as described in Avila’s
 work. The historical data before MagPrex® installation did not encounter this same problem, as
 struvite was not purposefully precipitated in this scenario. No correction was therefore needed
 for this measurement.

 172 The Water Research Foundation

Struvite and Biosolid Offset:
Transportation of the biosolid and struvite product were both included within the system
boundaries, as these are greatly affected by the performance of the MagPrex® unit. Application
of the struvite and biosolid including the associated agricultural P and N emissions to soil were
not included within the bounds, as the focus of this LCA was on actual plant operation. The
cutoff of the system was therefore at the transportation of each product to their respective
facilities.

While the LCA conducted was an attributional LCA, an equivalent inorganic rock phosphate
fertilizer credit was applied for the struvite harvested from the MagPrex® unit through system
expansion using the avoided-burden method (Thomassen et al. 2008). This represents the
fertilizer production at A1 Organics that is replaced by use of the struvite. Including this credit
to the system emphasizes the reframing of WRRFs not just as nutrient removal systems, but as
nutrient recovery systems. This is especially critical for nutrients such as phosphorus, as there is
a finite supply of inorganic rock phosphorus. Because the focus of this LCA was on phosphorus
recovery and mass flow in the system, only phosphorus was included as a fertilizer nutrient.
Nitrogen content of the struvite was not a factor in this LCA.

Struvite information is shown in Table A-41 below for the initial and optimized MagPrex®
operation. This available content was then equated to the equivalent fertilizer offset in kg-P2O5.
The inclusion of the struvite product as a fertilizer offset was modeled after a study conducted
by Sena et. al 2021. In this study, struvite was equated to 0-48-0 triple superphosphate (also in
kg-P2O5) assuming 48% of this phosphorus fertilizer is P content by mass (Sena et al. 2021).
 Table A-41. Struvite P Information.
 Input Before MagPrex® (2015- Initial Optimized Unit
 2020) MagPrex® MagPrex®
                                                         (2021-2022)         (June-July 2024)
 Struvite daily recovery – 281 939 lbs/day
 Struvite P content – 0.109 0.109 g-P/g struvite
Daily struvite P recovery – 14 46 Kg-P/day

It should be noted that the biosolids P content did improve with MagPrex® operation from 26.1
to 33.7 mg-P/g of dry solids. A large portion of the struvite precipitated by the MagPrex® unit is
contained within the biosolid cake which is land applied at Metro’s METROGRO farm. While the
nutrient content of this product is important, the biosolid phosphorus content was not factored
into this analysis for two main reasons. System expansion through the avoided burden of
fertilizer must decrease demand for fertilizer production in the market (Thomassen et al. 2008).
This is much more clear cut with the direct replacement of A1 organic fertilizer products at their
facility than it is with the land application of the biosolid as a fertilizer at METROGRO farm.
Further, consideration of the P to N ratio is paramount to creating a balanced biosolid for crops
with large nitrogen requirements (Wisdom et al. 2017). One component of the MagPrex®
operation efforts is focused on shifting the fraction of recovered struvite from the biosolid to
the MagPrex® struvite product. Including a fertilizer credit only for the P content of the
MagPrex® product therefore emphasizes the efforts made towards this goal.

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 173

Energy Basis:
Energy usage information was provided by Metro as 618 kwh/day to provide 600 scfm to the
MagPrex® reactor for initial operation. While the airflow currently lies at around 1200 scfm
now, Airflow to MagPrex® was tested in a range from 500 scfm to 1200 scfm with minimal
differences in performance of the system. Electricity was therefore assumed not to decrease
between the current MagPrex® operation and the optimized scenario. Metro’s current goals for
MagPrex® operation focus more on MgCl2 use and struvite harvesting.

The electricity generation mix information for Metro was sourced from the Colorado electricity
mix from March of 2022 as shown in Table A-44 of section A.4.5 Supplemental Tables and
Figures (U.S. EIA 2022). Relevant processes from Ecoinvent for each electricity source in the
WECC power grid were input into the Metro energy mix process.

Transportation options from the Ecoinvent database are described in the overall LCA
framework report. Table A-42 shows distances traveled by MgCl2 and dewatering polymer from
the chemical manufacturers to Metro. It also shows the distance traveled of the struvite
product from Metro to A1 Organics.
 Table A-42. PAA Transportation.
 Parameter MgCl2 Polymer Struvite
      distance traveled (km)        885                    1448                         8.5
 Location Rowley, UT Bloomington, MN A1 Organics facility

From the distances traveled by each relevant product and from the annual mass of each
chemical/product transported to and from Metro each year, transportation values were
calculated in tkm/year.

MgCl2 is transported to RWHTF as a bulk liquid solution by rail in reusable tanker trailers.
Dewatering polymer comes in plastic flexible 750 kg intermediate bulk container (FIBC) bags by
freight truck. The pallets used to transport these bags were assumed reusable and were
therefore not a factor. The FIBC bag was input as a process using Ecoinvent polypropylene and
electricity as shown in Table A-44 of section A.4.5 Supplemental Tables and Figures. This input
information was described in a packaging LCA by Durao and Corbion (Durao 2021).

Biosolid transportation to the METROGRO farm was input based on the biosolid trucking
delivery schedule provided by Metro. The average truck load in each period was calculated by
dividing the daily average wet tons of cake produced by the daily average number of trucks.
This was then multiplied by half of the total distance traveled each day by all trucks to calculate
the transportation of the biosolid to the METROGRO farm in km. The return trips were
accounted for in the inherent Ecoinvent process because of the inclusion of a portion of road
and truck infrastructure (Spielmann 2007). These calculations are presented in Table A-52 of
section A.4.5 Supplemental Tables and Figures.

174 The Water Research Foundation

A.4.4 Impact Analysis
Solids Handling LCA Results:
The following section presents the results of this LCA normalized per m3 of treated wastewater.
As is shown in Figure A-41, global warming potential (GWP) and cumulative energy demand
(CED) slightly decreased between the baseline scenario without MagPrex® to the initial scenario
MagPrex® online. MagPrex® optimization further decreased both impacts. Decreases in impact
are associated with polymer use (including transport + packaging), biosolid haul trucking, and
the offset from struvite. Increases in impact with MagPrex® online are associated with MgCl2,
MgCl2 and struvite product trucking, and electricity for MagPrex® blowers. In both impact
categories, the dewatering polymer was the main contributor to the overall impact score.

Figure A-41. LCA Results for Global Warming Potential (GWP, Left) and Cumulative Energy Demand (CED, Right)
 in Each Scenario by Process Contribution, Normalized Per m3 of Treated Water.

Figure A-42. shows similar results for freshwater and marine eutrophication to what was
exhibited in global warming potential and cumulative energy demand. An even more drastic
decrease between pre and post-MagPrex® scenarios however were exhibited in these
categories. Just as for GWP and CED, the major decreases between scenarios is attributed
towards reduced dewatering polymer usage of the system at large.

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 175

Figure A-42. LCA Results for Freshwater Eutrophication (FE, Right) and Marine Eutrophication (ME, Left) in Each
 Scenario by Process Contribution, Normalized Per m3 Of Treated Water.

Figure A-43 shows that water footprint actually increased from before MagPrex® operation to
the initial MagPrex® operation, due to the relatively large water footprint of MgCl2
manufacturing. The optimized MagPrex® however was lower.

 Figure A-43. LCA Results for Water Footprint (WF) in Each Scenario by Process Contribution,
 Normalized Per m3 of Treated Water.

176 The Water Research Foundation

Figure A-44 shows the percent contribution of each impact as a percentage of the total impact
in each category for the current MagPrex® operation. The total impact included only positive
contributing contributions and not the credit applied for the recovered struvite so as to add up
to 100%. The relative percentage of impact the struvite could counter was then determined as
shown in Figure A-44.

The impact associated with the MagPrex® reactor itself was relatively small compared to the
impacts of the downstream processes it affects. Downstream polymer impacts (including
packaging and transport) and biosolid transportation from dewatering made up the majority of
the total impact score in each category.

The impact associated with air stripping in the MagPrex® reactor made up at most about ~3% of
the total impact, validating the assumption that MagPrex® electricity use is a relatively minor
focus of this work. The assumption of no change in air stripping energy between this case and
the goal case is also then relatively minor.

 Figure A-44. Process Contribution Breakdown as a Relative Percentage of Total Impact in Each Impact Category
 (Not Including Struvite Offset). Impacts Making Up Less than 0.5% of the Total Impact Were Not Shown.

MagPrex® focused LCA Results:
Focusing now on just the operation of the MagPrex® unit, impacts associated with MgCl2, MgCl2
transportation, electricity for MagPrex® CO2 air stripping, and struvite transportation/credit
were analyzed for the current operation and for the theoretical operation with better MgCl2
use and struvite harvesting. As is expected, Figures A-45 through A-47 show a decrease in
impact for every category analyzed.

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 177

Transportation of the struvite had almost negligible impact. Compared to impacts associated
with struvite and MgCl2, electricity was relatively small as well. Additionally, the greatest
change in impact for each category was through optimization of the struvite recovery. This
emphasizes the importance of improved struvite recovery when focusing solely on MagPrex®
reactor operation. The increased struvite recovery here only represents an increase in daily
weight of struvite separated. Another consideration must go into the quality of the struvite
product being formed. Ensuring an adequate enough particle size for the use of struvite as a
fertilizer is necessary for the credit to be applied to this system. In the case that this is not
achieved, the offset credited for the struvite as a fertilizer offset within the market may not be
justified even with the potential phosphorus that is contained in this product.

Figure A-45. LCA Results for Global Warming Potential (GWP, Right) and Cumulative Energy Demand (CED, Left)
 in Each Scenario by Process Contribution, Normalized Per m3 of Treated Water.

Polymer Use and Biosolid Hauling Comparison Results:
Polymer addition affects the wet tons per day of biosolid needing to be hauled, both of which
have associated LCA impacts. To investigate how the LCA results shifted with varying polymer
addition and subsequently, varying biosolids hauling, the relationship between total polymer
usage and wet tons per day hauled (normalized by feed solids to the centrifuge) was
determined for the three periods in this study (before MagPrex®, after MagPrex®, and the final
MagPrex® after optimization to the system). Figure A-48. Shows the relationship determined
between these two parameters during all periods.

Figure A-48. Wet Tons Per Day Hauled Normalized Per Feed Solids Dry Tons to Centrate as a Function of Polymer
 Usage From Historical Data During All Periods.

Assuming the same average centrate feed solids and distance hauled per truck across all three
periods, the LCA impacts at varying polymer doses was determined based on the impacts of the
polymer plus the impacts of the biosolid hauling (as determined based on the relationships
derived above). Figures A-49 through A-53 show these results. As can be seen in the figures, for
most impacts such as freshwater eutrophication, marine eutrophication, and water footprint,
there is essentially an almost linear relationship between polymer dose and the associated
impacts of polymer + biosolid hauling. For the global warming and marine eutrophication
impact however, there seems to be a potential dosing point that can minimize these impacts at
around 2000 lbs/day of polymer (particularly for the final MagPrex® scenario) due to the
relative impact of biosolid hauling compared to polymer addition in these impact categories.

Results were normalized per m3 of treated water in the mainstream liquid treatment. Cost
savings associated with chemical polymer and MgCl2 resulted in a cost savings of around
$0.004/m3 of treated water. Costs associated with MagPrex® electricity were minimal, and
remained the same between scenarios.

 The first option follows the inputs of Sena et al. 2021, which in turn follows similar methods
 presented by Igos et al 2017. In the referenced analyses, MgCl2 was input as a proxy process
 using the formation of MgCl2 through carbochlorination of MgO plus the addition of water
 content in order to formulate the MgCl2 solution. It was not indicated in these studies the
 percent solution MgCl2 used. The ratios of MgO, Cl2 and Carbon in the current study were
 therefore kept consistent with the inputs outlined by Igos et al., but the water content was
 adjusted in this analysis to produce a 30% MgCl2 solution. Weights of MgO, Cl2 and Carbon were
 all included in this 30% portion. Even with this conservative approach, the water content added
 to get 30% w/w solution was still only 21.9 kg as opposed to the 996 kg of water added in the
 study (to make a m3 of solution. Instead of reporting this as m3 of solution, all of the following
 comparisons were presented per kg of 30% solution for comparability.

 The second process comparison was mined NaCl as a proxy to the mined MgCl2 product of
 Envirotech. Both are major products from the Great Salt Lake and are extracted using similar
 methods. As reported in the USLCI database documentation, 95% of the process was mined
 from salt brine. The impacts were multiplied by 30% to be in a comparable unit of “kg of 30%
 NaCl solution” as opposed to “kg of NaCl”. Lastly the inputs from Compass Minerals for their
 mined MgCl2 product were analyzed as well.

 Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 195

The comparison between each process option can be seen in Figure A-56. The MgCl2 process
using the Compass Minerals inputs varied from 9 - 53% of the impact of the MgCl2 process as
input using the manufactured MgCl2 proxy following the methods of Sena et al. 2017. The
mined NaCl option varied from 1-13% of the impact of the manufactured proxy. This illustrates
that the manufactured proxy may overestimate the impact compared to the mined process
option counterparts. Mined NaCl did not have water foot printing information. Water footprint
using the other two methods were comparable, the manufactured proxy method being 88% of
the Compass Minerals mined process option.

The inputs from Compass Minerals were used to provide a better representation of
Envirotech’s MgCl2 production, as both companies mine MgCl2 from the Great Salt Lake
(LaLonde 2019).

 Following the Methods of Past Literature on a Kg Basis Is Much Higher Than the Mined Process Options.

A.5 Application 5 – Holistic Biosolids Optimization
A.5.1 Overview of Application
Goal: To assess the comparative environmental impact of DC Water’s dewatering system at
Blue Plains advanced wastewater treatment plant with manual polymer dose control, and with
the goal for acoustic-based control.

  Figure A-57. Environmental Impacts for the Baseline Scenario With Manual Polymer Dosing and the Goal for
 Acoustic-Sensor Dosing. Results Presented as a Percentage of the Baseline Impact in Each Category.

 Table A-54. Overall Environmental Impacts Per m3 of Treated Water in Each Scenario.
 Impact Category Method Unit Manual Operation Acoustic Sensor
                                                                  (2017-2020)               Goal

 Global Warming ReCiPe kg CO₂ eq 0.0092 0.0082
 Freshwater Eutrophication ReCiPe kg P eq 3.67E-06 3.27E-06
