An LCA/TEA was conducted on DC Water’s dewatering system at Blue Plains advanced
wastewater treatment plant to compare manual polymer dose control with the goal for
acoustic-based control. In all impact categories analyzed, a decrease was observed between the
baseline scenario with manual polymer dosing versus the goal acoustic sensor-based polymer
dosing scenario. This reflected the decrease associated both with polymer consumption and in
trucking of the biosolid product.

Downstream processes affected by improved polymer dosing were initially considered in this
analysis. MeOH use in the mainstream DEMON reactor was hypothesized to decrease with
better polymer dosing and theoretically less return of the dewatering filtrate to the head of the

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 197

plant, however, this effect was not included within the analysis. Actual MeOH savings
associated with the returned filtrate are infeasible to quantify with available data. Further,
MeOH had a larger impact compared to any of the other processes involved within the actual
dewatering system boundaries.

Impacts associated with the containment and transportation of the polymer were negligible. In
all impact categories, polymer manufacturing was the highest contributing process. This was
particularly true for eutrophication potential and water consumption. The actual quality of the
biosolid product should also be considered. The biosolids from the Blue Plains facility are used
as a commercial soil amendment and are an important part of a sustainable circular economy.
This potential benefit (and a credit for fertilizer offset) was not included in this analysis for the
biosolid product.

A.5.2 Goal and Scope

This project is application #5 - holistic biosolids optimization as a part of the broader DOE
project: Integration of data-driven process control for maximization of energy and resource
efficiency in advanced water resource recovery facilities (WRRFs).

In the final step of DC Water’s Blue Plains biosolid facility, polymer is added along a belt filter
press to bind the solid and expel entrained water trapped within. The optimal dosing of the
polymer is critical in producing a quality biosolid that is sold commercially as a fertilizer
product. Not only does this have implications for the quality of the biosolid, it also has
important implications for biosolid transportation from the plant. Underestimating the required
polymer can lead to a large amount of water retained in the biosolid, which has negative
impacts on the biosolid load that must be hauled each day. The increased transportation needs
are both costly and environmentally detrimental. Conversely, too much polymer can also affect
the quality of the biosolid. More importantly, inefficient polymer use has important
environmental implications associated with its manufacturing and transportation to the plant.

While the environmental and economic implications of polymer dosing are many, current
dosing strategies often rely on manual visual inspection of the quality of the solid along the belt
filter press. Online sensors for optimal polymer dosing are not available, and lab samples are
infeasible to improve upon current strategies. To close this research gap, DC Water is
investigating methods for prediction of key dewatering parameters (i.e. %TS) based on the
sludge coming into the biosolids facility. Direct mechanistic models based on the primary and
secondary sludge characteristics coming into the biosolid facility are not enough for
dewaterability prediction. This is because processes upstream of the belt filter press are
complex, have the potential for unpredictable events such as over-acidification and gas hold-
up, and have a solid retention time delay on the order of ~15-25 days. The proposed strategy at
DC Water was an acoustic sensor to predict dewaterability at multiple points throughout the
system in order to predict an optimal polymer dose.

The environmental sustainability implications of improved polymer dosing were studied
through LCA by the comparison of two scenarios: Scenario 1 investigated the baseline manual

198 The Water Research Foundation

polymer dosing by using annual average data for chemical use and biosolid haul information
over 2017-2020 plant operation. Scenario 2 investigated the goal for polymer reduction and
biosolid hauling reduction for the acoustic sensor based scenario. The goal polymer reduction
was based on what was seen as achievable based on periods of plant performance when better
attention was paid to the polymer dosing. Polymer savings as well as biosolid cake
quality/dewaterability were the main inputs to the LCA.

LCA Objectives:
• To assess the comparative environmental impact of DC Water’s dewatering system with
 manual operation and goal acoustic sensor-controlled polymer dosing.
• To identify which components of the dewatering process have the largest impacts.

various treatment capacities, the results were normalized by the volume treated in the system.
Therefore, the functional basis for this LCA was one cubic meter of wastewater treated by the
overall plant. Presenting these results per cubic meter of treated water also made these results
more interpretable and relevant to plant operation. Impacts were calculated for a year of
operation. These results were then divided by the volume of water treated at the plant in this
timespan.

Another applicable functional unit could have been “m3 of solids handled” by the biosolids
system. The plant-wide treated water unit was selected to be consistent with the other
applications on this project and for ease of comparison.

The construction and demolition phases of the dewatering infrastructure were not included
within the system boundaries, as this was not expected to change between each scenario.
Infrastructure such as the sensors were also considered to be negligible compared to the full
operation of the system over a year.

The operational phase was the focus of this LCA specifically of the biosolids dewatering system
and the belt filter press. This included the material acquisition of the polymer. Packaging,
transportation, and manufacturing were all considered for the polymer input. Transportation of
the final dewatered solid was included in the system boundaries. The system diagram in Figure
A-58 shows the processes included in the system boundary. Downstream use of MeOH in the
main DEMON reactors was hypothesized to change. This was not factored into this analysis.
Additionally, the environmental benefit of the biosolid as a commercial soil amendment
product was also not included. While this has important implications for a sustainable circular
economy, the biosolid does not directly replace the sale of current agricultural products like
organic fertilizers. It is therefore difficult to include as a substitution, particularly in an
attributional LCA.

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 199

  Figure A-58. System Boundary for the LCA of the Dewatering System at DC Water's Blue Plains Advanced
 Wastewater Treatment Plant Includes Chemical Manufacturing, Packaging and Transportation of Polymer, as
 Well as Transportation of the Biosolid Products. Actual Application and Offset from the Biosolid Product Was
 Not Included Within the Boundaries.

A.5.3 Inventory Analysis

The majority of this analysis was conducted based on information provided by DC Water on the
operation of their plant. Transportation distances were determined using the information
provided by DC Water and based on Google Maps from the location of chemical manufacturers.
Packaging processes for the polymer FIBC super bag were sourced from a study conducted by
Durao 2021 on industrial packaging. Finally, the main database used for all of our relevant
processes in Simapro was the Ecoinvent database. Figure A-65 of section A.5.5, supplemental
Tables and Figures, shows the major data and source information for this analysis.

TEA/LCA Data Inputs:
Both a TEA and LCA were conducted in parallel to analyze the system's economic and
environmental sustainability impacts. Much of the input data for the TEA model overlapped
with the necessary information for the LCA model. Input information for the foreground system
for this LCA can be divided into three main categories: treatment basis, chemical use, and
transportation. Figure A-59 shows the two models working in parallel to take in the foreground
inputs and generate environmental and economic impact areas of focus.

200 The Water Research Foundation

 Figure A-59. Data Flow Overview of Main Parameters With Parallel LCA and TEA Models.

Table A-55 indicates cost information for relevant TEA inputs. These parameters were relayed
to NETL for the WaterTAP3 zero order model.
 Table A-55. Costing Information.
 Parameter Value Unit
 Fuel unit cost $2.5 $/gal
 Polymer unit cost $1.8 $/lb

Chemical and Treatment Basis:
The average overall plant flow rate for DC Water’s Blue Plains advanced wastewater treatment
plant was 300 MGD in our analysis. Results in this LCA were calculated for a year of operation
and were normalized by the volume of water treated in this timespan.

Polymer dose was determined based on annual averages over the past 4 years. The average
mass of polymer per ton of dry solids (lbs/ton TS) was multiplied by the dewatering solids load
(dtpd) each year. A polymer dose of about 24.8 lbs/ton TS was the average for this baseline.
The polymer dose and the solids load to be hauled were also determined for the goal acoustic
sensor based polymer dosing scenario.

Other Affected Processes:
Beyond the immediate sustainability implications for the dewatering system itself, upstream
and downstream processes affected by optimal polymer dosing were also considered in this
analysis. Methanol (MeOH) consumption in the DEMON reactors was hypothesized to be
affected by improved polymer dosing. When filtrate quality from the belt filter press is
extremely poor, it can be a reason to return filtrate to the head of the plant and the DEMON
reactors. Optimizing the plant’s polymer dose reduces the chances for poor/variable filtrate

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 201

quality. It was estimated that about 23 lbs COD/MG could be saved due to better filtrate quality
from optimized polymer dosing and dewatering of the solids.

This return typically occurs in the field when noticed by an operator and only occurs for short
periods of time. Because there may be other reasons for the bypass as well, it is difficult to
determine how much of this filtrate is being sent back due to poor filtrate quality. Reliable data
is therefore difficult to produce for poor filtrate caused bypass. Within the DEMON reactor, it is
also difficult to determine how much of the MeOH is needed specifically due to this filtrate.
Quantifying the actual MeOH savings that can be achieved from this project is therefore
practically infeasible. Additionally, in previous iterations of this LCA, MeOH was determined to
vastly outweigh the sustainability implications of any of the other components in this analysis.
This benefit is secondary to the primary goals of this project, focused on actual polymer
consumption and dewaterability of the biosolid product. Because of the constraints on actually
quantifying the associated MeOH savings, and because of its effect on the understanding of the
other parameters involved in this LCA, downstream impacts to MeOH consumption were
therefore not included in this iteration of the LCA.

Another important consideration pertained to the quality of the biosolid produced. The biosolid
product is land applied in nearby farm land and also sold commercially as a soil amendment
product. This has important implications for the circular economy of wastewater treatment.
However, this product does not directly replace the production of other commercial products
like organic fertilizers. It is difficult to include this product as a fertilizer offset, especially within
an attributional LCA. Substitution credit was therefore not considered for the biosolid. The LCA
was cut off after the transportation of the biosolid from the plant.

Transportation impacts were sourced from the Ecoinvent database, as described in the overall
LCA framework. Transportation values were input for this process as the mass of load carried
times the distance traveled for a unit of ton*km = tkm.

All of the polymer for DC Water is made by Polydyne in Riceboro, Georgia 996 km (620 miles)
away by truck. The distance to haul the biosolid was indicated by DC Water to be 129 km (80
miles) one way from the treatment plant by truck. The annual average polymer mass of the last
four years was used to calculate the annual polymer haul. Similarly, the annual average
estimate for wet tons per day over the last four years was used to calculate the annual biosolid
haul. Because of the importance of transportation in the LCA impacts, an independent check
was used to ensure our methods of calculating the loads of polymer and biosolids hauled were
accurate. This was especially important in the hauling of the biosolid product. This can be found
in section A.5.5, supplemental Tables and Figures.

Polymer is shipped in flexible intermediate bulk container “super” bags. 1000 kg FIBC bags were
included as an impact of polymer use. These were assumed not to be reused for a conservative
LCA. As is shown later in our results, this assumption proved to be negligible. The impact
associated with this packaging was negligible compared to other impacts such as the
manufacturing of the polymer. The FIBC bag was input as a process using Ecoinvent

202 The Water Research Foundation

polypropylene and electricity as shown in Table A-57 of section A.5.5, supplemental Tables and
Figures. Inputs were based on values from literature from an LCA on bulk packaging (Durao
2021). Under manual operation, an estimated 583 FIBC bags would be needed as compared to
518 in the acoustic sensor based scenario.

A.5.4 Impact Analysis
LCA Results:
The presented LCA results are normalized per m3 of treated water in the plant. As is shown in
Figure A-60, for both global warming potential and cumulative energy demand, there was a
decrease in overall impact between each scenario, reflecting the decrease both in polymer use
and in biosolid trucking. Polymer associated impacts were a larger contribution of the overall
impact than the biosolid transportation in both scenarios. Impacts associated with the
transportation and containment of the polymer were negligible in both categories.
Assumptions of non-reused packaging were therefore minimal.

Figure A-60. LCA Results Broken Down by Process for the Manual Polymer Dosing Period Compared to the Goal
  Acoustic Sensor Based Polymer Control for Global Warming Potential (Top) and Cumulative Energy Demand
 (Bottom) Normalized Per m3 of Treated Water.

Figure A-61 shows the same decrease across all impact categories reflected in freshwater
eutrophication and marine eutrophication. In both categories, polymer manufacturing was still
the largest contributor, but by an even more drastic amount. For marine eutrophication
specifically, this component made up almost all of the total impact.

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 203

Figure A-61. LCA Results Broken Down by Process for the Manual Polymer Dosing Period Compared to the Goal
 Acoustic Sensor Based Polymer Control for Freshwater Eutrophication (Top) and Marine Eutrophication
 (Bottom) Normalized Per m3 Of Treated Water.

Figure A-62 shows the same decrease for water footprint. In this case, the containment of the
polymer however had a larger impact than the transportation of the polymer.

Figure A-62. LCA Results Broken Down by Process for the Manual Polymer Dosing Period Compared to the Goal
 Acoustic Sensor Based Polymer Control for Water Footprint Normalized Per m3 of Treated Water.

As is shown in Figure A-63, in all impact categories, the manufacturing of the polymer was the
largest contributing factor. It also represented the largest change between the baseline manual
dosing scenario and the goal acoustic-sensor based control results. This indicated that reducing
the large impact of inefficiencies associated with polymer dosing should be a priority of this

204 The Water Research Foundation

project rather than dewaterability to reduce biosolid haul. It should be noted that the quality of
the biosolid product was not a factor captured in this LCA. The biosolid product is sold as a
commercial soil amendment and has important implications for the circular economy of
biosolids handling.

 Figure A-63. Percent Contribution of Processes in Each Impact Category for the Baseline Scenario. Percentages
 Only Shown if >1% of the Total Impact.

TEA Results:
A simple TEA was conducted for the manual dosing compared to the acoustic sensor dosing of
polymer to the dewatering system. The goal acoustic sensor polymer dosing showed OPEX
savings compared to the manual dosing period of $0.0007 (from $0.0067 to $0.0060 total OPEX
cost for the dewatering system), as seen in Figure A-64. All results are shown normalized per m3
product water treated in the liquid mainstream at the plant.

Integration of Data Driven Process Control for Maximizing Energy and Resource Efficiency in WRRFs 205

 Figure A-64. TEA Results for the Manual Polymer Dosing Period Compared to the Goal Acoustic Sensor Based
 Polymer Control for OPEX Costs.

  To ensure that polymer and biosolid calculations were accurate, an independent check was
  conducted using delivery frequency/load estimates. It was estimated that 2 trucks of polymer
  each month (each with a load of 45,000 lbs/truck) are shipped to Blue Plains wastewater
  treatment facility. Polymer consumption using this method is about 4.9E+05 kg annually. This is
  within 16% of our estimate for current polymer use, using the 4 year average annual polymer
  dose and solids loading.

  Biosolid loading was estimated to be about 150,000 wet tons per year hauled, or about 23 wet
  tons per truck at an average of 20-24 trucks per day. The annual biosolid haul reported was
  within 9% of our estimate using the 4 year average for wet tons per day hauled. The biosolid
  haul for 20 and 24 trucks per day was within 1.6% to 22% respectively of our baseline estimate
  as well.
